Engineering Management — December 2024

EXAMINATION OF MARINE ENGINEER OFFICER · Function: Marine Engineering Management at Management Level · M.E.O. Class I

Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

The stem prints three tasks and they are worth very different amounts. Explaining what the SDGs ARE is quick. Explaining their ROLE is the interesting part, because the SDGs are a General Assembly resolution that binds nobody - so the real question is how a non-binding framework changes behaviour at all, and the answer is comparability, universality, measurement, transparency and above all the mobilisation of finance and charterers. The third task, SPECIFIC ACTIONS, carries the most marks and is where answers usually collapse into generalities: 'reduce emissions' scores nothing, 'weather routeing, hull and propeller condition management, onshore power supply' scores. Choose SDG 13 and SDG 14 because each has dedicated IMO instruments behind it, which lets you cite regulation instead of sentiment - and remember the maritime instruments were moving fast in 2024, so the strategy, the mid-term measures and the Hong Kong Convention must each be stated at their December 2024 status.

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

Memory cue. Instrument: 70/1, 2015, 17 and 169, five Ps, leave no one behind (1). Role, six mechanisms: LANGUAGE, UNIVERSALITY, INTEGRATION, MEASUREMENT, TRANSPARENCY, FINANCE (2) - then the honest weakness. SDG 13 actions in ascending cost: OPERATE - RETROFIT - REFUEL - RENEW (5). SDG 14 actions outward from the ship: what LEAVES the hull, what is ON the hull, what the hull DOES, what happens at the END (7). Close on capital, cargo, asset value, direction (8).

Model written answer

1. What the Sustainable Development Goals are

The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are the core of "Transforming our world: the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development", adopted by the United Nations General Assembly by resolution A/RES/70/1 on 25 September 2015 and in effect from 1 January 2016. They succeeded the Millennium Development Goals, which ran to 2015.

The structure is worth stating precisely, because the numbers are marks:

  • 17 Goals and 169 targets, measured against a global indicator framework;
  • organised around five areas - People, Planet, Prosperity, Peace and Partnership;
  • carrying the central pledge to leave no one behind;
  • balancing the three dimensions of sustainable development - economic, social and environmental;
  • declared to be integrated and indivisible, and global in nature and universally applicable - they apply to developed and developing States alike, which is the principal break from the Millennium Development Goals.

2. The role of the SDGs in promoting global sustainability

The SDGs are not a treaty. They impose no legal obligation on any State and create no enforcement mechanism. The interesting question - and the one this limb is really asking - is therefore how a non-binding instrument achieves anything at all. It does so through six mechanisms:

  1. A common language and a common framework. Governments, financiers, corporations, classification societies and NGOs all now describe their environmental and social objectives against the same seventeen headings. That comparability is itself the achievement - it allows performance to be compared across sectors and borders in a way that was impossible before.
  2. Universality. Because the Goals apply to every State rather than only to developing ones, they reframe sustainability as a shared project instead of an aid programme.
  3. Integration. The Goals are expressly indivisible, which forces the recognition of trade-offs and co-benefits - decarbonising shipping (13) affects air quality and health (3), energy systems (7), industry and infrastructure (9) and ocean health (14) simultaneously.
  4. Measurement. 169 targets and a formal indicator framework turn aspiration into something that can be reported and audited. What is measured gets managed.
  5. Accountability by transparency rather than sanction. Review runs through the High-Level Political Forum and voluntary national reviews. There is no penalty - the pressure is reputational and peer-driven.
  6. Mobilisation of finance and of the private sector. This is the mechanism with the most practical bite for shipping. The SDGs have become the reference framework for ESG reporting, sustainable finance, green loans and charterers' requirements, so alignment has become a condition of access to capital and to cargo rather than a matter of goodwill.

The honest qualification, which a management-level answer should make: the same non-binding character is the SDGs' principal weakness. Progress depends on national will and on finance, delivery is uneven, and the framework is vulnerable to "SDG-washing" - the badging of existing activity against a convenient Goal. It is a framework for direction and comparison, not a regulatory instrument. In the maritime sector the actual obligations come from IMO conventions; the SDGs supply the direction those conventions are steered by.

3. Why the maritime sector matters, and how IMO aligns

Shipping is the dominant carrier of world trade by volume, and is simultaneously a material source of greenhouse gas and air emissions, a vector for invasive species, a source of operational and accidental marine pollution, and a global employer of seafarers. It therefore touches a large number of the Goals directly and is one of the few sectors regulated by a dedicated United Nations specialised agency.

The IMO has mapped its own work to the Goals and identifies SDG 14 as the Goal most directly relevant to its mandate, while its technical cooperation and capacity-building work is directed at a wider set. The two Goals chosen below are those where the maritime connection is strongest and the industry's actions are most concrete.

4. SDG 13 Climate Action - relevance to shipping

SDG 13 is "take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts".

Its relevance to shipping is twofold, and both halves should be stated:

  • shipping is a source. It is the most carbon-efficient mode of freight transport per tonne-kilometre, but the absolute volume of trade makes it a material emitter, and unlike shore industry it is a transboundary sector which no single State can regulate - which is precisely why it was left to IMO rather than being counted in national inventories under the Paris Agreement;
  • shipping is also exposed. Sea level rise, increased storm severity and changing ice conditions affect ports, coastal infrastructure and routeing - so the industry has an operational as well as a moral interest in the outcome.

The regulatory expression of SDG 13 in this sector is the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, resolution MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023, which superseded the 2018 Initial Strategy. Its levels of ambition are:

  • net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around, i.e. close to, 2050;
  • indicative checkpoints for 2030 and 2040, expressed as percentage reductions in total annual GHG emissions from international shipping against a 2008 baseline, each stated as a minimum with a higher "striving for" level;
  • uptake of zero or near-zero GHG emission technologies, fuels and energy sources to represent a share of the energy used by international shipping by 2030;
  • and a move from carbon intensity to well-to-wake, full lifecycle accounting of emissions.

The position at this sitting must be stated carefully. The short-term measures - the EEXI, the operational CII and the enhanced SEEMP - entered into force on 1 January 2023, so 2023 is the first full CII data year and the first ratings were issued during 2024. The mid-term measures - a technical measure such as a GHG fuel standard, and an economic measure such as a pricing mechanism - were still under development. MEPC 82, held from 30 September to 4 October 2024, carried that work forward and also opened a review of the short-term measures, referring it to a correspondence group and an intersessional working group reporting to MEPC 83 in 2025. No mid-term measure had been adopted.

5. SDG 13 - specific actions

The actions divide usefully into four groups, and giving them in this order shows engineering judgement rather than a list of green words:

GroupActionsComment
Operational - available now, at low capital costSpeed optimisation and slow steaming; weather routeing; just-in-time arrival negotiated with terminals; trim optimisation; hull and propeller condition management - coating selection, in-water inspection and polishing; voyage and cargo planning to reduce ballast legsThe SEEMP's four-step cycle is the management instrument through which these are planned, implemented, monitored and evaluated
Technical retrofitWaste heat recovery; shaft generators; propeller and rudder energy-saving devices, ducts and fins; air lubrication; wind assistance - rotor sails, suction wings, kites; engine derating and turbocharger cut-out; variable-frequency drives on pumps and fans; LED lighting and improved HVAC controlEach must be justified against the ship's actual trade - the SEEMP Guidelines warn that some measures are mutually exclusive
Fuels and energyLNG as a transitional fuel, with methane slip managed; methanol, ammonia and hydrogen; biofuels and drop-in blends; batteries and hybrid propulsion for suitable trades; onshore power supply (cold ironing) in port; dual-fuel and fuel-ready designs so a ship built now is not strandedThe Strategy's move to well-to-wake accounting is what makes the choice of fuel PRODUCTION pathway, not just the fuel, the decisive question
Fleet, commercial and organisationalFleet renewal with more efficient tonnage; charter party clauses allocating efficiency responsibility and permitting speed optimisation; digital performance monitoring and transparent emissions reporting; crew and shore-staff training in energy-efficient operation; participation in R&D funding and first-mover fuel initiativesThe split incentive between owner and charterer is the single largest non-technical obstacle, and is solved contractually

6. SDG 14 Life Below Water - relevance to shipping

SDG 14 is "conserve and sustainably use the oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development", and it is the Goal most directly connected to IMO's mandate. Target 14.1 - to prevent and significantly reduce marine pollution of all kinds - is the one shipping bears on most directly, and targets on marine ecosystems and on ocean acidification follow closely behind.

Shipping's impacts on the marine environment are:

  • operational and accidental pollution - oil, noxious liquid substances, sewage, garbage and plastics;
  • invasive aquatic species transferred in ballast water and as biofouling on hulls - one of the most serious and least reversible impacts, because a translocated species cannot be recovered;
  • toxic anti-fouling systems;
  • air emissions that acidify and enrich the sea - SOx and NOx deposition, and CO2 driving ocean acidification, which is where SDG 13 and SDG 14 meet;
  • underwater radiated noise, affecting marine mammals;
  • ship strikes on cetaceans;
  • end-of-life ship recycling, where poor practice contaminates the coastal environment.

The IMO instruments that implement this are MARPOL Annexes I to VI; the Ballast Water Management Convention 2004, in force 8 September 2017, with its D-1 exchange and D-2 performance standards; the AFS Convention 2001; the London Convention and Protocol on dumping; and the IMO Action Plan on marine plastic litter from ships.

One instrument requires care at this sitting. The Hong Kong International Convention for the Safe and Environmentally Sound Recycling of Ships, 2009 had its entry-into-force criteria met in June 2023, when Bangladesh and Liberia became Contracting States, and enters into force twenty-four months later on 26 June 2025. At the date of this examination it is therefore adopted and ratified but NOT YET IN FORCE - it comes into force six months after this paper was sat. That distinction between ratified and in force is exactly the kind of point a Class I examiner rewards, and getting it wrong in either direction is a visible error.

7. SDG 14 - specific actions

  1. Ballast water and biofouling. Operate and maintain the ballast water management system to the D-2 standard properly rather than nominally - crew training, sampling, and honest recording in the Ballast Water Record Book; and manage biofouling through a biofouling management plan, hull coating selection, in-water inspection and cleaning with capture. Biofouling management is a double win: it is also one of the largest available fuel-efficiency measures, so it serves SDG 13 at the same time.
  2. Zero-discharge discipline for garbage and plastics. Full segregation and Garbage Record Book accuracy under MARPOL Annex V, an absolute prohibition on plastics overboard, retention on board where reception facilities are inadequate, and elimination of single-use plastics in ship's stores - the industry's direct contribution to target 14.1.
  3. Oil and chemical pollution prevention. Rigorous Oil Record Book discipline and functioning oily-water separators with no bypass; bunkering procedures, checklists and scuppers plugged; the SOPEP or SMPEP exercised rather than filed; tank cleaning and slops managed properly.
  4. Sewage and grey water - treatment plants maintained and operated, and discharge only in accordance with Annex IV; voluntary grey water retention in sensitive areas.
  5. Air emissions that reach the sea - compliance with the sulphur limit and with ECA requirements, correct scrubber washwater management, and NOx-compliant operation, all of which reduce acidifying and eutrophying deposition.
  6. Anti-fouling systems compliant with the AFS Convention, with a valid International Anti-fouling System Certificate.
  7. Underwater radiated noise and ship strikes - design and operational measures to reduce radiated noise, particularly propeller cavitation, and compliance with routeing measures, areas to be avoided and speed restrictions in whale habitats.
  8. Responsible ship recycling - maintaining an accurate and updated Inventory of Hazardous Materials, and selecting recycling facilities operating to the Hong Kong Convention standard in advance of its entry into force, rather than waiting to be compelled.
  9. Protection of special areas and PSSAs - observing the stricter discharge and routeing requirements in Special Areas, Emission Control Areas and Particularly Sensitive Sea Areas.
  10. Beyond compliance - participation in ocean data collection, industry initiatives on plastics and biofouling, and transparent environmental reporting.

8. The management-level conclusion

The point on which to end - and the one that distinguishes a management-level answer from a general essay - is that for a shipowner in December 2024 the SDGs are no longer only an ethical framework; they have become a commercial and financing one.

  • Access to capital. Banks lending under sustainable-finance frameworks require alignment and portfolio emissions disclosure, so a fleet's carbon intensity affects its cost of borrowing.
  • Access to cargo. Major charterers set emissions and environmental criteria in their vetting and chartering decisions, so CII rating affects employability.
  • Asset value. A ship that cannot meet tightening carbon intensity requirements through its economic life faces early obsolescence, which makes newbuilding fuel choice a balance-sheet decision.
  • Regulatory direction. The SDGs are not enforceable, but they signal reliably where binding IMO regulation is heading - so alignment is also the cheapest available form of regulatory risk management.
  • Licence to operate - crew recruitment and retention, port and coastal State relations, and public reputation.

In short: the SDGs set the direction, IMO conventions turn that direction into obligation, and the SEEMP and the ship's management system turn obligation into daily practice. A management-level officer's role sits at the last of those three, and the value of understanding the first is knowing why the third keeps changing.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks in one limb, and the stem prints three distinct tasks: explain the role of the SDGs in promoting global sustainability; choose any two and discuss their relevance to the maritime sector; and highlight specific actions the shipping industry can take. The third task carries the most marks and is where most answers thin out into generalities.

The word that governs the whole answer is "specific". "Reduce emissions" and "protect the marine environment" score nothing. Weather routeing, hull and propeller condition management, onshore power supply, D-2 ballast water compliance, an accurate Inventory of Hazardous Materials - these score, because they are things an engineer actually does. The route therefore devotes a full step to actions under each chosen Goal.

Note also that the stem says "choose any two". That is a genuine choice, and choosing well is part of the answer. SDG 13 and SDG 14 are the strongest pair because they are the two with dedicated IMO instruments behind them, which lets you cite regulation rather than sentiment. SDG 8 (decent work, via the MLC), SDG 7 (clean energy), SDG 9 (industry and infrastructure) and SDG 3 (health and wellbeing) are all defensible alternatives - but say which two you have chosen and why, in one line, before you start.

Answering the 'role' limb properly

Most candidates answer this limb by describing what the SDGs are. The question asks about their role in promoting sustainability, which is a different and more interesting question, because the SDGs are not a treaty and bind nobody.

So the marks are in explaining how a non-binding instrument works at all: a common language and comparability; universality applying to every State; integration forcing recognition of trade-offs; measurement through 169 targets and an indicator framework; accountability by transparency through the High-Level Political Forum and voluntary national reviews rather than by sanction; and - the one with the most practical bite for shipping - mobilisation of finance and the private sector through ESG reporting, sustainable finance and charterers' requirements.

Adding the honest weakness - that non-binding status means uneven delivery and exposure to "SDG-washing", and that in this sector the actual obligations come from IMO conventions while the SDGs supply direction - turns a descriptive answer into an evaluative one, which is what management level means.

The numbers and the instrument

  • A/RES/70/1, adopted 25 September 2015, in effect 1 January 2016.
  • 17 Goals, 169 targets, with a global indicator framework.
  • Five Ps - People, Planet, Prosperity, Peace, Partnership.
  • Leave no one behind - the central pledge.
  • Integrated and indivisible; global in nature and universally applicable - the break from the Millennium Development Goals.
  • Review through the High-Level Political Forum and voluntary national reviews.

Naming the resolution and the two dates is cheap and immediately marks the answer out. So does saying 17 and 169 rather than "a number of goals and targets".

The three things that must be right for December 2024

This question has no single hard date in it, which makes it more dangerous rather than less - the temptation is to write about decarbonisation as it stands whenever you are revising. Three positions must be fixed at the sitting:

  1. The operative IMO climate instrument is the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023, with its net-zero-by-or-around-2050 ambition. It superseded the 2018 Initial Strategy and its far weaker 50%-by-2050 target. Citing the 2018 Strategy as current is a straightforward error at this sitting.
  2. The mid-term measures were NOT adopted. A technical measure such as a GHG fuel standard and an economic measure such as a pricing mechanism were under development. MEPC 82 (30 September to 4 October 2024) carried the work forward and referred the review of the short-term measures onward to MEPC 83 in 2025. Describing any mid-term measure as adopted is future contamination.
  3. The Hong Kong Convention was ratified but NOT YET IN FORCE. Its criteria were met in June 2023 and it enters into force on 26 June 2025 - six months after this examination.

That third point is the best single opportunity on this question. A sitting that falls six months before a convention's entry into force lets you demonstrate the adopted / ratified / in force distinction with a concrete, current example - and it is precisely what a candidate revising from later material would get wrong, because by then the Convention is simply in force.

The stage of the decarbonisation regime at this sitting

Worth holding, because it lets you write about the CII with confidence rather than vaguely: EEXI, the operational CII and the enhanced SEEMP entered into force on 1 January 2023. So at December 2024, 2023 is the first full CII data year and the first ratings have been issued during 2024 on that data. The regime is live and biting, not prospective - which is the correct tense for this sitting and is different from how a January 2024 answer would have read.

Where SDG 13 and SDG 14 meet

A point worth one sentence and worth a mark, because it demonstrates the Goals' claimed indivisibility rather than merely asserting it: CO2 absorbed by the ocean drives acidification, so reducing shipping's greenhouse gas emissions serves SDG 14 as well as SDG 13. The same is true in the other direction - biofouling management prevents invasive species transfer under SDG 14 and is one of the largest available fuel-efficiency measures under SDG 13.

If you can give one example of a single action serving both chosen Goals, you have shown you understand what "integrated and indivisible" means in practice.

Common mistakes

  • Describing what the SDGs are and never addressing their role - how a non-binding framework achieves anything.
  • Giving generic actions. The stem says "specific actions". Name the measure an engineer would actually implement.
  • Not stating which two Goals have been chosen, or choosing two with weak maritime connections.
  • Citing the 2018 Initial Strategy as the current IMO GHG strategy. It was superseded by MEPC.377(80) on 7 July 2023.
  • Describing an IMO mid-term measure as adopted. None was, at this sitting.
  • Describing the Hong Kong Convention as in force. It enters into force 26 June 2025, six months after this sitting.
  • Treating the SDGs as legally binding. They are not - the binding obligations are in the IMO conventions.
  • Confusing the SDGs with the Paris Agreement, which is a separate treaty and does not cover international shipping in national inventories.
  • Asserting a percentage for shipping's share of global emissions or of world trade. The figures vary widely by source; make the argument without them.
  • Writing only about the environment. SDG 8 decent work, delivered through the MLC, and SDG 5 gender equality are also live maritime connections.

Examiner traps

  • "Are the SDGs binding on India?" No - they are a General Assembly resolution, not a treaty. The binding maritime obligations come from IMO conventions, which India implements through the Merchant Shipping Act 1958 and subordinate rules.
  • "Which SDG is closest to IMO's mandate?" SDG 14, life below water - though IMO maps its work across many.
  • "What is the current IMO GHG strategy and what does it aim at?" The 2023 Strategy, MEPC.377(80) of 7 July 2023 - net-zero by or around 2050, with indicative checkpoints for 2030 and 2040 and a 2030 ambition for zero or near-zero fuels.
  • "Is the Hong Kong Convention in force?" Not at this sitting - criteria met June 2023, entry into force 26 June 2025.
  • "Give one action that serves two Goals at once." Biofouling management - it prevents invasive species transfer (14) and reduces fuel consumption (13).
  • "Why does well-to-wake accounting matter?" Because it counts emissions from fuel production as well as combustion, so the production pathway - not just the fuel - determines whether a fuel is genuinely low-carbon.

Likely oral follow-up

  • When were the SDGs adopted and by what instrument? UN General Assembly resolution A/RES/70/1, 25 September 2015, effective 1 January 2016.
  • How many Goals and targets? 17 Goals and 169 targets.
  • What are the five Ps? People, Planet, Prosperity, Peace, Partnership.
  • What is SDG 13 and SDG 14? Climate action; and conserve and sustainably use the oceans, seas and marine resources.
  • Which IMO convention addresses invasive species? The Ballast Water Management Convention 2004, in force 8 September 2017, with the D-1 and D-2 standards.
  • Name three operational measures to reduce fuel consumption. Speed optimisation, weather routeing, hull and propeller condition management.
  • Name three alternative fuels and one problem with each. LNG - methane slip; methanol - lower energy density; ammonia - toxicity and NOx.
  • What is cold ironing? Onshore power supply, allowing a ship in port to shut down auxiliary engines.
  • What document must be maintained for ship recycling? The Inventory of Hazardous Materials.
  • How do the SDGs affect a shipowner commercially? Through access to capital, access to cargo, asset value and regulatory direction.

Memory framework

For the instrument: 70/1, 2015, 17 and 169, five Ps, leave no one behind.

For the role limb, six mechanisms: LANGUAGE, UNIVERSALITY, INTEGRATION, MEASUREMENT, TRANSPARENCY, FINANCE - then the honest weakness.

For SDG 13 actions, four groups in ascending capital cost: OPERATE better - RETROFIT - REFUEL - RENEW. For SDG 14 actions, work outward from the ship: what leaves the hull (garbage, oil, sewage, ballast), what is on the hull (anti-fouling, biofouling), what the hull does (noise, ship strikes), and what happens at the end (recycling).

Regulation and source map

  • A/RES/70/1, 25 September 2015 - the 2030 Agenda; 17 Goals, 169 targets; five Ps; leave no one behind; HLPF and voluntary national reviews
  • SDG 13 - urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts
  • SDG 14 - conserve and sustainably use the oceans, seas and marine resources; target 14.1 on marine pollution
  • MEPC.377(80), 7 July 2023 - the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy; net-zero by or around 2050; 2030 and 2040 indicative checkpoints; 2030 zero or near-zero fuel uptake; well-to-wake accounting. Superseded the 2018 Initial Strategy
  • MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 - reg 20 goal, reg 26 SEEMP, reg 27 data collection, reg 28 CII; EEXI, CII and enhanced SEEMP in force 1 January 2023
  • MEPC.395(82), 4 October 2024 - the 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, operative at this sitting
  • Ballast Water Management Convention 2004 - in force 8 September 2017; D-1 exchange and D-2 performance standards
  • AFS Convention 2001; London Convention and Protocol; MARPOL Annexes I to VI; the IMO Action Plan on marine plastic litter from ships
  • Hong Kong Convention 2009 - criteria met June 2023; enters into force 26 June 2025; NOT in force at this sitting

Uncertainty and currency

Four limitations are recorded, and three of them are deliberate refusals to assert a number. No figure is given for shipping's share of global greenhouse gas emissions or of world trade - both circulate very widely, are quoted at materially different values by different sources and study years, and none was verified for this sitting; the argument is made qualitatively instead, which costs nothing because the examiner is not testing the statistic. No SDG progress figure is asserted for the same reason. And the numerical values of the 2030 and 2040 indicative checkpoints are described in structure - a percentage reduction against a 2008 baseline, stated as a minimum with a higher striving-for level - rather than quoted, because those values were not re-read from the resolution in this session. The net-zero-by-or-around-2050 ambition and the 2030 zero or near-zero fuel ambition are asserted, both having been established directly.

Two currency instructions for any later editor. The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy is the operative strategy here and no mid-term measure may be described as adopted - the work was referred onward to MEPC 83 in 2025, after this examination. And the Hong Kong Convention must remain "not yet in force" in this object: it entered into force on 26 June 2025, six months after this paper was sat, and an editor working from current material would find it described as in force and would introduce a state of affairs that did not exist at the sitting.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________main What the Sustainable Development Goals are
  2. __________main The role of the SDGs in promoting global sustainability
  3. __________main Why the maritime sector matters, and how IMO aligns
  4. __________main SDG 13 Climate Action - relevance to shipping
  5. __________main SDG 13 - specific actions
  6. __________main SDG 14 Life Below Water - relevance to shipping
  7. __________main SDG 14 - specific actions
  8. __________main The management-level conclusion
Flashcards 12

1 What the SDGs are. 2 Their role in promoting sustainability. 3 Why the maritime sector matters and which two Goals you choose. 4 SDG 13 relevance. 5 SDG 13 specific actions. 6 SDG 14 relevance. 7 SDG 14 specific actions. 8 The management-level conclusion.

The stem prints three tasks and the third - specific actions - carries the most marks, so it gets a full step under each chosen Goal.

UN General Assembly resolution A/RES/70/1, 'Transforming our world: the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development', adopted 25 September 2015 and in effect from 1 January 2016. It contains 17 Goals and 169 targets, organised around five areas - People, Planet, Prosperity, Peace and Partnership - with the pledge to leave no one behind, and declares the Goals integrated and indivisible, global in nature and universally applicable.

Naming the resolution and the two dates, and giving 17 and 169, immediately marks the answer out.

Through six mechanisms: a common language giving comparability across sectors and borders; universality, applying to every State rather than only developing ones; integration, forcing recognition of trade-offs and co-benefits; measurement, through 169 targets and an indicator framework; accountability by transparency, through the High-Level Political Forum and voluntary national reviews rather than sanction; and mobilisation of finance and the private sector through ESG reporting, sustainable finance and charterers' requirements - the mechanism with the most practical bite for shipping.

The stem asks about their ROLE, not what they ARE, and this is the distinction most answers miss.

The 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, resolution MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023, which superseded the 2018 Initial Strategy. It sets net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around, i.e. close to, 2050; indicative checkpoints for 2030 and 2040 against a 2008 baseline; an ambition for uptake of zero or near-zero GHG emission technologies, fuels and energy sources by 2030; and a move to well-to-wake lifecycle accounting.

Citing the 2018 Initial Strategy and its weaker 50%-by-2050 target is stale by eighteen months at this sitting.

Under development, and NOT adopted. A technical measure such as a GHG fuel standard and an economic measure such as a pricing mechanism were being developed; MEPC 82, held 30 September to 4 October 2024, carried the work forward and referred the review of the short-term measures to a correspondence group and an intersessional working group reporting to MEPC 83 in 2025.

Material written for any later sitting carries the outcome of that work, and back-reading it is future contamination.

No. Its entry-into-force criteria were met in June 2023, when Bangladesh and Liberia became Contracting States, and it enters into force twenty-four months later on 26 June 2025 - six months AFTER this examination. At this sitting it is adopted and ratified but not yet in force.

The clearest adopted-versus-ratified-versus-in-force illustration available on this question, and it inverts for any later sitting.

OPERATE - speed optimisation, weather routeing, just-in-time arrival, trim optimisation, hull and propeller condition management. RETROFIT - waste heat recovery, shaft generators, energy-saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance, variable-frequency drives. REFUEL - LNG with methane slip managed, methanol, ammonia, hydrogen, biofuels, batteries, onshore power supply, dual-fuel and fuel-ready designs. RENEW - fleet renewal, charter party clauses addressing the split incentive, digital performance monitoring, training.

Ordering by capital cost shows engineering judgement rather than a list of green words.

MARPOL Annexes I to VI; the Ballast Water Management Convention 2004, in force 8 September 2017, with the D-1 ballast water exchange standard and the D-2 ballast water performance standard; the AFS Convention 2001; the London Convention and Protocol; and the IMO Action Plan on marine plastic litter from ships. The Hong Kong Convention 2009 was not yet in force at this sitting.

SDG 14 is the Goal closest to IMO's mandate, so this is where regulation can be cited rather than sentiment.

What leaves the hull - D-2 ballast water compliance with honest record keeping, zero plastics overboard and Garbage Record Book discipline, oily-water separator with no bypass, Annex IV sewage treatment, sulphur and ECA compliance with scrubber washwater management. What is on the hull - AFS-compliant anti-fouling with a valid IAFS Certificate, and a biofouling management plan with in-water cleaning and capture. What the hull does - reduce underwater radiated noise from propeller cavitation, and observe routeing measures and speed restrictions in whale habitats. What happens at the end - an accurate Inventory of Hazardous Materials and recycling at Hong Kong Convention standard yards.

The outward-from-the-hull structure is what makes the list specific rather than generic.

Biofouling management. It prevents the transfer of invasive aquatic species, serving SDG 14, and simultaneously reduces hull resistance and therefore fuel consumption and emissions, serving SDG 13. The link runs the other way too: CO2 absorbed by the ocean drives acidification, so reducing greenhouse gas emissions under SDG 13 protects marine ecosystems under SDG 14.

It demonstrates the Goals' claimed indivisibility in practice instead of merely asserting it.

They are not binding - they are a UN General Assembly resolution, not a treaty, with no enforcement mechanism. The binding maritime obligations come from IMO conventions, principally MARPOL, the BWM Convention and the AFS Convention, which States implement domestically - in India through the Merchant Shipping Act 1958 and subordinate rules. The SDGs set direction; the conventions create obligation.

It is the distinction that makes the 'role' limb answerable, and it prevents the answer overclaiming.

Access to capital - sustainable finance frameworks and portfolio emissions disclosure affect the cost of borrowing. Access to cargo - charterers apply environmental criteria, so CII rating affects employability. Asset value - a ship unable to meet tightening carbon intensity requirements faces early obsolescence, making newbuilding fuel choice a balance-sheet decision. Regulatory direction - the SDGs signal where binding IMO regulation is heading. And licence to operate - crew recruitment, port State relations and reputation.

This is what turns a general essay into a management-level answer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
SDGs = core of the 2030 Agenda, UNGA resolution A/RES/70/1, adopted 25 September 2015, effective 1 January 2016; successor to the MDGs. 17 Goals, 169 targets, global indicator framework; five areas People, Planet, Prosperity, Peace, Partnership; pledge to leave no one behind; integrated and indivisible, universally applicable to all States. ROLE - and they are NOT a treaty: common language and comparability; universality; integration forcing trade-offs and co-benefits; measurement making progress auditable; accountability by transparency via the High-Level Political Forum and voluntary national reviews, not sanction; and mobilisation of finance - ESG, sustainable finance, charterers' criteria - which is the mechanism with real bite for shipping. Weakness: non-binding, uneven delivery, SDG-washing; the real maritime obligations come from IMO conventions. SDG 13 CLIMATE ACTION. Shipping is a source (most efficient per tonne-km but material in absolute terms; transboundary, hence IMO not national inventories) and exposed (sea level, storms, ice). Operative instrument: 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80), 7 July 2023, superseding the 2018 Initial Strategy - net-zero by or around 2050, indicative checkpoints 2030 and 2040 against a 2008 baseline, 2030 uptake of zero/near-zero fuels, and well-to-wake accounting. At this sitting EEXI, CII and enhanced SEEMP have been in force since 1 Jan 2023, 2023 is the first full CII data year and first ratings issued during 2024; mid-term measures NOT adopted (MEPC 82, Sept-Oct 2024, referred work to MEPC 83 in 2025). ACTIONS - OPERATE: speed optimisation, weather routeing, just-in-time arrival, trim, hull and propeller condition. RETROFIT: waste heat recovery, shaft generator, energy-saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance, VFDs. REFUEL: LNG (methane slip), methanol, ammonia, hydrogen, biofuels, batteries, onshore power supply, dual-fuel/fuel-ready. RENEW: fleet renewal, charter party clauses on the split incentive, performance monitoring, training. SDG 14 LIFE BELOW WATER - closest to IMO's mandate; target 14.1 marine pollution. Impacts: oil, NLS, sewage, garbage and plastics; invasive species via ballast water and biofouling; anti-fouling toxicity; SOx/NOx deposition and CO2-driven acidification (where 13 and 14 meet); underwater radiated noise; ship strikes; end-of-life recycling. Instruments: MARPOL I-VI; BWM Convention 2004, in force 8 Sept 2017, D-1 exchange and D-2 performance; AFS 2001; London Convention and Protocol; IMO marine plastic litter action plan. HONG KONG CONVENTION 2009 - criteria met June 2023, ENTERS INTO FORCE 26 JUNE 2025, so NOT YET IN FORCE at this sitting. ACTIONS: D-2 ballast compliance operated properly with an honest record book; biofouling management (serves 13 AND 14); zero plastics overboard and Garbage Record Book discipline; OWS with no bypass and Oil Record Book discipline; SOPEP/SMPEP exercised; Annex IV sewage; sulphur/ECA and scrubber washwater; valid IAFS Certificate; reduce propeller cavitation and observe whale routeing measures; accurate Inventory of Hazardous Materials and Hong Kong-standard recycling yards; observe Special Areas, ECAs and PSSAs. CONCLUSION: SDGs now drive access to capital, access to cargo, asset value, regulatory direction and licence to operate.

Major trap
Answering with generic aspirations when the stem asks for specific actions the shipping industry can take - 'reduce emissions' scores nothing where 'weather routeing, hull and propeller condition management, onshore power supply, D-2 ballast water compliance' scores. Three currency traps sit alongside it, and all three are things a candidate revising from later material would get wrong: the operative IMO climate instrument is the 2023 Strategy MEPC.377(80), not the superseded 2018 Initial Strategy; no mid-term measure had been adopted at this sitting, the work having been referred onward to MEPC 83 in 2025; and the Hong Kong Convention was NOT YET IN FORCE - its criteria were met in June 2023 but it enters into force on 26 June 2025, six months after this examination.

Understand this first

This looks like a general essay on noise and it is not. Every one of the five things the stem asks for - purpose, maximum allowable limits, areas of application, measures to reduce noise, and compliance requirements for design, equipment and operation - has an exact answer inside one short instrument, and the marks are in the exactness. The Code's own logic is worth grasping before the numbers: it is a DESIGN standard, proved on sea trials and recorded in a Noise Survey Report, and it caps two different things in two different chapters - chapter 4 caps the SPACE, chapter 5 caps the PERSON. The single most surprising fact, stated expressly at paragraph 1.1.3, is that although the Code is mandatory under SOLAS, the whole of chapter 5 remains recommendatory. And the number that ties the Code together is 85 dB(A): the workshop limit, the warning-notice threshold, the level above which protectors are required, and the level protectors must bring the ear below.

Knowledge map
IMO Code on Noise Levels On Board Ships
  1. framing
    1. 1What the Noise Code is, and what made it mandatory
      • annex to resolution MSC.337(91), adopted 30 November 2012
      • made mandatory by MSC.338(91) inserting SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, in force 1 July 2014
      • II-1/3-12.3 - ships shall be constructed to reduce noise in accordance with the Code
  2. main
    1. 2Purpose and significance
      • para 1.2.1 - safe working conditions: speech communication, hearing audible alarms, clear-headed decisions in control stations
      • para 1.2.2 - protection from noise-induced hearing loss
      • para 1.2.3 - acceptable comfort in rest and recreation spaces, and conditions for RECUPERATION
    2. 3Areas of application
      • para 1.3.1 - new ships of 1,600 GT and above
      • SOLAS II-1/3-12.1 - contract on or after 1 July 2014; OR keel on or after 1 January 2015; OR delivery on or after 1 July 2018
      • II-1/3-12.2 - ships delivered before 1 July 2018 with keels laid 1 Jan 2009 to 1 Jan 2015: reduce machinery noise, insulate or isolate, refuge from noise, ear protectors
    3. 4Maximum allowable noise limits - chapter 4
      • 4.1.1 - these are MAXIMA, not desirable levels
      • 4.1.2 - assessed before the ship is put in service
      • machinery spaces 110; machinery control rooms 75; workshops and non-specified work spaces 85
    4. 5Noise exposure limits - chapter 5
      • 5.1.1 - the chapter 4 limits are designed to deliver an Lex(24) not exceeding 80 dB(A)
      • 5.1.2 - above 85 dB(A), protection or time limits
      • 5.1.4 - no unprotected exposure to peaks above 135 dB(C)
    5. 6Measures to reduce noise on board
      • 7.1 - protectors are NOT a substitute for effective noise control; control at source first
      • SOURCE - quieter machinery, resilient mounting, balancing and alignment, flexible couplings, silencers, propeller design and clearance
      • PATH - acoustic enclosure, insulation and damping, floating floors, avoiding structure-borne short circuits
    6. 7Impact on crew health and on performance
      • HEALTH - noise-induced hearing loss, originating in the cochlea (1.4.24); cumulative, painless, IRREVERSIBLE
      • HEALTH - acoustic trauma from peaks above 135 dB(C)
      • HEALTH - disturbed sleep, impaired recuperation, fatigue, stress and cardiovascular strain
    7. 8Compliance requirements - design, equipment and operation
      • DESIGN - predict, arrange, specify to chapter 4 limits; chapter 6 acoustic insulation
      • chapter 6 - Rw 35 cabin to cabin; Rw 45 public spaces to cabins and hospitals; Rw 30 corridor to cabin; Rw 30 cabin to cabin with communicating door
      • 6.2.2 laboratory test to ISO 10140-2; 6.3.2 field R'w tolerance up to 3 dB
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

framing
  1. What the Noise Code is, and what made it mandatory
main
  1. Purpose and significance
  2. Areas of application
  3. Maximum allowable noise limits - chapter 4
  4. Noise exposure limits - chapter 5
  5. Measures to reduce noise on board
  6. Impact on crew health and on performance
  7. Compliance requirements - design, equipment and operation

Memory cue. Walk inward from the noise: machinery 110 -> control room 75 -> workshop 85 -> bridge 65 -> radio 60 -> cabin 60 (55 on the big ship) (4). Chapter 4 caps the SPACE, chapter 5 caps the PERSON (5). Measures run SOURCE - PATH - RECEIVER, protectors last (6). Health is cochlea, cumulative, irreversible; performance is speech, alarms, decisions (7). Compliance is design, trials, report, notices, SMS (8). One number holds it: 85.

Remember8 route headings
Cover62 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 62 separate facts to learn individually.

  • made mandatory by MSC.338(91) inserting SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, in force 1 July 2014
