Model written answer

1. What the Noise Code is, and what made it mandatory

The Code on noise levels on board ships is the annex to resolution MSC.337(91), adopted by the Maritime Safety Committee on 30 November 2012. It replaced in practice the earlier recommendatory Code of resolution A.468(XII) of 1981.

It was made mandatory by resolution MSC.338(91), adopted on the same day, which inserted a new SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, "Protection against noise", in force 1 July 2014. Regulation II-1/3-12.3 provides that ships shall be constructed to reduce onboard noise and to protect personnel from noise in accordance with the Code. The same amendment deleted the former SOLAS regulation II-1/36, which had been the Convention's only previous mention of noise protection - so the new regulation did not merely add a requirement, it replaced a general exhortation with a measurable standard.

One nuance is worth stating early because it recurs throughout: although the Code is legally treated as a mandatory instrument under SOLAS, certain provisions remain recommendatory. Paragraph 1.1.3 lists them - paragraphs 1.3.2 and 1.3.3, 3.4.2 and 3.4.3, the whole of chapter 5, section 6.3, section 7.3 and appendices 2, 3 and 4. So the maximum limits in chapter 4 are mandatory, while the exposure limits in chapter 5 are recommendatory. Candidates who say the whole Code is mandatory, or that none of it is, both lose the point.

2. Purpose and significance

Paragraph 1.2 states the purpose: to limit noise levels and reduce seafarers' exposure to noise, in order to -

  1. provide for safe working conditions, having regard to the need for speech communication and for hearing audible alarms, and to an environment in which clear-headed decisions can be made in control stations, navigation and radio spaces and manned machinery spaces;
  2. protect the seafarer from excessive noise levels which may give rise to noise-induced hearing loss; and
  3. provide an acceptable degree of comfort in rest, recreation and other spaces, and conditions for recuperation from the effects of exposure to high noise levels.

The significance is in the ordering. Limb 1 is a safety objective, not a health one - noise that masks an alarm or degrades a decision is a navigational and operational hazard. Limb 2 is occupational health. Limb 3 is habitability, and it introduces the idea of recuperation: the Code does not only cap the working environment, it requires a quiet place to recover in, which is why cabin limits are the tightest in the table.

Paragraph 1.1.1 adds the design philosophy: the Code is intended to provide the basis for a design standard, with compliance based on the satisfactory conclusion of sea trials resulting in the issue of a Noise Survey Report; ongoing operational compliance is predicated on the crew being trained in personal protection and the maintenance of mitigation measures, enforced through the processes put in place under SOLAS chapter IX - the ISM Code.

3. Areas of application

Ships. Paragraph 1.3.1: the Code applies to new ships of 1,600 gross tonnage and above. "New ship" is defined at 1.4.22 by reference to SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12.1, which applies the regulation to ships of 1,600 GT and above:

  • for which the building contract is placed on or after 1 July 2014; or
  • in the absence of a building contract, the keels of which are laid or which are at a similar stage of construction on or after 1 January 2015; or
  • the delivery of which is on or after 1 July 2018.

Older ships are not simply exempt. Regulation II-1/3-12.2 catches ships delivered before 1 July 2018 whose keels were laid on or after 1 January 2009 but before 1 January 2015: for those, measures shall be taken to reduce machinery noise in machinery spaces to levels acceptable to the Administration, and if it cannot be sufficiently reduced the source shall be insulated or isolated, or a refuge from noise provided if the space must be manned, with ear protectors provided as necessary.

The Code itself is permissive in two further directions, and both are among the recommendatory provisions: paragraph 1.3.2 - the provisions on hazardous noise levels, mitigation and personal protective gear may be applied to existing ships of 1,600 GT and above so far as reasonable and practical; and paragraph 1.3.3 - the Code may be applied to new ships of less than 1,600 GT.

Excluded ship types - paragraph 1.3.4, eleven of them: dynamically supported craft; high-speed craft; fishing vessels; pipe-laying barges; crane barges; mobile offshore drilling units; pleasure yachts not engaged in trade; ships of war and troopships; ships not propelled by mechanical means; pile driving vessels; and dredgers. SOLAS II-1/3-12.4 cross-refers to this same list.

Spaces and conditions. The Code applies to ships in port or at sea with seafarers on board (1.3.5). It is not intended to apply to passenger cabins and other passenger spaces except so far as they are work spaces (1.3.8). It covers only ship-related noise sources such as machinery and propulsion, and not wind, wave or ice noise, alarms or public address systems (1.3.10). Where major repairs, alterations and modifications are made to an existing ship, the areas changed shall meet the new-ship requirements so far as the Administration deems reasonable and practicable (1.3.9). And the Administration may grant a dispensation in special circumstances where compliance is documented to be impossible despite reasonable technical noise reduction - but not for cabins, unless exceptional circumstances prevail (1.3.6).

4. Maximum allowable noise limits - chapter 4

Paragraph 4.1.1: these are maximum levels, not desirable levels, and where reasonably practicable it is desirable to be lower. Paragraph 4.1.2: they are assessed before the ship is put in service, by equivalent continuous sound level measurement. The limits are in dB(A):

Space1,600 to 10,000 GT10,000 GT and above
4.2.1 Work spaces
Machinery spaces110110
Machinery control rooms7575
Workshops other than those forming part of machinery spaces8585
Non-specified work spaces (other work areas)8585
4.2.2 Navigation spaces
Navigating bridge and chartrooms6565
Look-out posts, including navigating bridge wings and windows7070
Radio rooms (equipment operating but not producing audio signals)6060
Radar rooms6565
4.2.3 Accommodation spaces
Cabins and hospitals6055
Messrooms6560
Recreation rooms6560
Open recreation areas (external recreation areas)7575
Offices6560
4.2.4 Service spaces
Galleys, without food processing equipment operating7575
Serveries and pantries7575
4.2.5 Normally unoccupied spaces9090

Three points earn marks beyond the numbers. First, only the accommodation spaces differ by ship size - cabins, messrooms, recreation rooms and offices are 5 dB(A) tighter on ships of 10,000 GT and above; every work, navigation and service space limit is the same for both sizes. Second, the machinery space limit of 110 dB(A) is not a level anyone may be exposed to unprotected: paragraph 4.1.3 states that it assumes hearing protectors meeting chapter 7 are worn, and that personnel entering spaces above 85 dB(A) should be required to wear them. Third, the machinery control room at 75 dB(A) against a machinery space at 110 is the Code's clearest single design statement: a 35 dB(A) step, bought by the control room enclosure, so that speech and alarms work where the decisions are made.

5. Noise exposure limits - chapter 5

Chapter 4 caps the space; chapter 5 caps the person. Paragraph 5.1.1 ties them together: the chapter 4 limits are designed so that, if complied with, seafarers will not be exposed to an Lex(24) exceeding 80 dB(A) - the equivalent continuous noise exposure over each 24-hour period. For a new ship this is verified from sea trial measurements by calculating the expected exposure of each category of crew member.

  • 5.1.2 - in spaces above 85 dB(A), suitable hearing protection should be used, or time limits applied, to maintain an equivalent level of protection.
  • 5.1.4 - no crew member should be exposed unprotected to peak values exceeding 135 dB(C).
  • 5.3.1 - even wearing hearing protectors, no seafarer should be exposed above 120 dB(A) or to an Leq(24) above 105 dB(A).
  • 5.3.5 - for exposures of less than eight hours, seafarers without protection should not be exposed above 85 dB(A); where they remain more than eight hours, an Leq(24) of 80 dB(A) should not be exceeded - so for at least a third of each 24 hours each seafarer should be in an environment below 75 dB(A).
  • 5.4 - as an alternative to the zone method, no unprotected seafarer above an Leq(24) of 80 dB(A), and daily exposure in spaces requiring protectors not to exceed four hours continuously or eight hours in total.
  • 5.5 - a hearing conservation programme for those working where LAeq exceeds 85 dB(A): initial and periodic audiometric tests by a trained and qualified person, instruction on the hazards and on proper use of protectors, maintenance of audiometric records, and periodic analysis of those records.

Section 5.3 expresses all this as five zones, A to E, on a plot of level against duration: zone A no exposure at all; zones B and C occasional exposure only, with protectors attenuating 25 to 35 dB(A) and at least 25 dB(A) respectively; zone D daily exposure with protectors of at least 25 dB(A) and a hearing conservation programme to be considered; zone E no protection required. Remember the note under the figure: to work in zones A to D, protectors bringing the level at the ear below 85 dB(A) are required; in zone E they are not required but should be accessible if the level is over 80 dB(A) for more than eight hours.

6. Measures to reduce noise on board

The Code's own hierarchy is unambiguous, at section 7.1: hearing protection is supplied only when the application of means for controlling sound at source does not reduce the level, and the provision of hearing protectors shall not be considered a substitute for effective noise control. Measures therefore run source - path - receiver:

  • At source - selection of quieter machinery and low-noise fans and compressors; resilient mounting of engines, gensets, pumps and gearboxes; balancing and alignment; flexible couplings in piping and exhaust systems; attention to propeller design and hull clearance to limit propeller-excited hull noise; silencers on engine intakes, exhausts and ventilation.
  • On the path - acoustic enclosure of dominant sources; insulation and damping treatments on bulkheads, decks and casings; floating floors and resilient deck coverings; avoiding structure-borne short circuits between machinery seatings and accommodation; arrangement and layout - siting cabins and hospitals away from casings, machinery, funnel uptakes and mooring machinery, which is the cheapest measure of all because it costs nothing at the design stage and is unaffordable later.
  • At the receiver - the machinery control room as an acoustic refuge; the refuge from noise required for older ships under SOLAS II-1/3-12.2; hearing protectors under chapter 7; and administrative controls - job rotation, limiting continuous exposure to four hours and eight hours in total, and scheduling noisy work.

Appendix 3 of the Code, Suggested methods of attenuating noise, collects these; it is one of the recommendatory appendices.

7. Impact on crew health and on performance

The Code separates these, and so should the answer.

Health. The primary effect is noise-induced hearing loss, defined at 1.4.24 as a hearing loss originating in the nerve cells within the cochlea attributable to the effects of sound. It is cumulative, painless and irreversible - which is precisely why it is regulated by exposure dose over 24 hours rather than by momentary discomfort, and why audiometric testing under 5.5 is the only way it is detected before it is disabling. Very high peaks - above the 135 dB(C) limit at 5.1.4 - risk acoustic trauma, immediate mechanical damage rather than gradual loss. Beyond hearing, sustained noise contributes to disturbed sleep and impaired recuperation, fatigue, elevated stress response and cardiovascular strain - which is why paragraph 1.2.3 speaks of comfort in rest and recreation spaces and of conditions for recuperation, and why cabin limits are the tightest in the Code.

Performance. Paragraph 1.2.1 identifies the operational consequences directly:

  • masking of speech communication - orders and reports misheard, on the bridge, in the engine room and during mooring and emergency operations;
  • masking of audible alarms - the safety case for the 60 dB(A) radio room and 65 dB(A) bridge limits;
  • degradation of clear-headed decision-making in control stations, navigation and radio spaces and manned machinery spaces - the Code's own phrase;
  • fatigue and reduced alertness carried into the next watch through disturbed rest, which links this Code directly to the fatigue regime and to MLC Regulation 4.3 on health and safety protection;
  • reduced situational awareness, including the loss of the diagnostic information an engineer takes from the sound of running machinery when protectors are worn.

The two are connected. A noisy ship is not only a ship whose crew lose hearing over a career; it is a ship on which an alarm is missed tonight. That is why noise sits in SOLAS chapter II-1 as a construction requirement and not merely in occupational health guidance.

8. Compliance requirements - design, equipment and operation

StageWhat is requiredWhere
DesignThe Code is a design standard. Predict levels, arrange spaces, specify machinery and treatments to meet the chapter 4 limits; provide acoustic insulation between accommodation spaces to the chapter 6 indices1.1.1, chapter 4, chapter 6
Construction / commissioningVerify by measurement under chapter 3 conditions; sea trials; produce the Noise Survey Report in the appendix 1 format before the ship is put in service1.1.1, 4.1.2, 4.3
EquipmentHearing protectors able to reduce levels to 85 dB(A) or less, selected by the HML method of ISO 4869-2:1994; noise-cancelling headsets permitted if equivalent in the unpowered condition; warning notices at entrances7.2.2, 7.2.2.1, 7.4
OperationExposure control and time limits; a section in the Safety Management System on the company's hearing protection policy, exposure limits and training, logged in training records; a hearing conservation programme with audiometric testing; the Noise Survey Report carried on board and accessible for the crew5.1.2, 5.1.3, 5.4, 5.5, 4.3.3

The Noise Survey Report (section 4.3) is the certificate-equivalent of this Code, and its detail is examinable: one report per ship, comprising information on the noise levels in the various spaces, showing the reading at each specified measuring point, with the points marked on a general arrangement plan or accommodation drawings attached to the report or otherwise identified; in the appendix 1 format; and it shall always be carried on board and be accessible for the crew.

Acoustic insulation between accommodation spaces (chapter 6) is the requirement most often forgotten. The airborne sound insulation of bulkheads and decks within the accommodation must meet, as a minimum, the weighted sound reduction index Rw of ISO 717-1:

  • cabin to cabin - Rw 35;
  • messrooms, recreation rooms, public spaces and entertainment areas to cabins and hospitals - Rw 45;
  • corridor to cabin - Rw 30;
  • cabin to cabin with a communicating door - Rw 30.

These are determined by laboratory test to ISO 10140-2 (6.2.2); where erection of materials is in doubt, field measurements may be taken and the apparent index R'w should comply with a tolerance of up to 3 dB (6.3.2).

Warning notices (section 7.4). Where the noise level in machinery spaces or other spaces exceeds 85 dB(A), entrances shall carry a warning notice comprising symbol and supplementary sign in the working language of the ship; if only a minor portion of the space is affected, the particular location or equipment shall be identified at eye level, visible from each direction of access. The Code prints four bands:

LevelSign
80-85 dB(A)HIGH-NOISE LEVEL - USE HEARING PROTECTORS
85-110 dB(A)DANGEROUS NOISE - USE OF HEARING PROTECTORS MANDATORY
110-115 dB(A)CAUTION: DANGEROUS NOISE - USE OF HEARING PROTECTORS MANDATORY - SHORT STAY ONLY
above 115 dB(A)CAUTION: EXCESSIVELY HIGH-NOISE LEVEL - USE OF HEARING PROTECTORS MANDATORY - NO STAY LONGER THAN 10 MINUTES
Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks in one limb, but the stem is really a five-part checklist and the examiner will mark against it: purpose and significance; key provisions including maximum allowable noise limits, areas of application and measures to reduce noise; the impact on crew health and performance; and the compliance requirements for ship design, equipment and operational practices. Miss one and you lose that block of marks however good the rest is. The route follows the printed order deliberately, so the answer can be marked straight down the page.

The discriminator on this question is numbers. Almost every candidate can write that noise is bad for hearing and that ear defenders should be worn. Very few can give the machinery space limit, the cabin limits and the fact that they differ by ship size, the 85 dB(A) threshold, the Lex(24) 80 dB(A) criterion and the four Rw values. Those are where the marks are, and they exist only in the Code.

The one distinction that separates a good answer from a correct one

Chapter 4 is mandatory; chapter 5 is not. Paragraph 1.1.3 lists the provisions that remain recommendatory, and the whole of chapter 5 - the exposure limits - is on that list, along with 1.3.2 and 1.3.3, 3.4.2 and 3.4.3, section 6.3, section 7.3 and appendices 2, 3 and 4.

So the space limits in the table are a hard SOLAS requirement; the personal exposure limits, the zones and the hearing conservation programme are recommendatory even though the Code is a mandatory instrument. This is genuinely counter-intuitive, it is stated expressly in the Code, and saying it correctly demonstrates the Code has been opened rather than summarised.

Application dates: the part most answers get wrong

"Applies to new ships of 1,600 GT and above" is only the first half. The three date criteria come from SOLAS II-1/3-12.1, not from the Code: contract on or after 1 July 2014; absent a contract, keel laid on or after 1 January 2015; or delivery on or after 1 July 2018. Any one of the three brings the ship in.

And older ships are not simply outside. Regulation II-1/3-12.2 imposes a real duty on ships delivered before 1 July 2018 whose keels were laid on or after 1 January 2009 but before 1 January 2015: reduce machinery space noise to levels acceptable to the Administration, and failing that insulate or isolate the source or provide a refuge from noise if the space must be manned. An answer that mentions this second tier is describing the regulation; one that stops at the first is describing half of it.

The numbers most worth carrying

  • 110 machinery spaces - and it assumes protectors are worn (4.1.3).
  • 75 machinery control rooms - the 35 dB(A) step that makes the control room work.
  • 85 workshops and non-specified work spaces - and also the threshold for warning notices and for requiring protectors.
  • 65 navigating bridge and chartrooms; 60 radio rooms; 70 look-out posts and bridge wings.
  • 60 / 55 cabins and hospitals - the only figures where ship size matters, tighter on 10,000 GT and above.
  • 90 normally unoccupied spaces; 75 galleys, serveries and pantries, and open recreation areas.
  • Lex(24) 80 dB(A) - the exposure criterion the whole table is designed to deliver.
  • 135 dB(C) unprotected peak; 120 dB(A) and Leq(24) 105 dB(A) even with protectors.
  • Rw 35 / 45 / 30 / 30 - cabin-cabin, public spaces to cabins, corridor-cabin, cabin-cabin with communicating door; 3 dB field tolerance.

If time is short, the four that carry most credit are 110, 75, 85 and 60/55, because they let you describe the whole design logic - protect the ear in the machinery space, protect speech in the control room, mark the hazard threshold, and protect sleep in the cabin.

Health versus performance - answer both, separately

The stem says "the impact of noise on crew health and performance", which is two things. Health is the cochlear, cumulative, irreversible hearing loss, plus acoustic trauma from peaks, plus disturbed sleep, fatigue and stress. Performance is masking of speech and alarms, degraded decision-making in control stations, reduced alertness on the following watch, and loss of the diagnostic sound cues an engineer relies on when protectors are worn.

The sentence that ties them together, and is worth stating explicitly: a noisy ship is not only one whose crew lose hearing over a career, it is one on which an alarm is missed tonight - which is why the requirement sits in SOLAS chapter II-1 as a construction standard rather than in health guidance alone.

Common mistakes

  • Saying the whole Code is mandatory. Chapter 5, sections 6.3 and 7.3, paragraphs 1.3.2, 1.3.3, 3.4.2, 3.4.3 and appendices 2 to 4 are recommendatory - paragraph 1.1.3.
  • Giving 1,600 GT without the three date criteria from SOLAS II-1/3-12.1.
  • Saying existing ships are exempt. The 2009-2015 build window carries a real machinery-noise duty under II-1/3-12.2.
  • Quoting one set of limits and not noticing that accommodation limits are 5 dB(A) tighter at 10,000 GT and above.
  • Presenting 110 dB(A) as an acceptable unprotected exposure. It assumes hearing protectors are worn.
  • Treating hearing protectors as the primary control. Section 7.1 says they are not a substitute for effective noise control.
  • Omitting chapter 6 acoustic insulation entirely - the four Rw values are quick marks.
  • Omitting the Noise Survey Report, which is the compliance document and must be carried on board and be accessible to the crew.
  • Forgetting that the Code covers only ship-related sources - not wind, wave, ice, alarms or public address (1.3.10).
  • Answering only on health when the stem also asks about performance.

Examiner traps

  • "Is the Noise Code mandatory?" Its application is mandatory under SOLAS II-1/3-12, but specified parts of it remain recommendatory - and chapter 5 is one of them.
  • "Your machinery space measures 108 dB(A). Compliant?" Against the chapter 4 limit of 110, yes - but personnel entering must wear protectors, the entrance must carry the 85-110 band notice, and the individual's Lex(24) still has to be controlled.
  • "Can the Administration dispense with a cabin limit?" Only in exceptional circumstances - dispensations under 1.3.6 shall not include cabins unless exceptional circumstances prevail.
  • "Which SOLAS regulation did this replace?" MSC.338(91) deleted the former regulation II-1/36 when it inserted II-1/3-12.
  • "What must the noise survey report show?" The reading at each specified measuring point, with the points marked on a general arrangement plan or accommodation drawings.
  • "How long may a seafarer work in a space requiring protectors?" Section 5.4 - not more than four hours continuously or eight hours in total in a day.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which resolution is the Noise Code, and which made it mandatory? MSC.337(91); MSC.338(91) inserting SOLAS II-1/3-12, in force 1 July 2014.
  • What is the limit in a machinery space and in a machinery control room? 110 and 75 dB(A).
  • What is the cabin limit? 60 dB(A), or 55 on ships of 10,000 GT and above.
  • At what level must a warning notice be posted? Above 85 dB(A).
  • What is the daily exposure criterion? Lex(24) not exceeding 80 dB(A).
  • What peak may an unprotected seafarer not exceed? 135 dB(C).
  • What sound reduction index is required cabin to cabin? Rw 35; and Rw 45 from public spaces to cabins and hospitals.
  • Which ships are excluded? The eleven types at Code paragraph 1.3.4 - dynamically supported craft, high-speed craft, fishing vessels, pipe-laying barges, crane barges, MODUs, pleasure yachts not in trade, warships and troopships, ships not mechanically propelled, pile driving vessels and dredgers.
  • What document evidences compliance? The Noise Survey Report, in the appendix 1 format, carried on board and accessible to the crew.

Memory framework

Walk the ship from the noisiest space inward and the whole table falls out: machinery space 110 -> control room 75 -> workshop 85 -> bridge 65 -> radio 60 -> cabin 60 (55 big ship). The single number that ties the Code together is 85 - it is the workshop limit, the warning-notice threshold, the level above which protectors are required, and the level protectors must bring the ear below.

For the structure of the Code: 1 general, 2 equipment, 3 measurement, 4 LIMITS, 5 EXPOSURE, 6 INSULATION, 7 PROTECTION - and the answer's five demands map onto chapters 1, 4, 5, 6 and 7. For measures: SOURCE - PATH - RECEIVER, in that order, because the Code insists protectors are last.

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution MSC.337(91), 30 November 2012 - the Code on noise levels on board ships
  • Resolution MSC.338(91), 30 November 2012, in force 1 July 2014 - inserted SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12 and deleted the former II-1/36
  • SOLAS II-1/3-12.1 - the three application criteria; .2 the 2009-2015 build window duty; .3 the mandatory-instrument provision; .4 exclusions by reference to Code 1.3.4
  • Code 1.1.3 - the provisions that remain recommendatory, including all of chapter 5
  • Code 1.2 - the threefold purpose; 1.3 application; 1.3.4 the eleven excluded types; 1.3.6 dispensations, not for cabins
  • Code chapter 4 - maximum acceptable sound pressure levels; 4.2 the limits table; 4.3 the Noise Survey Report
  • Code chapter 5 - exposure limits; 5.1.1 Lex(24) 80 dB(A); 5.3 zones A-E; 5.4 four/eight hours; 5.5 hearing conservation programme
  • Code chapter 6 - acoustic insulation; 6.2.1 Rw 35 / 45 / 30 / 30; 6.3.2 3 dB field tolerance
  • Code chapter 7 - hearing protection; 7.1 not a substitute for noise control; 7.2.2 reduce to 85 dB(A) or less; 7.4 the four warning-notice bands
  • Resolution A.468(XII) - the 1981 recommendatory Code, the predecessor; A.343(IX) - listening posts, expressly not superseded

Uncertainty and currency

Every dB figure, date and paragraph number in this answer comes from MSC.337(91) and MSC.338(91) read in full. Three limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed IMO consolidated SOLAS edition, so regulation II-1/3-12 is taken from its adopting resolution rather than from a consolidated text - sound for the regulation as adopted, and no later amendment to it was identified as at this sitting. Figure 1 of the Code is a graph and is described by its numerical anchors rather than redrawn. And no epidemiological statistic on hearing loss prevalence among seafarers is asserted; the health effects given are those the Code itself identifies.

On currency, this question is unusually quiet: the Code is unamended at this sitting and nothing in 2024 touched it. That is worth knowing precisely because the rest of this paper is not quiet - Q6 and Q8 both turn on instruments that moved during 2024. Here the risk is not staleness but imprecision.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing What the Noise Code is, and what made it mandatory
  2. __________main Purpose and significance
  3. __________main Areas of application
  4. __________main Maximum allowable noise limits - chapter 4
  5. __________main Noise exposure limits - chapter 5
  6. __________main Measures to reduce noise on board
  7. __________main Impact on crew health and on performance
  8. __________main Compliance requirements - design, equipment and operation
Flashcards 13

1 What the Code is and what made it mandatory. 2 Purpose and significance. 3 Areas of application. 4 Maximum allowable limits. 5 Noise exposure limits. 6 Measures to reduce noise. 7 Impact on health and on performance. 8 Compliance in design, equipment and operation.

The stem is a five-part checklist and the route follows its printed order so the answer can be marked straight down the page.

The Code is the annex to resolution MSC.337(91), adopted 30 November 2012. Resolution MSC.338(91) inserted SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, Protection against noise, in force 1 July 2014, which requires ships to be constructed in accordance with the Code. The same amendment deleted the former SOLAS regulation II-1/36.

The deletion of II-1/36 shows the regulation replaced a general exhortation with a measurable standard.

No. Paragraph 1.1.3 lists provisions that remain recommendatory, options for compliance or informative: paragraphs 1.3.2 and 1.3.3, 3.4.2 and 3.4.3, THE WHOLE OF CHAPTER 5, section 6.3, section 7.3 and appendices 2, 3 and 4. So the chapter 4 space limits are mandatory while the chapter 5 exposure limits are not.

Counter-intuitive, stated expressly, and the clearest sign the Code has been opened rather than summarised.

New ships of 1,600 GT and above. Under SOLAS II-1/3-12.1: building contract placed on or after 1 July 2014; or, absent a contract, keel laid or at a similar stage on or after 1 January 2015; or delivery on or after 1 July 2018. Any one criterion brings the ship in.

The three dates come from the SOLAS regulation, not from the Code, and most answers give only the tonnage.

No. SOLAS II-1/3-12.2 catches ships delivered before 1 July 2018 whose keels were laid on or after 1 January 2009 but before 1 January 2015: measures shall be taken to reduce machinery space noise to levels acceptable to the Administration; if it cannot be sufficiently reduced the source shall be insulated or isolated, or a refuge from noise provided if the space must be manned, with ear protectors provided as necessary.

The second tier of the regulation, and the part most answers omit entirely.

Machinery spaces 110 dB(A); machinery control rooms 75 dB(A); navigating bridge and chartrooms 65 dB(A); radio rooms 60 dB(A); cabins and hospitals 60 dB(A), or 55 dB(A) on ships of 10,000 GT and above.

The 110-to-75 step across the control room bulkhead is the Code's clearest design statement.

Only the accommodation spaces - cabins and hospitals, messrooms, recreation rooms and offices - are 5 dB(A) tighter on ships of 10,000 GT and above. Work, navigation and service space limits are identical for both sizes. Paragraph 4.1.3 states that the 110 dB(A) limit assumes hearing protectors meeting chapter 7 are worn, and that personnel entering spaces above 85 dB(A) should be required to wear them.

Two traps in one: quoting a single column, and presenting 110 dB(A) as a permissible unprotected exposure.

The chapter 4 limits are designed so that Lex(24) does not exceed 80 dB(A). Above 85 dB(A) protection or time limits apply. No unprotected exposure to peaks above 135 dB(C). Even wearing protectors, not above 120 dB(A) or Leq(24) 105 dB(A). Daily exposure in spaces requiring protectors not to exceed four hours continuously or eight hours in total. Where a seafarer is over eight hours in a high-noise space, Leq(24) 80 dB(A) should not be exceeded, so at least a third of each 24 hours should be below 75 dB(A).

Chapter 4 caps the space; chapter 5 caps the person. Both are needed for a complete answer.

Weighted sound reduction index Rw to ISO 717-1: cabin to cabin 35; messrooms, recreation rooms, public spaces and entertainment areas to cabins and hospitals 45; corridor to cabin 30; cabin to cabin with a communicating door 30. Determined by laboratory test to ISO 10140-2; in the field the apparent index R'w should comply with a tolerance of up to 3 dB.

Four quick numbers that most answers omit entirely because they forget chapter 6 exists.

A report made for each ship, comprising information on the noise levels in the various spaces on board and showing the reading at each specified measuring point, with the points marked on a general arrangement plan or accommodation drawings attached to the report or otherwise identified. It is in the format of appendix 1 and shall always be carried on board and be accessible for the crew.

It is the compliance document for this Code - the equivalent of a certificate - and 'accessible for the crew' is the part that is forgotten.

They are supplied when the application of means for controlling sound at source does not reduce the noise level to that specified, and their provision shall NOT be considered to be a substitute for effective noise control.

It fixes the source-path-receiver hierarchy and stops the answer becoming a paragraph about ear defenders.

Where noise in machinery or other spaces exceeds 85 dB(A), entrances shall carry a warning notice with symbol and supplementary sign in the ship's working language. The four bands are 80-85 dB(A) high noise, use hearing protectors; 85-110 dangerous noise, use mandatory; 110-115 mandatory and short stay only; above 115 mandatory and no stay longer than ten minutes.

The ten-minute limit above 115 dB(A) is exact, memorable and rarely quoted.

Health: noise-induced hearing loss originating in the cochlea - cumulative, painless and irreversible; acoustic trauma from peaks; disturbed sleep, impaired recuperation, fatigue, stress and cardiovascular strain. Performance: masking of speech communication and of audible alarms; degraded clear-headed decision-making in control stations, navigation and radio spaces; reduced alertness on the following watch; loss of diagnostic machinery sound cues when protectors are worn.

The stem asks for both, and they are different arguments - one is a career, the other is tonight's watch.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Code on noise levels on board ships = annex to MSC.337(91), adopted 30 Nov 2012; made mandatory by MSC.338(91) inserting SOLAS II-1/3-12, in force 1 July 2014 (which also deleted the old II-1/36); predecessor the recommendatory A.468(XII) of 1981. Mandatory instrument BUT para 1.1.3 keeps parts recommendatory - including ALL of chapter 5, plus 1.3.2, 1.3.3, 3.4.2, 3.4.3, 6.3, 7.3 and appendices 2-4. PURPOSE (1.2): safe working conditions - speech communication, audible alarms, clear-headed decisions; protection from noise-induced hearing loss; comfort in rest and recreation and conditions for recuperation. APPLICATION: new ships 1,600 GT+; SOLAS II-1/3-12.1 - contract on/after 1 Jul 2014, or keel on/after 1 Jan 2015, or delivery on/after 1 Jul 2018. II-1/3-12.2 - delivered before 1 Jul 2018 with keel 1 Jan 2009 to 1 Jan 2015: reduce machinery noise, else insulate/isolate or provide a refuge from noise, ear protectors as needed. Excluded (1.3.4), eleven types: dynamically supported craft, HSC, fishing vessels, pipe-laying barges, crane barges, MODUs, pleasure yachts not in trade, warships and troopships, non-mechanically propelled, pile drivers, dredgers. Applies in port and at sea with seafarers aboard; not passenger spaces except as work spaces; ship sources only - not wind, wave, ice, alarms or PA. Dispensations possible but not for cabins unless exceptional. LIMITS dB(A), chapter 4 (MAXIMA): machinery 110; machinery control room 75; workshops and non-specified work spaces 85; bridge and chartroom 65; look-out posts and bridge wings 70; radio room 60; radar room 65; cabins and hospitals 60 / 55; messrooms, recreation rooms, offices 65 / 60; open recreation 75; galleys, serveries, pantries 75; normally unoccupied 90. Only accommodation differs by size (tighter at 10,000 GT+). 110 assumes protectors are worn (4.1.3). EXPOSURE, chapter 5: design target Lex(24) 80 dB(A); above 85 protectors or time limits; unprotected peak not above 135 dB(C); even with protectors not above 120 dB(A) or Leq(24) 105; over eight hours Leq(24) 80, so a third of each 24 h below 75; max 4 h continuous / 8 h total in protector spaces; hearing conservation programme with audiometric tests. INSULATION, chapter 6: Rw 35 cabin-cabin; Rw 45 public spaces to cabins/hospitals; Rw 30 corridor-cabin; Rw 30 cabin-cabin with communicating door; field R'w tolerance 3 dB. PROTECTION, chapter 7: not a substitute for noise control (7.1); protectors to bring level to 85 dB(A) or less, HML method ISO 4869-2; warning notices above 85, bands 80-85 / 85-110 / 110-115 / >115 (no stay longer than 10 minutes). COMPLIANCE: design standard proved on sea trials; Noise Survey Report (appendix 1 format) showing the reading at each point marked on a GA plan, always carried on board and accessible to the crew; SMS section on hearing protection policy, exposure limits and training.

Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, inserted by resolution MSC.338(91) and in force 1 July 2014, requires ships to be constructed to reduce onboard noise and to protect personnel from noise in accordance with the Code on noise levels on board ships adopted by resolution MSC.337(91); it applies to ships of 1,600 gross tonnage and above contracted on or after 1 July 2014, or keel-laid on or after 1 January 2015, or delivered on or after 1 July 2018.
Major trap
Writing a general essay on noise and hearing protection instead of producing the Code's actual numbers. The stem asks for FIVE things - purpose and significance, maximum allowable limits, areas of application, measures to reduce noise, impact on health AND performance, and compliance requirements for design, equipment and operation - and each is a separate block of marks. Three specific traps: the whole Code is NOT mandatory (paragraph 1.1.3 keeps all of chapter 5 recommendatory); the 110 dB(A) machinery space limit ASSUMES hearing protectors are worn and is not a permissible unprotected exposure; and only the ACCOMMODATION limits change with ship size, being 5 dB(A) tighter at 10,000 GT and above.

Also on the platform: QP2412 Q6 - MLC 2006, under which health and safety protection and accident prevention is a certifiable matter · QP2412 Q5 - active and latent failures, and how a noise-degraded working environment becomes a latent condition · QP2412 Q4 - the classification and survey framework under which the noise survey report is verified

Understand this first

The NIR is one idea with a lot of consequences, and if you state the idea first the rest of limb (a) writes itself. The idea is that targeting stopped being a score a ship accumulates and became a PROFILE the ship carries - recalculated daily from what the ship is, who stands behind it, and what it has done over 36 months - and that profile decides both how often the ship is inspected and what kind of inspection it gets. The second structural change is just as important: the region stopped promising that each State would inspect a quarter of its callers and started promising that the region collectively would inspect them all. That is why THETIS and the arrival-reporting obligations had to exist at all. Limb (b) is where the marks are won or lost, because it asks three things - pros, cons, and what an owner should actually DO - and the third needs a management programme aimed at the profile itself, not a paragraph about maintaining the ship properly.

Knowledge map
New Inspection Regime of the Paris MoU
  1. a)
    1. 1What the NIR is, and what it replaced
      • entered into force 1 January 2011, in parallel with the EU port State control Directive
      • replaced the TARGET FACTOR with the SHIP RISK PROFILE
      • profile decides both HOW OFTEN and WHAT KIND of inspection
    2. 2The Ship Risk Profile
      • three categories - High Risk, Standard Risk, Low Risk; Standard is the RESIDUAL category
      • type of ship - chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker, bulk carrier, passenger ship are higher risk
      • age of ship - over 12 years attracts higher risk
    3. 3Company performance - the new parameter
      • the genuinely novel element of the NIR
      • formula over the company's fleet deficiencies and detentions in the last 36 months
      • identified by IMO company number
    4. 4The selection scheme - windows and priorities
      • High Risk 5 to 6 months; Standard Risk 10 to 12 months; Low Risk 24 to 36 months
      • measured from the last inspection in the region; the span RESTARTS after any inspection
      • Priority II - window OPENED, ship MAY be selected
    5. 5Types of inspection
      • initial - certificates, documents, overall condition
      • more detailed - where CLEAR GROUNDS are established
      • expanded - defined-scope examination of risk areas
    6. 6Reporting obligations and THETIS
      • ETA72 - 72 hours, from ships eligible for expanded inspection
      • ETA72 content includes hull configuration, cargo and ballast tank condition, volume and type of cargo for tankers, and the date of the last expanded inspection
      • ETA24 - 24 hours, from ALL ships, at any time regardless of the window
    7. 7Refusal of access - banning
      • extended from certain ship types to ALL types, and from Black List flags to Grey List flags too
      • black-listed flag - banned after the THIRD detention in the last 36 months
      • grey-listed flag - banned after the THIRD detention in the last 24 months
  2. b)
    1. 8Pros of the NIR
      • risk-proportionate use of finite inspection resource
      • real reward for quality - LRS inspected every 24 to 36 months instead of annually
      • no escape by routing, because the regional commitment covers all ships
    2. 9Cons of the NIR
      • collective punishment - a good ship penalised for sister vessels' detentions
      • flag and RO parameters largely outside the individual operator's control
      • 36-month historic lag - quicker to punish than to reward
    3. 10How owners and managers should respond
      • MEASURE - calculate the profile for every ship, know the window dates, treat movement to Low Risk as a board-level KPI
      • CHOOSE - deficiencies and detentions are controllable; flag and RO are not controllable but ARE selectable, so flag and class choice become risk decisions
      • PROTECT THE FLEET - one detention degrades every sister ship, so corrective action must be fleet-wide, through ISM root cause analysis, CAPA, internal audit and management review
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

a)
  1. What the NIR is, and what it replaced
  2. The Ship Risk Profile
  3. Company performance - the new parameter
  4. The selection scheme - windows and priorities
  5. Types of inspection
  6. Reporting obligations and THETIS
  7. Refusal of access - banning
b)
  1. Pros of the NIR
  2. Cons of the NIR
  3. How owners and managers should respond

Memory cue. Limb (a) in five beats: PROFILE - WINDOW - PRIORITY - INSPECTION - BAN (steps 2, 4, 4, 5, 7), with reporting and THETIS as the plumbing (6). Profile parameters: what the ship IS (type, age), who STANDS BEHIND it (flag, RO, company), what it has DONE (deficiencies, detentions). Windows: 5-6 / 10-12 / 24-36. Limb (b) response: MEASURE it, CHOOSE what you can, PROTECT the fleet, PREPARE the ship, HANDLE the day (10).

Remember10 route headings
Cover76 core points beneath them

Memorise the 10 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 76 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What the NIR is, and what it replaced
  • entered into force 1 January 2011, in parallel with the EU port State control Directive
  • replaced the TARGET FACTOR with the SHIP RISK PROFILE
  • profile decides both HOW OFTEN and WHAT KIND of inspection
  • replaced each State inspecting 25% of individual ships with a SHARED regional commitment to inspect ALL ships
  • ranked the ISM company by performance for the first time
  • region: European coastal States and the North Atlantic basin from North America to Europe
  • at this sitting 27 maritime authorities, with the Russian Federation SUSPENDED
2. The Ship Risk Profile
  • three categories - High Risk, Standard Risk, Low Risk; Standard is the RESIDUAL category
  • type of ship - chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker, bulk carrier, passenger ship are higher risk
  • age of ship - over 12 years attracts higher risk
  • flag performance - White, Grey or Black List, and whether the IMO audit is complete
  • recognized organization performance
  • company performance
  • number of deficiencies and number of detentions over 36 months in the region
  • RECALCULATED DAILY; a sister ship's inspection can immediately move this ship's profile
  • weightings are in Annex 7 Table 1 and are not expected to be reproduced
3. Company performance - the new parameter
  • the genuinely novel element of the NIR
  • formula over the company's fleet deficiencies and detentions in the last 36 months
  • identified by IMO company number
  • compared against the average of all vessels inspected in the region
  • ranked very low, low, medium or high
  • any refusal of access of one of its ships damages the ranking
  • intent: reach past the ship to the quality of MANAGEMENT
4. The selection scheme - windows and priorities
  • High Risk 5 to 6 months; Standard Risk 10 to 12 months; Low Risk 24 to 36 months
  • measured from the last inspection in the region; the span RESTARTS after any inspection
  • Priority II - window OPENED, ship MAY be selected
  • Priority I - window PASSED, ship WILL be selected and must be inspected
  • before the window opens and absent factors, NO priority status - inspection not obligatory
  • OVERRIDING factor - Priority I irrespective of the window; e.g. collision, discharge of harmful substances
  • UNEXPECTED factor - Priority II irrespective of the window; e.g. failure to report ETA, outstanding deficiencies, cargo problems
5. Types of inspection
  • initial - certificates, documents, overall condition
  • more detailed - where CLEAR GROUNDS are established
  • expanded - defined-scope examination of risk areas
  • on a periodic inspection an EXPANDED inspection is mandatory for every High Risk Ship
  • and for every bulk carrier, chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker or passenger ship OVER 12 YEARS OLD, whatever its profile
  • other Standard and Low Risk Ships get an initial inspection, escalating on clear grounds
  • any ADDITIONAL inspection is at least a more detailed inspection
6. Reporting obligations and THETIS
  • ETA72 - 72 hours, from ships eligible for expanded inspection
  • ETA72 content includes hull configuration, cargo and ballast tank condition, volume and type of cargo for tankers, and the date of the last expanded inspection
  • ETA24 - 24 hours, from ALL ships, at any time regardless of the window
  • ATA and ATD - actual times of arrival and departure, new under the NIR
  • THETIS is the NIR information database, fed through SafeSeaNet
  • FAILURE TO REPORT IS ITSELF AN UNEXPECTED FACTOR - that is the enforcement mechanism
7. Refusal of access - banning
  • extended from certain ship types to ALL types, and from Black List flags to Grey List flags too
  • black-listed flag - banned after the THIRD detention in the last 36 months
  • grey-listed flag - banned after the THIRD detention in the last 24 months
  • after a SECOND ban, any subsequent detention leads to a ban regardless of flag listing
  • jumping detention, or failing to sail to an agreed repair yard, leads to a ban
  • lifting - 3 months, then 12 months, then 24 months with more stringent conditions, then PERMANENT
  • lists published annually from a three-year rolling period, minimum 30 inspections; the 2023 lists applied from 1 July 2024 are operative here
8. Pros of the NIR
  • risk-proportionate use of finite inspection resource
  • real reward for quality - LRS inspected every 24 to 36 months instead of annually
  • no escape by routing, because the regional commitment covers all ships
  • reaches the COMPANY, where substandard operation originates
  • transparent and predictable - the operator can calculate the profile in advance
  • escalating published banning drives persistent offenders out, and pressures flags and ROs
  • harmonisation across member States through common criteria and a common database
9. Cons of the NIR
  • collective punishment - a good ship penalised for sister vessels' detentions
  • flag and RO parameters largely outside the individual operator's control
  • 36-month historic lag - quicker to punish than to reward
  • age over 12 years is a crude proxy for condition
  • administrative burden of ETA72, ETA24, ATA and ATD
  • 5 to 6 month expanded-inspection cycle is heavy for high risk ships
  • residual inconsistency in deficiency coding and professional judgement between PSCOs
  • severe consequences of banning, with appeal that does not suspend detention
  • perverse incentives toward cosmetic preparation rather than genuine improvement
10. How owners and managers should respond
  • MEASURE - calculate the profile for every ship, know the window dates, treat movement to Low Risk as a board-level KPI
  • CHOOSE - deficiencies and detentions are controllable; flag and RO are not controllable but ARE selectable, so flag and class choice become risk decisions
  • PROTECT THE FLEET - one detention degrades every sister ship, so corrective action must be fleet-wide, through ISM root cause analysis, CAPA, internal audit and management review
  • PROTECT THE FLEET - PSC record due diligence on acquisition and on taking over management
  • PREPARE - self-inspection and mock PSC built on published deficiency statistics; focus on ISM, fire safety, LSA, MLC and pollution prevention
  • PREPARE - readiness for expanded inspection scope; officers able to DEMONSTRATE systems, not just produce certificates; prepare for announced Concentrated Inspection Campaigns
  • HANDLE THE DAY - rigorous ETA/ATA/ATD reporting, since failure is an unexpected factor
  • HANDLE THE DAY - senior officer accompanies the PSCO; rectify before departure; never jump detention or miss the agreed repair yard
  • HANDLE THE DAY - appeal to the national authority or through the flag State, but the appeal does NOT lift the detention
  • STRATEGIC CLOSE - inspection frequency is now a commercial variable management can move
Model written answer

1. What the NIR is, and what it replaced

The New Inspection Regime (NIR) of the Paris Memorandum of Understanding on Port State Control entered into force on 1 January 2011. It was implemented in parallel with the European Union's port State control Directive, so within the EU and EFTA member States it is law as well as an administrative arrangement.

It changed the regime in three fundamental ways:

  1. the Target Factor was replaced by the Ship Risk Profile. Under the old system a ship accumulated points and was targeted when it crossed a threshold. Under the NIR every ship carries a continuously recalculated risk profile placing it in one of three categories, and that profile determines how often it is inspected and what kind of inspection it gets;
  2. the commitment changed from each member State inspecting 25% of individual ships calling at its ports to a shared commitment of the region as a whole to inspect ALL ships visiting its ports and anchorages. Coverage became collective rather than proportional, so a ship can no longer evade inspection by routing to a State that has met its quota;
  3. the company responsible for ISM management is ranked by performance for the first time, and that ranking feeds the profile of every ship in its fleet.

The regional scope covers the European coastal States and the coastal States of the North Atlantic basin from North America to Europe. At this sitting the Memorandum records 27 maritime authorities as members, with the Russian Federation suspended until further notice.

2. The Ship Risk Profile

The Ship Risk Profile classifies every ship as a High Risk Ship (HRS), a Standard Risk Ship (SRS) or a Low Risk Ship (LRS). A ship that is neither high nor low risk is a standard risk ship - so SRS is the residual category, not a middle band anyone qualifies for.

It is calculated from generic and historic parameters, using the record of inspections in the Paris MoU area over the last 36 months:

ParameterEffect
Type of shipChemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker, bulk carrier and passenger ship are the higher-risk types
Age of shipShips over 12 years old attract higher risk
Flag performancePosition on the White, Grey or Black List; and whether the flag has completed the IMO audit
Recognized organization performanceThe performance of the RO(s) that issued the ship's statutory certificates, itself listed as high, medium, low or very low
Company performanceThe ISM company's performance ranking - see below
Number of deficienciesDeficiencies recorded at inspections in the region over 36 months
Number of detentionsDetentions in the region over 36 months

Two features of the calculation matter practically. It is recalculated daily, so a profile is never static. And because company performance is one of the inputs, the inspection result of one ship can immediately affect the profile of every other ship in the same ISM company - a genuinely new form of collective consequence.

In broad terms a Low Risk Ship is a newer ship of a non-high-risk type, on a White List flag whose administration has completed the IMO audit, classed and certified by a high-performing recognized organization, operated by a high-performing company, with few deficiencies and no detentions over the period. A High Risk Ship is at the opposite end - a higher-risk type, older, on a Black or Grey List flag, with a poorly performing RO or company and a record of deficiencies and detentions. The precise weightings are set out in Table 1 of Annex 7 of the Memorandum and a candidate is not expected to reproduce them.

3. Company performance - the new parameter

This was the genuinely novel element of the NIR. The Paris MoU applies a formula taking into account the deficiencies and detentions of the whole of a company's fleet over the last 36 months, identified through the IMO company number, and compares it against the average of all vessels inspected in the Paris MoU region. The company is then ranked very low, low, medium or high. Any refusal of access imposed on one of its ships has a negative impact on the ranking.

The policy intent is to reach past the individual ship to the quality of management, and to remove the advantage previously enjoyed by an operator who kept one showpiece vessel in good order while running the rest of the fleet down.

4. The selection scheme - windows and priorities

The profile determines the periodic inspection window measured from the last inspection in the Paris MoU region:

ProfileWindowEffect
High Risk Ship5 to 6 monthsMay be inspected from month 5; must be inspected after month 6
Standard Risk Ship10 to 12 monthsMay be inspected from month 10; must be inspected after month 12
Low Risk Ship24 to 36 monthsMay be inspected from month 24; must be inspected after month 36

The window restarts after any inspection. Priorities follow directly from it:

  • Priority II - the window has opened. The ship may be selected for a periodic inspection.
  • Priority I - the window has passed. The ship will be selected and must be inspected.
  • before the window opens, and absent any factor below, the ship has no priority status and member States are not obliged to inspect it - though they may.

Two categories of event override the timetable entirely:

  • an overriding factor makes the ship Priority I irrespective of the window - for example a collision, grounding or stranding, or a discharge of harmful substances, a suspension or withdrawal of class for safety reasons, or a report that the ship is operating dangerously;
  • an unexpected factor makes the ship Priority II irrespective of the window - for example failure to comply with the ETA reporting obligations, outstanding deficiencies, or reported cargo problems, particularly with noxious or dangerous cargo.

5. Types of inspection

The NIR retained the three inspection types but changed who receives which:

  • Initial inspection - verification of certificates and documents, the overall condition of the ship, and compliance with on-board conditions;
  • More detailed inspection - conducted where clear grounds are established that the condition of the ship, equipment or crew does not substantially meet the requirements;
  • Expanded inspection - a defined-scope examination of risk areas.

On a periodic inspection, an expanded inspection is mandatory for every High Risk Ship, and for every bulk carrier, chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker or passenger ship older than 12 years, whatever its profile. A Standard or Low Risk Ship that is not one of those types receives an initial inspection, escalating to a more detailed inspection if clear grounds appear. Any additional inspection - one triggered by an overriding or unexpected factor - is at least a more detailed inspection, and may be an expanded inspection at the PSCO's professional judgement where the ship is high risk or one of the listed types.

6. Reporting obligations and THETIS

The NIR could not work without knowing where ships are, so it introduced a reporting chain:

  • ETA72 - 72 hours before arrival, from ships eligible for an expanded inspection: all High Risk Ships and all bulk carriers, chemical tankers, gas carriers, oil tankers and passenger ships over 12 years old. The notification must include ship identification, planned duration of call, planned operations, planned surveys and substantial repair work, the date of the last expanded inspection in the region, and for tankers the hull configuration (single hull, single hull with segregated ballast tanks, or double hull), the condition of cargo and ballast tanks (full, empty or inerted) and the volume and type of cargo;
  • ETA24 - 24 hours before arrival, from all ships, at any time and regardless of the inspection window, or on leaving the previous port if the voyage is shorter than 24 hours;
  • ATA and ATD - the actual times of arrival and departure, a new obligation covering all ships calling at all ports and anchorages in the region.

The information is transferred by the relevant authority into THETIS, the NIR information database, fed through SafeSeaNet. THETIS is what makes a shared regional commitment possible: it calculates the profile, holds the inspection history and tells any member State which ships in its ports are due. Failure to report is itself an unexpected factor, and so exposes the ship to inspection regardless of its window - which is the enforcement mechanism for the reporting duty.

7. Refusal of access - banning

The NIR extended banning from certain ship types to all ship types and from Black List flags to Grey List flags as well:

  • a ship flying a Black-listed flag is banned after the third detention in the last 36 months;
  • a ship flying a Grey-listed flag is banned after the third detention in the last 24 months;
  • after a second banning, any subsequent detention leads to a ban regardless of the flag's listing;
  • a ship that jumps detention or fails to sail to an agreed repair yard is banned.

The bans escalate and cannot be lifted before: 3 months after the first; 12 months after the second; 24 months after the third, with more stringent conditions to be met before the 24 months elapse; and the fourth is a permanent ban.

The flag lists themselves are published annually from a three-year rolling period of inspections and detentions, for flags with at least 30 inspections in the period. The lists operative at this examination are the 2023 performance lists, applied from 1 July 2024.

8. Pros of the NIR

  1. Risk-proportionate effort. Inspection resource is directed at the ships most likely to be substandard instead of being spread evenly, which is a better use of a finite number of PSCOs.
  2. A real reward for quality. A Low Risk Ship faces inspection every 24 to 36 months rather than annually - fewer interruptions, less off-hire risk, lower cost, and a commercial and chartering advantage.
  3. No escape by routing. The shared regional commitment to inspect all ships removes the old possibility of avoiding attention by calling at States that had met their 25% quota.
  4. It reaches the company, not just the ship. Company performance makes fleet-wide management quality consequential, which is where substandard operation actually originates.
  5. Transparency and predictability. The profile can be calculated by the operator in advance through the Paris MoU ship risk calculator, so a company can see the effect of a deficiency before the next port.
  6. Escalating, published banning. Persistent offenders are progressively excluded from the region entirely, and the reputational effect on flags and ROs drives improvement upstream.
  7. Harmonisation. A common database, common criteria and common procedures across the member States reduce inconsistency between ports.

9. Cons of the NIR

  1. Collective punishment through company performance. A well-run ship's profile can deteriorate because of detentions on sister vessels it has no connection with, and a newly acquired ship inherits the company's record.
  2. Parameters largely outside the operator's control. Flag and RO performance are set by the conduct of other owners entirely; a diligent operator on a Grey List flag is penalised for the flag's aggregate record.
  3. Historic lag. The profile runs on a 36-month record, so genuine improvement takes years to show, while a single bad inspection registers immediately - the system is quicker to punish than to reward.
  4. Age as a crude proxy. A well-maintained 13-year-old tanker is treated as higher risk than a neglected 11-year-old one.
  5. Increased administrative burden from the ETA72, ETA24, ATA and ATD reporting obligations, falling largely on the master and the agent.
  6. Frequency and duration for high-risk ships. A 5 to 6 month cycle of expanded inspections is a heavy operational and commercial burden, and expanded inspections are long.
  7. Residual inconsistency. Deficiency coding and the exercise of professional judgement still vary between PSCOs and between States, so the same condition may be recorded differently in different ports.
  8. Severe consequences of banning, which can effectively end a ship's commercial life in the region, with limited and slow appeal - an appeal does not suspend the detention.
  9. Perverse incentives. The regime can encourage cosmetic preparation for inspection and the avoidance of honest self-reporting, rather than genuine improvement.

10. How owners and managers should respond

The correct answer to this limb is not "comply with the regulations". It is a management programme aimed at the profile itself, and it has five parts.

1. Manage the profile as a measured asset. Calculate the Ship Risk Profile for every vessel using the Paris MoU calculator, know each ship's category and the date its window opens, and treat movement toward Low Risk as a board-level KPI alongside off-hire and fuel. Model the effect of a prospective detention before it happens.

2. Attack the controllable parameters, and choose the uncontrollable ones. Deficiencies and detentions are within the operator's control and are where effort pays. Flag and RO performance are not controllable day to day - but they are selectable. Flag choice and class choice become risk decisions with a measurable inspection consequence, and a White List flag whose administration has completed the IMO audit, with a high-performing RO, is worth paying for.

3. Protect company performance as a fleet-wide asset. Because one ship's detention degrades every sister ship's profile, the company must run fleet-wide corrective action: analyse every deficiency across the fleet, apply the lesson to all vessels rather than the one inspected, and use the ISM Code's own machinery - root cause analysis, corrective and preventive action, internal audit and management review - to close the loop. Due diligence on acquisition and on takeover of management must include the incoming ship's and company's PSC record.

4. Prepare the ship and the crew for the inspection they will actually get. Self-inspection regimes and mock PSC inspections built on the published deficiency and detention statistics; particular focus on the recurring detainable areas - ISM implementation, fire safety, life-saving appliances, MLC working and living conditions, and pollution prevention; and readiness for the expanded inspection scope if the ship is high risk or one of the five types over 12 years old. Familiarisation so that officers can demonstrate systems in operation rather than merely produce certificates. Concentrated Inspection Campaign topics are announced in advance and should be prepared for specifically.

5. Handle the process professionally when it happens. Rigorous compliance with the ETA72, ETA24, ATA and ATD obligations, since failure is itself an unexpected factor that invites inspection. A senior officer to accompany the PSCO throughout. Deficiencies rectified before departure wherever possible, and where not, agreement of a proper rectification plan - never a detention jumped and never a failure to proceed to an agreed repair yard, both of which trigger an immediate ban. Where a finding is genuinely unjustified, the company may appeal to the national PSC authority or through the flag State Administration, but the appeal does not lift the detention, so the operational answer is always to fix first and argue afterwards.

The strategic point to close on: under the NIR, inspection frequency has become a commercial variable that management can move. A company that gets its fleet into the Low Risk band buys itself fewer inspections, less disruption, a better chartering position and lower cost - so the response to the NIR is not defensive compliance but the recognition that quality management now has a directly measurable financial return.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight marks each, and they test different skills. Limb (a) is recall of a system: profile, categories, windows, priorities, inspection types, reporting, banning. Limb (b) is evaluation and application - pros, cons, and a management response - and it is the limb that separates candidates, because most will write four thin advantages and stop.

The examiner has printed three tasks in limb (b): pros, cons, and how would owners/managers respond. That third task carries real weight and needs a structured programme, not a sentence about maintaining the ship properly. The route gives it five headings so it cannot be skimped.

Note the printed stem is defective - it reads "Explain the salient features NIR", with a word apparently missing. It plainly means the salient features of the NIR, and the answer proceeds on that basis.

The one idea that unlocks limb (a)

Everything in the NIR flows from a single change: targeting moved from the ship's accumulated points to a continuously recalculated risk profile, and the profile decides both HOW OFTEN and WHAT KIND. Once that is stated, the windows, the priorities and the inspection types all follow as consequences rather than as separate facts to be memorised.

Add the second structural change - the shift from 25% of ships per State to a shared commitment to inspect all ships in the region - and you have explained why THETIS had to exist and why the ATA/ATD reporting obligations were introduced. A system that promises to inspect every ship must know where every ship is.

Company performance is the answer to 'what was NEW?'

If asked what was genuinely novel about the NIR, the answer is company performance. Risk profiles and inspection types existed in some form before; ranking the ISM company across its whole fleet by the IMO company number, over 36 months, against the regional average, did not.

It is also the hinge between the two limbs. In limb (a) it is a parameter. In limb (b) it is simultaneously the strongest pro - it reaches the management quality where substandard operation originates - and the sharpest con - a good ship is penalised for its sister vessels' record. A candidate who uses the same fact on both sides of the argument is doing exactly what "pros and cons" asks for.

The numbers to carry

  • 1 January 2011 - the NIR enters into force.
  • 5-6 / 10-12 / 24-36 months - the windows for High, Standard and Low Risk. If you remember nothing else numerical, remember these.
  • 36 months - the inspection history period, and the company performance period.
  • 12 years - the age threshold, both for risk and for the five ship types that always get an expanded inspection.
  • 72 / 24 hours - ETA72 for expanded-inspection ships, ETA24 for all ships.
  • 3 detentions in 36 months (Black) or 3 in 24 months (Grey) - the first ban.
  • 3, 12, 24 months, then permanent - the ban durations.
  • 30 inspections in the three-year period - the threshold for a flag to be listed at all.
  • 25% - the OLD individual commitment the NIR replaced.

What was true in December 2024 specifically

The NIR is structurally unchanged since 2011, so this question is not a currency minefield. But three things are sitting-relative and are worth getting right:

  • the White, Grey and Black Lists in force are the 2023 performance lists, approved by the Committee at its 57th meeting and applied from 1 July 2024. The lists change every July, so "the current list" always means the one adopted the previous summer;
  • the Russian Federation is suspended from the Paris MoU;
  • revisions to the Ship Risk Profile aligned with the recast EU Port State Control Directive - including revised methodologies for flag State and RO performance and the incorporation of environmental criteria into the profile - were in preparation and not operative. That Directive applies from 2027. Do not write that environmental parameters form part of the Ship Risk Profile; at this sitting they do not.

That last point is the future-instrument trap on this question, and it is exactly the kind of thing a candidate revising from current material in a later year would get wrong.

Common mistakes

  • Confusing the Ship Risk Profile with the old Target Factor. The NIR replaced one with the other.
  • Getting the windows wrong, or giving a single figure instead of a range. They are ranges: 5-6, 10-12, 24-36 months.
  • Reversing Priority I and Priority II. Priority I means the window has PASSED and the ship MUST be inspected; Priority II means it has OPENED and the ship MAY be.
  • Confusing overriding and unexpected factors. Overriding gives Priority I; unexpected gives Priority II.
  • Saying only High Risk Ships get expanded inspections. So do all bulk carriers, chemical tankers, gas carriers, oil tankers and passenger ships over 12 years old, whatever their profile.
  • Omitting company performance - the genuinely new parameter.
  • Omitting the banning regime, which is a third of limb (a)'s substance.
  • Answering limb (b) with pros and cons only, and never addressing how owners and managers should respond.
  • Treating flag and RO performance as things the operator cannot influence at all. They cannot be controlled, but they can be chosen.
  • Saying environmental criteria are part of the Ship Risk Profile. Not at this sitting.
  • Confusing the Paris MoU with the Tokyo MoU, which has its own regime and its own lists.

Examiner traps

  • "Your ship was inspected four months ago in Rotterdam and is High Risk. Can it be inspected in Antwerp next week?" It has no priority status until month 5, so there is no obligation - but a member State may still inspect if it thinks fit, and an overriding or unexpected factor would make it Priority I or II immediately.
  • "A sister ship was detained. Does that affect your ship?" Yes - through company performance, and the profile is recalculated daily, so the effect can be immediate.
  • "You failed to send the ETA24. What follows?" Failure to comply with reporting obligations is an unexpected factor, making the ship Priority II irrespective of the window, and national penalties may also apply.
  • "What happens if you sail with an unrectified detention?" Jumping detention leads to a ban, as does failing to sail to an agreed repair yard.
  • "How long before a first ban can be lifted?" Three months - then 12, then 24 with more stringent conditions, then permanent.
  • "Does an appeal suspend a detention?" No. The company may appeal to the national authority or through the flag State, but the ship stays detained meanwhile.

Likely oral follow-up

  • When did the NIR come into force, and what did it replace? 1 January 2011; the Target Factor, and the 25% individual inspection commitment.
  • Name the Ship Risk Profile parameters. Type, age, flag performance and IMO audit, RO performance, company performance, deficiencies and detentions over 36 months.
  • What are the three categories and their windows? High 5-6 months, Standard 10-12, Low 24-36.
  • Distinguish Priority I from Priority II. Window passed, must inspect; window open, may inspect.
  • Give an overriding factor and an unexpected factor. Collision or a discharge of harmful substances; failure to report ETA, or outstanding deficiencies.
  • Which ships must have an expanded inspection? Every High Risk Ship, and every bulk carrier, chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker or passenger ship over 12 years old.
  • What reporting is required? ETA72 for expanded-inspection ships, ETA24 for all ships, plus ATA and ATD.
  • What is THETIS? The NIR information database, fed via SafeSeaNet, which holds the profiles and inspection history.
  • When is a ship banned? Third detention in 36 months on a black-listed flag, or in 24 months on a grey-listed flag; and after the second ban any further detention.
  • How is company performance calculated? From the fleet's deficiencies and detentions over 36 months by IMO company number, against the regional average, ranked very low to high.

Memory framework

For limb (a), five beats: PROFILE - WINDOW - PRIORITY - INSPECTION - BAN. Who you are, when you are due, whether you must be done, what kind you get, and what happens if you keep failing. Reporting and THETIS sit underneath as the plumbing that makes it work.

For the profile parameters: TYPE, AGE, FLAG, RO, COMPANY, DEFICIENCIES, DETENTIONS - the first two are what the ship is, the next three are who stands behind it, the last two are what it has done.

For limb (b)'s response programme: MEASURE it, CHOOSE what you can, PROTECT the fleet, PREPARE the ship, HANDLE the day.

Regulation and source map

  • Paris MoU Memorandum, Annex 7, Table 1 - the Ship Risk Profile criteria and weightings
  • Paris MoU Selection Scheme - windows, Priority I and II, overriding and unexpected factors
  • Paris MoU White, Grey and Black List methodology - three-year rolling period, minimum 30 inspections; the 2023 lists applied from 1 July 2024 are operative at this sitting
  • THETIS, fed through SafeSeaNet - the NIR information database
  • The EU port State control Directive, implemented in parallel with the NIR in EU and EFTA member States
  • Resolution A.1185(33), Procedures for Port State Control 2023 - the IMO-level procedures operative at this sitting, which sit behind all regional MoU regimes
  • The III Code, A.1070(28) paragraphs 52 to 63 - the port State obligations, including no more favourable treatment and PSCO independence

Uncertainty and currency

Three limitations are recorded and they are worth understanding rather than skipping. No numerical weightings from Table 1 of Annex 7 are asserted anywhere in this answer. The table could not be retrieved from the Paris MoU's own published source by automated means, and a fabricated or half-remembered weighting would be worse than none - so the answer names every parameter and describes each one's qualitative effect, which is what a candidate can realistically deploy in any event. No per-flag listing or detention statistic is asserted, because the lists move every July. And the membership is stated as the organisation states it - 27 maritime authorities with the Russian Federation suspended - without asserting a count of active members, since suspension does not remove membership.

On currency, one instruction for a later editor: the revised Ship Risk Profile aligned to the recast EU Port State Control Directive, including environmental criteria, is a FUTURE instrument at this sitting and must not be presented as operative. That Directive applies from 2027. Anyone updating this object from current Paris MoU material would find the revised profile described as in force and would introduce future contamination into a December 2024 paper.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 10 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  9. __________b) Cons of the NIR
  10. __________b) How owners and managers should respond

The second change is what forced THETIS and the arrival-reporting obligations into existence.
