It was the genuinely new element of the NIR, and it is both the strongest pro and the sharpest con in limb (b).

ETA72 - 72 hours before arrival from ships eligible for an expanded inspection, including for tankers the hull configuration, cargo and ballast tank condition, and volume and type of cargo. ETA24 - 24 hours before arrival from all ships, at any time regardless of the window. ATA and ATD - actual times of arrival and departure. Data goes to THETIS via SafeSeaNet. Failure to report is itself an unexpected factor, rendering the ship liable to inspection irrespective of the period since the last one.

The enforcement of the reporting duty is the reporting duty's own breach becoming a targeting trigger.

A ship on a black-listed flag is banned after the third detention in the last 36 months; on a grey-listed flag, after the third detention in the last 24 months. After a second banning, any subsequent detention leads to a ban regardless of flag listing. Jumping detention or failing to sail to an agreed repair yard also leads to a ban. Bans cannot be lifted until 3 months after the first, 12 months after the second, 24 months after the third with more stringent conditions, and the fourth is permanent.

Two flags, two periods, four escalating durations - all exact and all examinable.

The 2023 performance lists, approved at the 57th Committee meeting and applied from 1 July 2024. Do NOT say that environmental criteria form part of the Ship Risk Profile - the revised profile aligned with the recast EU Port State Control Directive was still in preparation, and that Directive applies from 2027.

The lists change every July, and the environmental parameters are the future-instrument trap on this question.

MEASURE - calculate every ship's profile, know the window dates, treat movement toward Low Risk as a board-level KPI. CHOOSE - deficiencies and detentions are controllable, flag and RO are selectable, so flag and class choice become risk decisions. PROTECT THE FLEET - one detention degrades every sister ship, so corrective action must be fleet-wide, with PSC due diligence on acquisition. PREPARE - self-inspection and mock PSC built on published deficiency statistics, readiness for expanded scope, officers able to demonstrate systems. HANDLE THE DAY - rigorous reporting, escorted inspection, rectify before departure, never jump detention; appeal does not lift the detention.

This is the third printed task in limb (b) and the one that separates candidates.

As a pro: it reaches past the individual ship to the quality of fleet management, which is where substandard operation actually originates, and removes the advantage of keeping one showpiece vessel in good order. As a con: it is collective punishment - a well-run ship's profile deteriorates because of detentions on sister vessels it has no connection with, and a newly acquired ship inherits the company's record.

Deploying one fact on both sides is exactly what a 'pros and cons' limb is asking for.

Quick revision
15-second recall
NIR = Paris MoU New Inspection Regime, in force 1 January 2011, alongside the EU PSC Directive. Replaced the Target Factor with the Ship Risk Profile, and replaced each State inspecting 25% of callers with a shared regional commitment to inspect ALL ships. Region: European coastal States and the North Atlantic basin; at this sitting 27 maritime authorities, Russian Federation SUSPENDED. SHIP RISK PROFILE - HRS / SRS / LRS (Standard is the residual). Parameters, over 36 months in the region: type (chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker, bulk carrier, passenger ship are higher risk), age (over 12 years), flag (White/Grey/Black List and IMO audit), RO performance, company performance, number of deficiencies, number of detentions. Recalculated DAILY - a sister ship's detention can move your profile at once. Weightings are in Annex 7 Table 1. COMPANY PERFORMANCE was the new element: fleet deficiencies and detentions over 36 months by IMO company number, against the regional average, ranked very low / low / medium / high; a ban on any ship damages it. WINDOWS from last inspection in the region: HRS 5-6 months, SRS 10-12, LRS 24-36; restarts after any inspection. Priority II = window OPENED, MAY be inspected. Priority I = window PASSED, MUST be inspected. Overriding factor (collision, discharge of harmful substances) → Priority I irrespective of window. Unexpected factor (failure to report ETA, outstanding deficiencies, cargo problems) → Priority II irrespective of window. INSPECTION TYPES: initial; more detailed on clear grounds; expanded. Expanded is mandatory for every HRS and for every bulk carrier, chemical tanker, gas carrier, oil tanker or passenger ship over 12 years old. Any additional inspection is at least more detailed. REPORTING: ETA72 (expanded-inspection ships; includes tanker hull configuration, tank condition, cargo volume and type, date of last expanded inspection), ETA24 (ALL ships, always), ATA and ATD. Data goes to THETIS via SafeSeaNet. Failure to report is itself an unexpected factor. BANNING: Black flag - 3rd detention in 36 months; Grey flag - 3rd detention in 24 months; after a 2nd ban any detention bans regardless of flag; jumping detention or missing the agreed repair yard bans. Lifting: 3, 12, 24 months, then PERMANENT. Lists from a three-year rolling period, minimum 30 inspections; the 2023 lists applied from 1 July 2024 are operative here. PROS: risk-proportionate resource; real reward for quality; no escape by routing; reaches the company; transparent and predictable; escalating banning; harmonisation. CONS: collective punishment via company performance; flag and RO outside daily control; 36-month lag punishes faster than it rewards; age a crude proxy; reporting burden; heavy 5-6 month cycle for HRS; residual PSCO inconsistency; severe banning with appeal that does not suspend detention; cosmetic-compliance incentives. RESPONSE: MEASURE the profile as a KPI; CHOOSE flag and RO deliberately; PROTECT the fleet with fleet-wide CAPA and PSC due diligence on acquisition; PREPARE with self-inspection, mock PSC and CIC readiness; HANDLE the day with rigorous reporting, escorted inspection, rectification before departure, and never jumping detention.

Critical numbers
  • NIR in force 1 January 2011
  • High Risk Ship - inspected between 5 and 6 months after the last inspection in the region
  • Standard Risk Ship - between 10 and 12 months
  • Low Risk Ship - between 24 and 36 months
  • 36 months - the inspection history period and the company performance period
  • 12 years - the age threshold, and the age above which five ship types always get an expanded inspection
  • ETA72 for expanded-inspection ships; ETA24 for all ships
  • Black-listed flag - ban after the third detention in 36 months
  • Grey-listed flag - ban after the third detention in 24 months
  • Ban lifting - 3 months, 12 months, 24 months, then permanent
  • 30 inspections in the three-year period - threshold for a flag to be listed
  • 25 per cent - the individual inspection commitment the NIR replaced
Critical regulation
The Ship Risk Profile, set out in Table 1 of Annex 7 of the Paris MoU Memorandum, classifies every ship as High, Standard or Low Risk from the type and age of the ship, the performance of its flag, recognized organization and ISM company, and the number of deficiencies and detentions recorded in the region over the preceding 36 months; the resulting profile determines both the interval between inspections and the type of inspection the ship receives.
Major trap
Answering limb (b) with pros and cons only and never addressing the third printed task - how owners and managers should respond. That response must be a management programme aimed at the profile itself: measure it, choose flag and RO deliberately, protect the fleet through fleet-wide corrective action because company performance is shared, prepare for the inspection the ship will actually get, and handle the inspection day professionally. In limb (a) the recurring traps are reversing Priority I and Priority II, and saying only High Risk Ships receive expanded inspections when all bulk carriers, chemical tankers, gas carriers, oil tankers and passenger ships over 12 years old do as well. One currency trap: environmental criteria in the Ship Risk Profile belong to the recast EU Directive applying from 2027 and are NOT part of the regime at this sitting.

Also on the platform: QP2412 Q9 - the III Code, where port State control obligations and PSCO independence are set out · QP2412 Q4 - classification societies and recognized organizations, whose performance is a Ship Risk Profile parameter · QP2412 Q6 - MLC 2006, one of the instruments enforced through port State control inspection

Understand this first

The odd thing about SOLAS is what it does not contain. It has a great deal to say about stability, fire and life-saving, and nothing at all about how thick a plate has to be. Hull strength was always the classification societies' private business with the owner and the underwriter. Regulation II-1/3-1 is SOLAS pointing at that private rule book and saying: that one, and you must keep to it for the ship's whole life - which is how losing class stopped being a commercial embarrassment and became a breach of the Convention. Limb (b) is a different kind of question: two survey names that sound interchangeable and are not. The periodical survey belongs to only two certificates and runs at two different frequencies, and knowing that is most of the six marks.

The SOLAS reason is one sentence: SOLAS never had scantlings, so it borrowed class's (5). Then alignment, not survey names (6). Limb (b) closes on annual is a LOOK, periodical is a TEST - and periodical belongs to only two certificates (7).

Model written answer

1. What a classification society is, and its two distinct roles

A classification society is a technical body that publishes its own rules for the design, construction and survey of ships, assigns class to a ship that meets them, and maintains that class by periodical survey. Its origin is commercial: underwriters needed an independent judgement of the ship they were asked to insure. Everything else follows from that.

The distinction to make at the outset is that a society wears two hats:

  • Classification - a private, contractual relationship with the owner, under the society's own rules. Class is not a government function, and the society does not act for the flag State when it assigns it.
  • Statutory work as a Recognized Organization - work delegated by the flag State under SOLAS regulation XI-1/1, in which the society surveys and certifies against the conventions on the Administration's behalf, subject to the Code for Recognized Organizations, resolutions MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory since 1 January 2015. Here the society acts for the flag State - which retains responsibility.

2. How class rules are made

Rules are not written from theory alone. They are built from service experience - damage and defect returns from the surveyed fleet; from casualty investigation; from research and direct calculation, including finite element analysis and fatigue assessment; and from technical committees drawing on owners, builders, makers and the society's own surveyors.

Because a ship trades worldwide and may change flag and society, divergence between societies is itself a hazard, so the members harmonise through IACS: Unified Requirements setting minimum standards each member must implement in its own rules, Unified Interpretations of convention text, Procedural Requirements, and jointly developed rules - most importantly the Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers.

3. What the structural, mechanical and electrical rules cover

  • Structural - scantlings of plating, stiffeners and primary members; longitudinal and local strength; fatigue life; corrosion additions and coating requirements; loading conditions and hull girder limits; and the enhanced close-up survey requirements for bulk carriers and tankers.
  • Mechanical - main propulsion and essential auxiliary machinery; shafting, propellers and gearing; boilers and pressure vessels; piping systems; steering gear; and the type approval of components and materials.
  • Electrical - generation, distribution and protection, including discrimination and short-circuit capability; essential and emergency services and their sources; cable installation; high-voltage systems; and equipment for hazardous areas.

4. Implementation through the ship's life

  • Design - plan approval of structure, machinery and electrical installations against the rules, review of direct calculations, and type approval of equipment so that approved components need not be re-justified on each ship.
  • Construction - approval of materials and their manufacturers; certification of components; qualification of welding procedures and welders; witnessing of non-destructive testing and tank testing; survey during construction at the yard; and attendance at trials before the certificate is issued.
  • Maintenance in service - the periodical survey cycle that keeps class alive: annual, intermediate and renewal or special surveys, docking and in-water surveys, tailshaft and boiler surveys. Alternatives exist where they can be justified - continuous survey of hull or machinery spreading the items across the cycle, and an approved planned maintenance scheme allowing certain machinery items to be credited on the Chief Engineer's records. Where a defect is found the society may impose a condition of class or recommendation with a date for rectification, and class may be suspended or withdrawn if it is not met.

5. Why compliance with class rules is now mentioned in SOLAS chapter II-1

The reference is SOLAS regulation II-1/3-1, "Structural, mechanical and electrical requirements for ships". It provides that ships shall be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with the structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society which is recognized by the Administration in accordance with the provisions of regulation XI-1/1, or with applicable national standards of the Administration which provide an equivalent level of safety.

The reason it is there is the gap it closes. SOLAS regulates subdivision and damage stability, machinery and electrical installations, fire protection and life-saving. But SOLAS never contained scantling rules - it does not state how thick a plate must be or what section modulus a hull girder must have. That had always been left to the classification societies as a matter of private contract between owner, society and underwriter. The consequence was that, as a matter of convention law, there was no requirement that a ship be built to any structural standard at all: class was commercially indispensable and legally optional.

Regulation II-1/3-1 converts that commercial arrangement into a statutory obligation, and four things follow:

  1. compliance with a recognized society's rules becomes a SOLAS requirement, enforceable by the flag State and inspectable by port State control;
  2. the obligation runs to maintenance, not only to design and construction - so loss or suspension of class becomes a SOLAS non-compliance, not merely a commercial and insurance problem;
  3. the society whose rules are relied on must be recognized under XI-1/1 and must operate under the RO Code, so the standard is not simply whatever any body chooses to publish; and
  4. an Administration may still use its own national standards, but only where they provide an equivalent level of safety - preserving flag State sovereignty without opening a loophole.

This also completes the picture begun by goal-based standards: for bulk carriers and oil tankers the class rules relied on under II-1/3-1 must themselves have been verified at IMO as conforming to the goals and functional requirements under SOLAS II-1/3-10. SOLAS therefore requires compliance with class rules, and separately requires those rules to have been shown to be good enough.

(The printed question refers to "SOLAS ch.ll-1", using two lower-case letter L's. The reference intended is chapter II-1, and within it regulation 3-1.)

6. The harmonized system, and where these surveys sit

Before harmonization each certificate carried its own survey dates, so a ship could be under survey almost continuously and a certificate could still expire while the ship was at sea. The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, introduced by the 1988 SOLAS and Load Lines Protocols and extended to MARPOL by MEPC.39(29) and to Annex VI by MEPC.132(53), aligns the certificates on a common five-year cycle with common anniversary dates and a common window of three months either side, so that surveys can be taken together in one attendance.

The guidance operative at this sitting is the Survey Guidelines under the HSSC, 2023, resolution A.1186(33), adopted 6 December 2023 by the 33rd Assembly, which revokes A.1156(32). The system uses initial, annual, intermediate, periodical, renewal and additional surveys, plus inspection of the outside of the ship's bottom.

7. Annual versus periodical - the difference and the relevance

Annual surveyPeriodical survey
Applies toCargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate, Cargo Ship Safety Construction Certificate and the International Load Line CertificateOnly the Cargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate and the Cargo Ship Safety Radio Certificate
WhenWithin three months before or after each anniversary dateSafety Equipment: within three months before or after the second or third anniversary date, taking the place of one of the annual surveys. Safety Radio: within three months before or after each anniversary date
ScopeA certificate examination, a visual examination of a sufficient extent of the ship and its equipment, and certain tests to confirm the condition is being properly maintained - including that no unapproved modifications have been madeAn inspection with tests when necessary of the equipment, to confirm it complies, is in satisfactory condition and is fit for the service intended, plus a check that certificates, record books, manuals and documentation are on board
CharacterA general confirmation that maintenance is being kept upA more searching examination of the equipment itself

The relevance is that a five-year certificate would otherwise be an unverified assertion. The renewal survey establishes the ship's condition at the start of the cycle; the annual and periodical surveys are the checkpoints that keep that finding credible through it, and their common window is what allows them to be combined into a single attendance rather than surveying the ship piecemeal. The periodical survey exists because life-saving, fire-fighting and radio equipment deteriorates and is consumed in a way structure is not - so equipment gets a searching mid-cycle test, and the radio installation, on which distress communication depends absolutely, gets one every year.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The printed split is 10 + 6, and it must be respected. Limb (a) carries three tasks in ten marks - rule formation, implementation across the lifecycle, and the SOLAS reason - and the third is where the discrimination lies, so it deserves real space rather than a closing sentence. Limb (b) is six marks for a comparison: a table earns them faster than prose, and there is no credit for padding it out.

The heart of limb (a): what gap does II-1/3-1 close?

Most candidates will state that SOLAS II-1/3-1 requires compliance with class rules. Very few will say why that regulation had to exist, and that is the whole question. SOLAS has never contained scantling rules - it does not say how thick a plate must be. Structural standards were always a matter of private contract between owner, society and underwriter. So before II-1/3-1 a ship could in principle satisfy SOLAS while being built to no structural standard whatever, because class was commercially indispensable but legally optional. The regulation makes it legally compulsory, and extends the duty to maintenance - which is what turns loss of class from a commercial problem into a convention breach that port State control can act on.

The two-hats distinction is the other half of limb (a)

Classification is private and contractual; statutory certification as a Recognized Organization is delegated by the flag State under SOLAS XI-1/1 and governed by the RO Code. The same surveyor may do both on the same day, which is exactly why the distinction is worth stating. Note the resolution numbers carefully: the RO Code is MSC.349(92) with MEPC.237(65), mandatory from 1 January 2015. Getting the session number wrong here is an easy and visible slip.

Limb (b) has one fact almost nobody knows

The periodical survey is not simply "the survey that happens periodically". It attaches to only two certificates - Safety Equipment and Safety Radio - and it has a different frequency for each. For the Safety Equipment Certificate it falls at the second or third anniversary and replaces one of the annual surveys. For the Safety Radio Certificate it falls at every anniversary. A candidate who states both frequencies, and notes that the radio survey is annual because distress communication cannot be allowed to degrade unnoticed, has said something precise where most answers say something vague.

Why harmonization was needed at all

The point of HSSC is not the survey types; it is the alignment. Before it, certificates carried independent dates, so a ship might be under survey almost continuously and could still find a certificate expiring at sea. HSSC puts everything on a common five-year cycle with common anniversary dates and a common three-months-either-side window, so surveys can be combined into one attendance. If limb (b) explains the alignment rather than merely listing survey names, it has answered "relevance" as well as "difference".

Which edition of the Survey Guidelines - and why the answer here is NOT the one you will find today

The Survey Guidelines under the HSSC are re-adopted by the IMO Assembly roughly every two years, and each edition revokes the last. At the date of this examination the operative edition was resolution A.1186(33), the 2023 Guidelines, adopted 6 December 2023 by the 33rd Assembly and revoking A.1156(32).

Be careful with anything you read about this now. The 34th Assembly adopted A.1207(34), the 2025 Guidelines, on 3 December 2025 - a full two years after this paper was sat. Current material will tell you that A.1186(33) has been revoked and that citing it is a mistake. For this sitting that is exactly backwards. The substance is unaffected either way: the annual and periodical survey definitions, frequencies and certificate attachments are the same in both editions. Only the resolution number and date differ - which is what makes this the most dangerous kind of error, because an answer citing the wrong edition reads as entirely correct in every other respect.

The general rule this illustrates is worth more than the particular fact: an Assembly boundary is the adoption date, not the meeting month, and any instrument re-adopted on a cycle has to be pinned to your own sitting rather than to whatever is current when you revise.

Common mistakes

  • Treating classification and statutory certification as the same activity. One is private contract; the other is delegated flag State authority.
  • Saying SOLAS contains the structural rules. It does not - that is precisely why II-1/3-1 refers out to class rules.
  • Citing the RO Code as MSC.349(90). It is MSC.349(92), with MEPC.237(65).
  • Saying periodical surveys apply to all certificates. They apply only to the Cargo Ship Safety Equipment and Cargo Ship Safety Radio Certificates.
  • Giving one frequency for the periodical survey. There are two, depending on the certificate.
  • Confusing the periodical survey with the intermediate survey. Both may fall at the second or third anniversary, but the intermediate attaches to different certificates and has a different scope.
  • Citing A.1207(34) as the Survey Guidelines for this sitting. That resolution was adopted on 3 December 2025, two years after this examination. The edition in force here is A.1186(33).
  • Forgetting that II-1/3-1 permits national standards of equivalent safety as an alternative to class rules.
  • Omitting the word maintained from II-1/3-1. It is the word that does the work.

Examiner traps

"Your ship loses class mid-voyage. Is that a SOLAS matter?" Yes. II-1/3-1 requires the ship to be maintained in compliance with the rules of a recognized society, so loss of class is a convention non-compliance and not merely a commercial and insurance issue.

"Must a ship be classed?" Strictly, it must comply with the requirements of a recognized society or with national standards of the Administration providing an equivalent level of safety. In practice class is universal, but the regulation offers the alternative and saying so shows the text has been read.

"When is your periodical survey due?" Ask which certificate. Safety Equipment: second or third anniversary, in place of an annual. Safety Radio: every anniversary.

"Who is responsible if the recognized organization gets it wrong?" The Administration. Recognition delegates the work, not the responsibility - which is the point of the RO Code.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which SOLAS regulation requires compliance with class rules, and where does the recognition come from? II-1/3-1, with recognition under XI-1/1.
  • What is the RO Code and when did it become mandatory? MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory from 1 January 2015.
  • Name three IACS instrument types. Unified Requirements, Unified Interpretations, Procedural Requirements.
  • What is the harmonized survey window? Three months before or after the anniversary date.
  • Which certificates carry a periodical survey? Cargo Ship Safety Equipment and Cargo Ship Safety Radio.
  • Which resolution carried the HSSC Survey Guidelines at this sitting? A.1186(33), adopted 6 December 2023, revoking A.1156(32).
  • What is a condition of class? A defect the society requires to be rectified by a stated date, failing which class may be suspended or withdrawn.
  • What alternatives to open survey exist for machinery? Continuous survey of machinery, and an approved planned maintenance scheme.
  • Which ships are covered by goal-based standards under II-1/3-10? Bulk carriers and oil tankers.

Memory framework

For limb (a), three lifecycle verbs: approve, witness, survey - plan approval at design, witnessing at construction, periodical survey in service (step 4). For the SOLAS reason, one sentence: SOLAS never had scantlings, so it borrowed class's (step 5). For limb (b), annual is a look, periodical is a test - and periodical belongs to only two certificates (step 7).

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS regulation II-1/3-1 - designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with the rules of a recognized classification society, or national standards of equivalent safety
  • SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 - recognition of organizations acting on behalf of the Administration
  • Code for Recognized Organizations - resolutions MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory from 1 January 2015
  • SOLAS regulation II-1/3-10 - goal-based standards; the class rules relied on must be verified as conforming
  • IACS Unified Requirements, Unified Interpretations and Procedural Requirements; Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers
  • 1988 SOLAS and Load Lines Protocols - introduced the HSSC; extended to MARPOL by MEPC.39(29) and to Annex VI by MEPC.132(53)
  • Resolution A.1186(33) - Survey Guidelines under the HSSC, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023; revokes A.1156(32). This is the edition in force at this sitting
  • A.1186(33) section 4.2 - annual surveys, three months either side of each anniversary date
  • A.1186(33) section 4.3 - intermediate survey at the second or third anniversary
  • A.1186(33) section 4.4 - periodical surveys; Safety Equipment at the second or third anniversary in place of an annual, Safety Radio at each anniversary

Uncertainty and currency

The survey material is primary: A.1186(33) annex sections 4.2, 4.3 and 4.4 are the source of the annual, intermediate and periodical definitions and frequencies. Two limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed IMO consolidated SOLAS edition, so the text of regulations II-1/3-1, II-1/3-10 and XI-1/1 rests on authoritative secondary reproduction - the substance is not in doubt but the wording is not from a licensed consolidated text. And no count of IACS member societies is asserted anywhere: membership has changed more than once in recent years and a wrong number is worse than no number. One error is worth recording because it recurs: the RO Code is MSC.349(92), not MSC.349(90) as some search summaries suggest.

Finally, the Survey Guidelines edition here is deliberately the 2023 one. A later editor working from current IMO material will find A.1186(33) described as revoked and may "correct" it to A.1207(34). That would introduce a resolution adopted two years after this examination. If a current note is wanted, add it separately and labelled as such.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) What a classification society is, and its two distinct roles
  2. __________a) How class rules are made
  3. __________a) What the structural, mechanical and electrical rules cover
  4. __________a) Implementation through the ship's life
  5. __________a) Why compliance with class rules is now mentioned in SOLAS chapter II-1
  6. __________b) The harmonized system, and where these surveys sit
  7. __________b) Annual versus periodical - the difference and the relevance
Flashcards 11

1 What a class society is and its two roles. 2 How rules are made. 3 What the rules cover. 4 Implementation through the ship's life. 5 Why compliance is now in SOLAS II-1. 6 The harmonized system. 7 Annual versus periodical.

Steps 1 to 5 are the ten-mark limb (a); steps 6 and 7 are the six-mark limb (b).

Classification is a private, contractual relationship with the owner under the society's own rules. Statutory certification as a Recognized Organization is work delegated by the flag State under SOLAS XI-1/1, governed by the RO Code, in which the society acts for the Administration - which retains responsibility.

The same surveyor may do both on the same day, which is exactly why the distinction has to be stated.

That ships be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with the structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society recognized by the Administration under regulation XI-1/1, or with applicable national standards of the Administration providing an equivalent level of safety.

Note all three verbs - designed, constructed AND maintained. The third is what makes loss of class a convention breach.

Because SOLAS never contained scantling rules. Structural standards were a matter of private contract between owner, society and underwriter, so class was commercially indispensable but legally optional. II-1/3-1 makes compliance a convention requirement, extending to maintenance.

This is the discriminating point of the whole question.

The Code for Recognized Organizations, adopted by resolutions MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory from 1 January 2015 under SOLAS, MARPOL and the Load Line Protocol.

It is MSC.349(92), not (90) - an easy and visible slip.

Within three months before or after each anniversary date of the certificate. It consists of a certificate examination, a visual examination of a sufficient extent of the ship and its equipment and certain tests, confirming the condition is being maintained and that no unapproved modifications have been made.

The 'no unapproved modifications' limb is the part most candidates omit.

Only the Cargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate and the Cargo Ship Safety Radio Certificate. For Safety Equipment, within three months before or after the second or third anniversary date, taking the place of one of the annual surveys. For Safety Radio, within three months before or after each anniversary date.

Two certificates, two different frequencies - the fact most answers miss.

An annual survey is largely a visual confirmation that maintenance is being kept up. A periodical survey is an inspection with tests when necessary of the equipment itself, confirming it complies, is in satisfactory condition and is fit for the intended service, together with a documentation check.

Annual is a look; periodical is a test. Equipment is consumed in a way structure is not.

Resolution A.1186(33), Survey Guidelines under the Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, 2023, adopted 6 December 2023 by the 33rd Assembly, revoking A.1156(32). Be careful because A.1207(34) was adopted on 3 December 2025 - two years AFTER this examination - and current material will tell you A.1186(33) is revoked. For this sitting it is the correct citation.

The survey substance is identical in both editions, so a wrong citation yields an answer that reads as entirely correct. That is what makes it dangerous.

No. SOLAS II-1/3-1 requires the ship to be maintained in compliance with a recognized society's rules, so loss of class is a convention non-compliance, enforceable by the flag State and inspectable by port State control.

It is the practical consequence of the word 'maintained' in the regulation.

Both may fall at the second or third anniversary and both may take the place of an annual survey, but they attach to different certificates and have different scopes. The intermediate survey is directed at the ship's condition and structure under the Safety Construction and Load Line certificates; the periodical survey is an equipment inspection with tests under the Safety Equipment and Safety Radio certificates.

The coincident timing is exactly why they get merged in weak answers.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Two hats. Classification is a private, contractual relationship with the owner under the society's own rules - not a government function. Statutory work as a Recognized Organization is delegated by the flag State under SOLAS XI-1/1, governed by the RO Code, MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory since 1 January 2015; the Administration retains responsibility. Rules are made from service experience and defect returns, casualty investigation, research and direct calculation including FEA and fatigue assessment, and technical committees - harmonised across societies through IACS: Unified Requirements, Unified Interpretations, Procedural Requirements, and the Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers. They cover structural (scantlings, hull girder strength, fatigue, corrosion additions, close-up survey), mechanical (propulsion, shafting, boilers, piping, steering gear, type approval) and electrical (generation, distribution and protection, essential and emergency services, high voltage, hazardous areas). Implementation: plan approval and type approval at design; material and welder qualification, NDT and tank testing witnessed, survey during construction and trials; in service the survey cycle, with continuous survey and an approved planned maintenance scheme as alternatives, and a condition of class where a defect is found - class may be suspended or withdrawn. Why it is in SOLAS: regulation II-1/3-1 requires ships to be designed, constructed AND maintained to the structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a society recognized under XI-1/1, or to national standards of equivalent safety. It exists because SOLAS never contained scantling rules - structural standards were private contract, so class was commercially indispensable but legally optional. Now compliance is a convention requirement, it runs to maintenance so loss of class is a SOLAS non-compliance inspectable by port State control, and under II-1/3-10 the rules relied on must themselves be verified as conforming to goal-based standards. (b) HSSC, from the 1988 SOLAS and Load Lines Protocols and extended by MEPC.39(29) and MEPC.132(53), aligns certificates on a common five-year cycle, common anniversary dates and a common three months either side window so surveys combine into one attendance. Guidelines at this sitting: A.1186(33), HSSC 2023, adopted 6 December 2023, revoking A.1156(32). Annual survey - Safety Equipment, Safety Construction and Load Line certificates; every anniversary, three months either side; certificate examination plus visual examination with certain tests, confirming maintenance and no unapproved modifications. Periodical survey - ONLY Safety Equipment and Safety Radio; Equipment at the second or third anniversary in place of an annual, Radio at EVERY anniversary; an inspection with tests when necessary of the equipment plus a documentation check. Relevance: a five-year certificate would otherwise be an unverified assertion - the renewal survey sets the condition, the annual and periodical surveys keep it credible, and equipment is tested harder because it is consumed in a way structure is not.

Critical regulation
SOLAS regulation II-1/3-1 - ships shall be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with the structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society recognized by the Administration under regulation XI-1/1, or with applicable national standards providing an equivalent level of safety.
Major trap
Failing to explain WHY II-1/3-1 exists - that SOLAS never contained scantling rules, so structural standards were private contract until this regulation made them statutory - or stating a single frequency for the periodical survey when it differs between the Safety Equipment and Safety Radio certificates. There is a third trap peculiar to this sitting: the edition of the HSSC Survey Guidelines. The edition in force here is A.1186(33) of 6 December 2023. A.1207(34) was adopted on 3 December 2025, two years AFTER this examination, and current material will wrongly tell you that citing A.1186(33) is out of date. The survey substance is identical in both, so a wrong citation produces an answer that reads as entirely correct.

Understand this first

Two limbs that look separate and are actually the same idea approached from opposite ends. Limb (a) asks how you investigate backwards from an event to something the organisation can change. Limb (b) asks about the model that explains why events happen at all - Reason's organisational accident model, in which an accident requires an unsafe act at the sharp end to coincide with weaknesses that management put into the system long before. Put them together and the answer has a spine: root cause analysis is precisely the technique by which an active failure is converted into information about latent conditions. The regulatory hook that most candidates miss is the Casualty Investigation Code's insistence that a safety investigation is separate from blame and liability - that separation is not a nicety, it is the thing that makes root cause analysis possible, because an investigation that can attribute liability will never get past the last person to touch the system.

Knowledge map
Root Cause Analysis, Active Failures and Latent Failures
  1. a)
    1. 1What root cause analysis is, and why it is required
      • structured method to find the underlying organisational reasons an event occurred
      • purpose is PREVENTION of recurrence, not identification of the immediate failure
      • Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84) of 2008; Parts I and II mandatory via SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010
    2. 2The principles of root cause analysis
      • seek causes, not culprits
      • every accident has multiple causes - a single-cause finding is an incomplete investigation
      • go beyond the immediate; keep asking why until you reach something the organisation controls
    3. 3The steps of a root cause analysis
      • recognise and report; make the situation safe first
      • preserve and collect evidence - VDR, alarms, logs, PMS and defect records, work and rest hours, permits, risk assessments, drawings, early witness accounts
      • establish the sequence of events - timeline or event and causal factor chart
    4. 4Proximate cause versus root cause
      • proximate - the event directly preceding the outcome, at the sharp end, immediate in time
      • root - the underlying systemic reason, at the blunt end, often years earlier
      • correcting the proximate restores the situation; correcting the root prevents recurrence
  2. b)
    1. 5The accident causation models
      • sequential models first - the domino model, an accident as the last of a line of falling dominoes
      • useful for the idea of a chain, but encourages a search for one removable cause close to the accident
      • the organisational accident model - James Reason - defences in depth, the 'Swiss cheese' model
    2. 6Active failures
      • unsafe acts by people in DIRECT CONTACT with the system, effects felt ALMOST IMMEDIATELY
      • slips and lapses - skill-based; intention right, execution or memory wrong; e.g. opening the adjacent valve from habit
      • mistakes - planning failures; rule-based misapplication or knowledge-based in a novel situation; e.g. misdiagnosing a burning exhaust valve as an instrument fault
    3. 7Latent failures
      • delayed consequences of decisions by designers, builders, procedure writers, regulators and management
      • present in the system BEFORE the accident, often for years, dormant until combined with local triggers
      • Reason's term - RESIDENT PATHOGENS
    4. 8Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine
      • active failures are hard to prevent and easy to blame; the next person in the same conditions makes the same slip
      • latent conditions are the manageable target - findable PROACTIVELY by audit, risk assessment, near-miss analysis and trend data
      • that is the justification for the ISM Code's audit and review machinery
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

a)
  1. What root cause analysis is, and why it is required
  2. The principles of root cause analysis
  3. The steps of a root cause analysis
  4. Proximate cause versus root cause
b)
  1. The accident causation models
  2. Active failures
  3. Latent failures
  4. Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine

Memory cue. Limb (a) sequence: COLLECT - SEQUENCE - CAUSAL FACTORS - ROOT - CORRECT AND VERIFY (3). Proximate versus root: SHARP END vs BLUNT END, NOW vs YEARS AGO (4). Limb (b): ACTIVE = who was there, felt at once (6). LATENT = who decided, felt much later (7). The image: the holes MOVE, and the accident needs them to line up through every layer (5). Close on the join: RCA turns an active failure into information about latent conditions (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover62 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 62 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What root cause analysis is, and why it is required
  • structured method to find the underlying organisational reasons an event occurred
  • purpose is PREVENTION of recurrence, not identification of the immediate failure
  • Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84) of 2008; Parts I and II mandatory via SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010
  • safety investigation required into every VERY SERIOUS MARINE CASUALTY - total loss, death, or severe damage to the environment
  • the Code separates the investigation from blame and liability - and that separation is what MAKES root cause analysis possible
  • ISM Code regulation 9 - non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported, investigated, analysed; corrective action procedures
2. The principles of root cause analysis
  • seek causes, not culprits
  • every accident has multiple causes - a single-cause finding is an incomplete investigation
  • go beyond the immediate; keep asking why until you reach something the organisation controls
  • base findings on evidence; gather perishable evidence first
  • establish the sequence before attempting the explanation
  • test each causal factor by removal - would the outcome have changed?
  • a root cause must be ACTIONABLE - 'human error' is not one
  • correct at the level the cause sits; a systemic cause cannot be fixed by retraining one person
  • close the loop - verify implementation AND effectiveness, and share across the fleet
  • just culture - honest reporting must be safe, or the raw material never arrives
3. The steps of a root cause analysis
  • recognise and report; make the situation safe first
  • preserve and collect evidence - VDR, alarms, logs, PMS and defect records, work and rest hours, permits, risk assessments, drawings, early witness accounts
  • establish the sequence of events - timeline or event and causal factor chart
  • identify causal factors and test each by removal
  • analyse to root cause using five whys, fishbone, fault tree or barrier analysis
  • identify the failed defences - absent, inadequate, bypassed or unmaintained
  • develop corrective AND preventive action, each with an owner and a date
  • implement and verify - two checks, implementation and EFFECTIVENESS
  • communicate the lesson fleet-wide and feed it into risk assessment, procedures, training and management review
4. Proximate cause versus root cause
  • proximate - the event directly preceding the outcome, at the sharp end, immediate in time
  • root - the underlying systemic reason, at the blunt end, often years earlier
  • correcting the proximate restores the situation; correcting the root prevents recurrence
  • worked example - seized LO pump bearing back through an unrestored isolation, a permit close-out that was not enforced, a form that required no signature, to a generic procedure never reviewed ship-specifically
  • correcting the proximate replaces a bearing; correcting the root revises the permit system fleet-wide
  • NOTE the different sense of 'proximate cause' in marine insurance - the dominant or effective cause of a loss; related but not the same test
5. The accident causation models
  • sequential models first - the domino model, an accident as the last of a line of falling dominoes
  • useful for the idea of a chain, but encourages a search for one removable cause close to the accident
  • the organisational accident model - James Reason - defences in depth, the 'Swiss cheese' model
  • successive layers: design, engineered safeguards, procedures, supervision, training, alarms, the crew
  • each layer has holes, and THE HOLES MOVE
  • an accident occurs only when the holes in EVERY layer momentarily line up
  • failures divide by WHERE they originate and HOW FAST their effect appears
6. Active failures
  • unsafe acts by people in DIRECT CONTACT with the system, effects felt ALMOST IMMEDIATELY
  • slips and lapses - skill-based; intention right, execution or memory wrong; e.g. opening the adjacent valve from habit
  • mistakes - planning failures; rule-based misapplication or knowledge-based in a novel situation; e.g. misdiagnosing a burning exhaust valve as an instrument fault
  • violations - deliberate departures; routine, situational, exceptional; e.g. a permit signed retrospectively
  • they are the MOST VISIBLE element, which is why investigations stop at them
  • a ROUTINE VIOLATION IS DIAGNOSTIC of a latent condition
7. Latent failures
  • delayed consequences of decisions by designers, builders, procedure writers, regulators and management
  • present in the system BEFORE the accident, often for years, dormant until combined with local triggers
  • Reason's term - RESIDENT PATHOGENS
  • two effects - error-provoking conditions in the workplace, and long-lasting holes in the defences
  • design - poor ergonomics; identical adjacent valves with unclear labelling
  • procedure - generic, unusable or absent; a checklist never revised for this installation
  • manning and resourcing - a manning level that makes the rest-hour regime unachievable; deferred maintenance
  • training - familiarisation that certifies attendance rather than capability
  • maintenance management - generic PMS intervals, tolerated defect backlog
  • culture - production pressure, discouraged reporting, near misses not investigated
8. Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine
  • active failures are hard to prevent and easy to blame; the next person in the same conditions makes the same slip
  • latent conditions are the manageable target - findable PROACTIVELY by audit, risk assessment, near-miss analysis and trend data
  • that is the justification for the ISM Code's audit and review machinery
  • the accident needs BOTH - which is why trivial errors sometimes cause catastrophes and usually cause nothing
  • chance is real, so OUTCOME IS A POOR MEASURE OF BEHAVIOUR
  • hence near misses must be investigated with the same rigour - free evidence about the defences
  • blame destroys the data; just culture separates honest error from recklessness and wilful violation
  • CLOSING LINE - RCA is the technique that converts an active failure into information about latent conditions
Model written answer

1. What root cause analysis is, and why it is required

Root cause analysis (RCA) is a structured method of investigating an event in order to identify the underlying organisational and systemic reasons it occurred, so that corrective action can be taken which prevents recurrence - as distinct from identifying the immediate technical or human failure and stopping there.

The regulatory footing matters, because it explains the method's whole orientation. The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) in 2008 and made mandatory as to Parts I and II by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 with effect from 1 January 2010, requires a marine safety investigation into every very serious marine casualty - one involving the total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. Critically, the Code provides that a marine safety investigation is conducted to prevent future casualties, and is separate and distinct from, and must not seek to apportion, blame or determine liability.

That separation is the licence for root cause analysis. An investigation that may apportion blame will meet defensive witnesses and will stop at the individual; only an investigation insulated from liability can follow the chain back into the design office, the manning decision and the procedure that was written badly.

The same discipline is required of the company internally by the ISM Code regulation 9: the safety management system must include procedures ensuring that non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences are reported, investigated and analysed with the objective of improving safety and pollution prevention, and the Company must establish procedures for the implementation of corrective action.

2. The principles of root cause analysis

  1. Seek causes, not culprits. The purpose is prevention, not attribution. Blame stops the enquiry at the last person to touch the system.
  2. Every accident has multiple causes. A single-cause finding is almost always an incomplete investigation.
  3. Go beyond the immediate. Keep asking why until the answer reaches something the organisation controls - a decision, a procedure, a resource, a design.
  4. Base findings on evidence. Physical evidence, records, data and interviews - and gather perishable evidence first.
  5. Establish the sequence before the explanation. Build the timeline of what happened before attempting why.
  6. Distinguish causal factors from contributing conditions. Test each candidate by asking: if this had not existed, would the event have been prevented or mitigated?
  7. A root cause must be actionable. "Human error" is not a root cause because nothing can be done to it; "the procedure did not cover this configuration" is, because it can be rewritten.
  8. Corrective action must address the level at which the cause sits. A systemic cause cannot be corrected by retraining one individual.
  9. Close the loop. Verify that corrective action was implemented and that it worked, and feed the finding back across the fleet.
  10. Just culture. Honest reporting must be safe, or the raw material of the analysis never arrives. The line is drawn at recklessness and wilful violation, not at error.

3. The steps of a root cause analysis

  1. Recognise and report the event - accident, near miss, non-conformity or hazardous occurrence. Immediate actions to make the situation safe come first.
  2. Preserve and collect evidence. Physical evidence, VDR and alarm data, engine and bridge logs, planned maintenance and defect records, work and rest hours, permits to work, risk assessments, procedures and drawings, and witness accounts taken early while memory is fresh.
  3. Establish the sequence of events. Build a timeline, or an event and causal factor chart, of what actually happened - what, where, when, in what order.
  4. Identify the causal factors. At each step ask what conditions or actions allowed the event to progress, and test each by removal - if it were absent, would the outcome change?
  5. Analyse to root cause. Apply a technique - the five whys, a cause-and-effect (fishbone) diagram, fault tree analysis, barrier analysis - to drive each causal factor back to the organisational decision, condition or omission behind it.
  6. Identify the failed defences. Ask which barriers should have stopped this and why each did not - absent, inadequate, bypassed or not maintained.
  7. Develop corrective and preventive action. Corrective action fixes this occurrence; preventive action removes the condition that produced it. Assign an owner and a date to each.
  8. Implement and verify. Confirm the action was taken, and then confirm it was effective - these are two different checks and the second is usually the one omitted.
  9. Communicate and share the lesson - across the fleet, not just to the ship involved - and feed it into risk assessments, procedures, training, design standards and the next management review.

Note that steps 8 and 9 mirror the ISM Code's own machinery of corrective action, internal audit and management review, and the same plan - implement - monitor - evaluate loop that governs the SEEMP. It is one management discipline applied to different subject matter.

4. Proximate cause versus root cause

Proximate (immediate) causeRoot cause
What it isThe event or condition directly preceding and producing the outcomeThe underlying systemic or organisational reason the proximate cause was able to occur
Where it sitsAt the sharp end - the equipment, the watch, the operatorAt the blunt end - design, procedure, resourcing, management decision
In timeImmediate - seconds to hours beforeOften months or years earlier
Correcting itRestores the situation. Does not prevent recurrencePrevents recurrence, and usually prevents other events too
Typical finding"The bearing seized"; "the officer misread the gauge""The PMS interval was set from a generic template and never revised for this duty"

Worked example. A main engine lubricating oil pump fails and the engine is damaged.

  • Proximate cause - the pump bearing seized through loss of lubrication, and the standby pump did not cut in.
  • Why? The standby pump's auto-start had been isolated during an earlier repair and not restored.
  • Why? There was no permit-to-work close-out check confirming that isolations were removed.
  • Why? The permit system's close-out step was not enforced because the form did not require a signature against each isolation.
  • Why? The form was adopted unchanged from a generic package and was never reviewed against the ship's own systems.
  • Root cause - the company's safety management system adopted generic procedural documents without ship-specific review, so a defence that existed on paper did not exist in practice.

Correcting only the proximate cause replaces a bearing. Correcting the root cause revises the permit system across the fleet - and in doing so removes a hazard that would equally have produced a different accident somewhere else.

A note on terminology, which is worth a mark: the phrase proximate cause is also a term of art in marine insurance, where it means the dominant or effective cause of a loss for the purpose of deciding whether the loss is covered by a peril insured against. In the casualty-investigation sense used here it means the immediate cause in the causal chain. The two usages are related but are not the same test, and should not be conflated.

5. The accident causation models

Early models were sequential - the domino model, in which an accident is the last of a line of falling dominoes and removing one stops the sequence. They usefully introduced the idea of a chain, but they encouraged the search for a single removable cause, and they place that cause close to the accident.

The model that introduced the terms in the question is the organisational accident model developed by James Reason, commonly represented as defences in depth or the "Swiss cheese" model. In it, a complex system is protected by successive layers of defence - design features, engineered safeguards, procedures, supervision, training, alarms and the crew themselves. Each layer has weaknesses, represented as holes. The holes are not static: they open, close and move. An accident occurs only when the holes in every layer momentarily line up, allowing a hazard to pass through all the defences to the loss.

Within that model, failures divide into two kinds by where they originate and how quickly their effect appears.

6. Active failures

Active failures are unsafe acts committed by people in direct contact with the system - the sharp end - whose effects are felt almost immediately. They are the errors and violations of operators: the watchkeeper, the engineer, the pilot, the rating.

They are usefully classified as:

  • Slips and lapses - skill-based failures of execution or memory, where the intention was correct but the action was not. Example: an engineer intends to open the correct valve and, working from habit on a familiar manifold, opens the adjacent one; or a duty officer omits a step in a checklist he has completed a thousand times.
  • Mistakes - failures of planning, where the action went as intended but the intention was wrong. Rule-based, where a good rule is misapplied, or knowledge-based, where the situation is novel. Example: an officer misdiagnoses a fluctuating exhaust temperature as an instrumentation fault and continues, when the correct diagnosis was a burning exhaust valve.
  • Violations - deliberate departures from a rule or procedure. Routine violations become the normal way the job is done because the procedure is cumbersome; situational violations arise because the procedure cannot be complied with in the circumstances, for instance where manning or time does not allow it; exceptional violations occur in unusual situations. Example: a permit to work signed retrospectively because the routine has always been to work first and paper afterwards.

Two things must be said about active failures. They are the most visible element of any accident and the easiest to identify - which is precisely why investigations stop at them. And a routine violation is diagnostic of a latent condition: if a whole department is habitually departing from a procedure, the procedure, the manning or the time allowed is the real finding.

7. Latent failures

Latent failures - or latent conditions - are the delayed consequences of decisions taken by designers, builders, procedure writers, regulators and senior management. They are present in the system before the accident, often for years, lying dormant and harmless until they combine with local circumstances and active failures to produce a loss. Reason described them as resident pathogens: present in the body of the system, doing nothing until conditions favour them.

They have two kinds of effect - they create error-provoking conditions in the workplace, and they create long-lasting holes or weaknesses in the defences.

Where it originatesLatent conditionExample
DesignPoor ergonomics and equipment layoutTwo dissimilar valves, identical in appearance, mounted adjacent with unclear labelling - which makes the slip in section 6 almost inevitable eventually
ProcedureProcedures that are generic, unusable, contradictory or absentA checklist adopted unchanged from another ship class and never revised for this installation
Manning and resourcingMinimum safe manning applied to a workload it does not support; deferred maintenance under budget pressureFatigue arising from a manning level that makes the rest-hour regime unachievable in a short-sea trade
Training and competenceFamiliarisation that certifies attendance rather than capabilityAn officer who has never operated the system in the abnormal mode in which it eventually fails
Maintenance managementPMS intervals set generically; defect backlog toleratedThe unrevised lubricating-oil pump interval in the worked example above
Organisational cultureProduction pressure over safety; reporting discouraged; near misses not investigatedA vessel where near-miss reporting has fallen to zero, which is evidence of suppression, not of safety

8. Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine

The distinction is not academic; it decides what an organisation does after an accident.

  • Active failures are hard to prevent and easy to blame. People will always make errors; error is a normal feature of competent human performance, not a defect in it. Disciplining the individual who made the slip changes nothing, because the next person in the same conditions will make the same slip.
  • Latent conditions are the manageable target. They can be identified proactively - by audit, inspection, risk assessment, near-miss analysis and trend data - before any accident, because they are present in the system all along. This is the entire justification for the ISM Code's audit and review machinery.
  • The accident needs both. A latent condition alone usually produces nothing; an active failure alone is usually caught by a defence. The loss occurs when an active failure meets a set of latent conditions that have opened the holes in every layer at once - which is why apparently trivial errors sometimes produce catastrophes and identical errors usually produce nothing at all.
  • Chance is real, and outcome is a poor measure of behaviour. Because the alignment of the holes is partly a matter of timing, the same unsafe act may on one day be a near miss and on another a total loss. That is why near misses must be investigated with the same rigour as accidents - they are free evidence about the state of the defences, delivered without the cost.
  • Blame destroys the data. If reporting an error is punished, the reports stop, the latent conditions become invisible, and the organisation loses the only early warning it has. Hence just culture: distinguish honest error, which is reported and learned from, from recklessness and wilful violation, which are not excused.

The practical conclusion, and the right note to end on: root cause analysis is the technique that converts an active failure into information about latent conditions. The proximate cause tells you what happened at the sharp end; the root cause tells you which hole in which defence let it through, and that is the only finding a company can actually act on across a fleet.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight marks each, and the two limbs are closely related but must not be merged. Limb (a) is method - principles, steps, and one clean distinction. Limb (b) is theory applied - it names a concept from accident causation models and asks for discussion with suitable examples.

The single easiest way to lift this answer above the average is to join the two limbs at the end: root cause analysis is the technique by which an active failure is converted into information about latent conditions. Most candidates answer the two limbs as though they were unrelated questions on the same paper. They are the same idea from two directions.

Note also that limb (b) prints "giving suitable examples". Examples are not decoration here; they are part of the printed instruction, and an answer that defines active and latent failures abstractly has left marks on the table.

The two definitions, stated precisely

Get these two sentences right and limb (b) has a spine:

  • Active failures are unsafe acts committed by people in direct contact with the system, whose effects are felt almost immediately.
  • Latent failures are the delayed consequences of decisions taken by designers, procedure writers and management, which lie dormant in the system until they combine with local triggering conditions and active failures to produce a loss.

The two discriminators buried in those definitions are WHO (sharp end versus blunt end) and WHEN (immediate versus delayed, often by years). If time is short, say those two things and give one example of each.

Reason's model - attribute it, and use the right image

The terms come from the organisational accident model developed by James Reason, usually drawn as defences in depth or the Swiss cheese model. Naming the model and its author is worth a mark and takes four words.

But use the image accurately. The point of the model is not that there are holes; it is that the holes move, and that an accident happens only when they line up through every layer at once. That is what explains the two things candidates find puzzling: why a trivial slip occasionally causes a catastrophe, and why the same slip usually causes nothing. It also produces the strongest single argument in limb (b) - since alignment is partly a matter of timing, outcome is a poor measure of behaviour, and near misses therefore deserve the same investigation as accidents.

If a diagram is drawn, keep it small and label it: successive slices representing design, engineered safeguards, procedures, supervision and the operator; holes in each; one trajectory passing through all of them. Do not spend five minutes on it.

The regulatory hook that most answers miss

This looks like a pure management-theory question, and answers that treat it that way score respectably and no better. The hook that lifts it is the Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84) of 2008, Parts I and II made mandatory by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 with effect from 1 January 2010, which requires a safety investigation into every very serious marine casualty - total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment.

The provision that actually matters for this question is the Code's statement that a marine safety investigation is conducted to prevent future casualties and is separate and distinct from, and must not seek to apportion, blame or determine liability. That is not incidental - it is the reason root cause analysis is possible at all. An investigation that can attribute liability meets defensive witnesses and stops at the individual. Making that connection explicitly is the mark of a management-level answer.

The internal counterpart is ISM Code regulation 9: non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported, investigated and analysed with the objective of improving safety, and corrective action procedures established. Together these give you the external duty and the internal duty.

Do not say 'human error' is a root cause

This is the most valuable single point in limb (a). "Human error" is a proximate cause, never a root cause, because nothing can be done to it - you cannot issue a corrective action against human nature. The test of a root cause is that it is actionable: it names something the organisation controls and could change. "The officer misread the gauge" is not actionable. "The gauge was unlabelled and identical to the adjacent one" is.

The same test disposes of the other weak findings that appear in real investigation reports - "lack of situational awareness", "failure to follow procedure", "complacency". Each is a restatement of what happened, not an explanation of why it was allowed to happen.

The five whys, used properly

The worked example in the model answer runs a five-whys chain from a seized bearing to a generic procedure adopted without ship-specific review. Two cautions worth carrying:

  • Five is not a rule. Stop when you reach something the organisation controls and can change, whether that takes three steps or seven.
  • A single chain is a simplification. Real accidents have several converging chains, which is why the fishbone diagram and fault tree analysis exist - they allow multiple branches where the five whys allows one. Saying this shows you understand the technique's limits rather than reciting it.

Common mistakes

  • Defining active and latent failures without examples, when the stem prints "giving suitable examples".
  • Treating latent as meaning "hidden equipment defect". A latent condition is a decision and its consequences - a procedure, a manning level, a design choice - not a dormant mechanical fault.
  • Saying "human error" is a root cause.
  • Confusing proximate cause in casualty investigation with proximate cause in marine insurance. Note the difference expressly; it earns a mark.
  • Presenting the Swiss cheese holes as fixed. They move, and that is the point of the model.
  • Giving the steps of RCA but omitting verification of effectiveness, which is the step organisations actually omit.
  • Omitting the fleet-wide sharing of the lesson - the finding is worthless if it stays on one ship.
  • Not naming a technique. The five whys, fishbone, fault tree and barrier analysis are cheap marks.
  • Attributing the Swiss cheese model to IMO. It is Reason's model, adopted into IMO guidance.
  • Ignoring near misses, which is where the model's practical payoff lies.

Examiner traps

  • "Give me a latent failure on your last ship." Have one ready that is a decision, not a defect - a generic checklist, a PMS interval never revised, a manning level that made the rest-hour regime unachievable.
  • "Whose fault was the accident?" Resist. The Casualty Investigation Code separates safety investigation from blame and liability precisely so that the question is not asked in these terms.
  • "Why investigate a near miss when nothing happened?" Because whether an event becomes a loss depends partly on the chance alignment of the holes, so a near miss is evidence about the state of the defences delivered without the cost.
  • "Your crew routinely bypasses a procedure. Is that a violation or a latent condition?" Both - and the routine violation is diagnostic of the latent condition. If everyone departs from the procedure, the procedure, the manning or the time allowed is the finding.
  • "Which is easier to eliminate, active or latent?" Latent, because they are present in the system all along and can be found proactively by audit and risk assessment. Active failures are a permanent feature of human performance.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define active and latent failure. Unsafe acts at the sharp end with immediate effect; delayed consequences of blunt-end decisions, dormant until triggered.
  • Who developed the model? James Reason - the organisational accident or Swiss cheese model.
  • Name three RCA techniques. Five whys, cause-and-effect (fishbone) diagram, fault tree analysis - and barrier analysis.
  • Distinguish proximate from root cause. Immediate event in the chain versus the underlying organisational reason it could occur.
  • Which resolution is the Casualty Investigation Code, and what made it mandatory? MSC.255(84), 2008; SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010, for Parts I and II.
  • What is a very serious marine casualty? One involving total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment.
  • Which ISM Code regulation deals with accidents and corrective action? Regulation 9.
  • Classify errors. Slips and lapses (skill-based), mistakes (rule- and knowledge-based), and violations (routine, situational, exceptional).
  • Why must an investigation be separate from blame? Because attribution produces defensive witnesses and stops the enquiry at the individual, leaving the latent conditions in place.

Memory framework

For limb (a), the RCA sequence in five beats: COLLECT - SEQUENCE - CAUSAL FACTORS - ROOT - CORRECT AND VERIFY. Gather the evidence, build the timeline, find what mattered, drive each one back to something the organisation controls, then fix it and prove the fix worked.

For proximate versus root: SHARP END versus BLUNT END, and NOW versus YEARS AGO. Correcting the proximate cause restores the situation; correcting the root cause prevents the recurrence.

For limb (b): ACTIVE = who was there, felt at once. LATENT = who decided, felt much later. And the closing line that joins the limbs - RCA is how an active failure becomes information about latent conditions.

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution MSC.255(84), 2008 - Casualty Investigation Code; investigation to prevent future casualties, separate and distinct from blame or liability
  • SOLAS regulation XI-1/6, effective 1 January 2010 - makes Parts I and II of the Code mandatory
  • Very serious marine casualty - total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment; a safety investigation is required into every one
  • ISM Code regulation 9 - reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; corrective action procedures
  • ISM Code regulation 1.2.2 - assess all identified risks and establish appropriate safeguards; regulation 12 - Company verification, review and evaluation
  • A.1070(28) paragraphs 38 to 41 - flag State marine safety investigations by impartial and objective investigators; the three-days-absence reporting threshold
  • Reason's organisational accident model - active failures, latent conditions, defences in depth (Swiss cheese)
  • Generic Error Modelling System - slips and lapses, rule- and knowledge-based mistakes, and violations
  • Techniques: five whys, cause-and-effect (Ishikawa) diagram, fault tree analysis, event and causal factor charting, barrier analysis

Uncertainty and currency

This is the least date-sensitive question on the paper, and that is itself worth knowing when planning revision time. The Casualty Investigation Code is unamended at this sitting, the ISM Code provisions relied on are long settled, and the accident causation material is established literature rather than a regulatory instrument that moves.

Four limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed copy of the Casualty Investigation Code or the ISM Code, so their provisions rest on the adopting resolution and authoritative reproduction, and nothing is quoted verbatim at length. The active/latent distinction and the defences-in-depth model are attributed to Reason and to the human-factors literature; they are widely reproduced in IMO and flag State guidance but are not themselves the text of an IMO instrument, and the answer does not present them as one. No casualty statistic is asserted - figures for the proportion of casualties attributed to human factors circulate widely, vary by study and period, and none was verified for this sitting, so the argument is made without leaning on a number. And the RCA techniques are presented as established industry techniques, not as convention requirements, because that is what they are.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) What root cause analysis is, and why it is required
  2. __________a) The principles of root cause analysis
  3. __________a) The steps of a root cause analysis
  4. __________a) Proximate cause versus root cause
  5. __________b) The accident causation models
  6. __________b) Active failures
  7. __________b) Latent failures
  8. __________b) Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine
Flashcards 13

Most answers treat this as pure theory. The separation from blame is what allows an investigation to reach past the individual.

Three limbs, exactly stated - and the threshold that makes investigation mandatory rather than discretionary.

Recognise and report, making the situation safe first; preserve and collect evidence; establish the sequence of events; identify the causal factors and test each by removal; analyse to root cause using a technique; identify which defences failed and why; develop corrective and preventive action with an owner and a date; implement and verify both implementation and effectiveness; communicate the lesson fleet-wide.

The single most valuable point in limb (a), and the one that distinguishes a management-level answer.

The root cause is the underlying systemic or organisational reason the proximate cause was able to occur - at the blunt end, often years earlier, and correcting it prevents recurrence and usually prevents other events too.

Sharp end versus blunt end, now versus years ago, restore versus prevent.

In marine insurance it means the dominant or effective cause of a loss, for deciding whether the loss arises from a peril insured against. In casualty investigation it means the immediate cause in the causal chain. They are related but are not the same test and should not be conflated.
