A near miss is evidence about the current state of the defences delivered without the cost of an accident - and it is the main proactive source of latent-condition data.

The practical payoff of the whole model, and a standard oral question.

Latent conditions. Active failures are a permanent feature of competent human performance and cannot be eliminated - the next person in the same conditions makes the same slip. Latent conditions are present in the system all along and can therefore be found proactively, before any accident, by audit, inspection, risk assessment, near-miss analysis and trend data. That is the justification for the ISM Code's audit and management review machinery.

It converts the theory into a management conclusion, which is what a Class I examiner is looking for.

Quick revision
15-second recall
RCA = structured investigation to find the underlying organisational reasons an event occurred, so recurrence can be prevented. Regulatory hook: Casualty Investigation Code, MSC.255(84) (2008); Parts I and II mandatory via SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010; safety investigation required into every very serious marine casualty = total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. The Code makes the investigation separate from blame and liability - and that separation is what makes RCA possible. Internally: ISM Code reg 9 - non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported, investigated, analysed; corrective action procedures. PRINCIPLES: causes not culprits; multiple causes always; go beyond the immediate; evidence-based; sequence before explanation; test causal factors by removal; a root cause must be ACTIONABLE (so 'human error' is NOT a root cause); correct at the level the cause sits; verify effectiveness and share fleet-wide; just culture. STEPS: report and make safe → preserve and collect evidence (VDR, alarms, logs, PMS, rest hours, permits, drawings, early witness accounts) → establish the sequence → identify causal factors → analyse to root cause (five whys, fishbone/Ishikawa, fault tree, barrier analysis) → identify failed defences → corrective AND preventive action with owner and date → implement and verify effectiveness → communicate fleet-wide. PROXIMATE vs ROOT: proximate = directly preceding event, sharp end, immediate, fixing it restores the situation. Root = underlying systemic reason, blunt end, often years earlier, fixing it prevents recurrence. (Note: 'proximate cause' in marine insurance means the dominant or effective cause of a loss - related but a different test.) MODELS: sequential/domino first; then Reason's organisational accident model, drawn as defences in depth / Swiss cheese - layers of defence each with holes, the holes MOVE, and an accident occurs only when they line up through every layer. ACTIVE FAILURES = unsafe acts by people in direct contact with the system, effects felt almost immediately: slips and lapses (skill-based - opening the adjacent valve from habit), mistakes (rule- or knowledge-based - misdiagnosing a burning exhaust valve as an instrument fault), violations (routine, situational, exceptional - a permit signed retrospectively). A routine violation is diagnostic of a latent condition. LATENT FAILURES/CONDITIONS = delayed consequences of decisions by designers, procedure writers, management; present for years, dormant - Reason's 'resident pathogens'; they create error-provoking conditions and holes in the defences: poor ergonomics and labelling; generic unrevised procedures; manning that makes the rest-hour regime unachievable; familiarisation certifying attendance not capability; generic PMS intervals and defect backlog; culture of production pressure and suppressed reporting. WHY IT MATTERS: active failures are inevitable and blaming changes nothing; latent conditions are the manageable target, findable proactively by audit, risk assessment and near-miss analysis - the justification for ISM audit and review. The accident needs both; chance means outcome is a poor measure of behaviour, so near misses deserve the same rigour; blame destroys the reporting data. CLOSE: RCA is how an active failure becomes information about latent conditions.
Keywords
causes not culprits; 'human error' is not a root causeproximate = sharp end, now; root = blunt end, years agoroot cause must be ACTIONABLEReason's organisational accident model; the holes MOVEactive = direct contact, immediate; latent = decisions, delayedroutine violation is diagnostic of a latent conditionlatent conditions are the proactively manageable targetnear misses deserve the same rigour as accidentsMSC.255(84); SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010; ISM reg 9
Critical numbers
  • Casualty Investigation Code - resolution MSC.255(84), adopted 2008
  • SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - effective 1 January 2010, making Parts I and II mandatory
  • Very serious marine casualty - total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment
  • ISM Code regulation 9 - reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences
  • A.1070(28) paragraph 40 - accidents involving absence from duty of three days or more are investigated and the results made public
  • Three classes of unsafe act - slips and lapses, mistakes, violations
  • Three classes of violation - routine, situational, exceptional
Critical regulation
The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) and made mandatory as to Parts I and II by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 with effect from 1 January 2010, requires a marine safety investigation into every very serious marine casualty and provides that such an investigation is conducted to prevent future casualties and is separate and distinct from, and must not seek to apportion, blame or determine liability.
Major trap
Offering 'human error' as a root cause. It is a proximate cause and never a root cause, because nothing can be done to it - the test of a root cause is that it names something the organisation controls and could change. The same test disposes of 'lack of situational awareness', 'failure to follow procedure' and 'complacency', each of which restates what happened rather than explaining why it was allowed to happen. Two further traps: treating a latent failure as a dormant mechanical defect when it is a management or design DECISION and its consequences; and defining active and latent failures abstractly when the stem expressly prints 'giving suitable examples'.

Also on the platform: QP2412 Q8 - the SEEMP self-evaluation and improvement cycle, the same corrective-action discipline applied to energy efficiency · QP2412 Q2 - the Noise Code, an example of a designed-in latent condition being regulated at source · QP2412 Q9 - the III Code, under which flag States investigate casualties and report to the Organization

The regulatory anchor is the Code of the International Standards and Recommended Practices for a Safety Investigation into a Marine Casualty or Marine Incident (Casualty Investigation Code), adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) in 2008, Parts I and II of which were made mandatory by amendments to SOLAS chapter XI-1 inserting regulation XI-1/6, effective 1 January 2010. The Code's central principle - that a marine safety investigation is conducted to prevent future casualties and is separate and distinct from, and must not seek to apportion, blame or determine liability - was established from the Code itself, as was the requirement to conduct a safety investigation into every VERY SERIOUS MARINE CASUALTY, defined as one involving the total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. The definitions of active and latent failures are taken from the established organisational accident model developed by James Reason and adopted into IMO human-element guidance; the answer attributes the model and does not present it as an IMO instrument. The ISM Code anchor for corrective action is regulation 9, non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences. No date-sensitive claim is made anywhere in this answer.

  • PRIMARY: SOLAS regulation XI-1/6, inserted by the 2008 amendments and effective 1 January 2010, making Parts I and II of the Casualty Investigation Code mandatory
  • PRIMARY: Casualty Investigation Code - the obligation to conduct a marine safety investigation into every very serious marine casualty, defined as a marine casualty involving the total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment
  • PRIMARY: ISM Code regulation 9 - reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; the requirement that the safety management system include procedures ensuring these are reported, investigated and analysed with the objective of improving safety and pollution prevention, and that the Company establish procedures for the implementation of corrective action
  • PRIMARY: ISM Code regulation 1.2.2 - the safety management objectives of the Company include assessing all identified risks and establishing appropriate safeguards; and regulation 12 - Company verification, review and evaluation
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: the organisational accident model developed by James Reason - the distinction between ACTIVE failures, being unsafe acts committed by people in direct contact with the system whose effects are felt almost immediately, and LATENT failures or latent conditions, being the delayed consequences of decisions taken by designers, builders, procedure writers and management, which lie dormant until combined with local triggering factors; and the associated defences-in-depth or 'Swiss cheese' representation
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: the Generic Error Modelling System distinction between skill-based slips and lapses, rule-based and knowledge-based mistakes, and violations - routine, situational and exceptional - used here to classify active failures
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: IMO human element guidance on the investigation of human factors in marine casualties, requiring investigators to have access to expertise in interviewing technique, evidence gathering and evaluation of the effects of the human element
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: A.1070(28) annex paragraphs 38 to 41 - flag State marine safety investigations, impartial and objective investigators, and the reporting threshold of an accident involving absence from duty of three days or more
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: established root cause analysis techniques - the five whys, the cause-and-effect (Ishikawa or fishbone) diagram, fault tree analysis, event and causal factor charting, and barrier analysis

Unresolved
  • MIW holds no licensed copy of the Casualty Investigation Code or of the ISM Code. The provisions relied on are established from the adopting resolution and from authoritative reproduction rather than from a licensed consolidated text, and nothing is quoted verbatim at length.
  • The active/latent failure distinction and the defences-in-depth model are attributed to James Reason and to the human-factors literature. They are widely reproduced in IMO and flag State guidance but are not themselves the text of an IMO instrument, and the answer does not present them as one.
  • No casualty statistic is asserted - for example, no figure for the proportion of marine casualties attributed to human factors. Such figures circulate widely, vary by study and period, and none was verified for this sitting. The answer makes the argument without leaning on a number.
  • The five whys, fishbone, fault tree, event and causal factor charting and barrier analysis are established industry techniques rather than instruments mandated by any convention; they are presented as techniques, not requirements.

Understand this first

Two questions in one, and they test different things. Limb (a) asks how the Convention is BUILT - and the reason it is built that way is that the MLC had to consolidate some sixty-eight earlier instruments, bind States firmly on rights, leave them room on method, and still be amendable without convening a new conference. That produces the three levels (Articles, Regulations, Code) and the mandatory-versus-due-consideration split inside the Code, all running vertically on a shared numbering scheme across five Titles. Limb (b) then lands on the one document where that architecture becomes visible on a ship: the DMLC, whose Part I is the flag State saying WHAT its law requires and whose Part II is the shipowner saying HOW this particular ship keeps to it. Get those two authorships the right way round and most of limb (b) follows. One date matters more than any other on this paper: the 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024, in the month of this examination.

Memory cue. Structure is A-R-C: Articles, Regulations, Code - and in the Code, A is mandatory, B is not (steps 1-2). Numbering stacks: x.y, Ax.y, Bx.y. Five Titles follow the seafarer: GET the job, the TERMS, LIVE aboard, STAY WELL, who CHECKS (3). Amendments by date, not tense (4). Then the DMLC: Part I is the LAW, Part II is the SHIP (6, 7) - or I says WHAT, II says HOW.

Model written answer

1. What the MLC is, and why its structure is unusual

The Maritime Labour Convention, 2006 is an International Labour Organization convention, adopted at the 94th (Maritime) Session of the International Labour Conference, which consolidated some sixty-eight earlier maritime labour Conventions and Recommendations into a single instrument. It is widely described as the fourth pillar of the international regulatory regime for quality shipping, standing alongside SOLAS, MARPOL and STCW.

Its structure is not the usual ILO shape, and that is deliberate: it had to be firm on rights but flexible on implementation, and amendable at the speed the industry changes rather than by adopting a fresh convention each time.

2. The three-level structure

The Convention is in three parts:

  1. The Articles - the treaty framework. General obligations, definitions and scope, the amendment procedures, entry into force, and the fundamental rights and principles. This is the part that binds a Member as a matter of treaty law.
  2. The Regulations - the core requirements, set out by subject. Each Regulation states a purpose and the substantive obligation in short form.
  3. The Code - the detail of how the Regulations are to be implemented.

The Code is itself in two parts, and the distinction is the single most examinable point in limb (a):

  • Part A - Standards, which are mandatory. A ratifying Member must give effect to them.
  • Part B - Guidelines, which are not mandatory but to which a Member must give due consideration in deciding how to implement the Part A Standard.

The three levels stack vertically on the same subject, and they share a numbering scheme, which is what makes the Convention navigable. For any topic there is a Regulation x.y, a mandatory Standard Ax.y and a Guideline Bx.y. So Regulation 5.1.3 (Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC) is amplified by mandatory Standard A5.1.3 and by Guideline B5.1.3.

Flexibility is delivered by two further devices. Article VI paragraph 3 permits a Member to implement a Part A Standard through a provision that is substantially equivalent - one that gives full effect to the general object and purpose of the provision - provided this is recorded. And Title 3 permits certain exemptions by the competent authority. Both must be declared, which is where the DMLC in limb (b) comes in.

3. The five Titles

The Regulations and the Code are organised into five Titles, each covering the same subject matter at all three levels:

TitleNameWhat it covers
Title 1Minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a shipMinimum age; medical certification; training and qualifications; recruitment and placement services
Title 2Conditions of employmentSeafarers' employment agreements; wages; hours of work and hours of rest; entitlement to leave; repatriation; compensation for loss of the ship; manning levels; career and skill development
Title 3Accommodation, recreational facilities, food and cateringAccommodation and recreational facilities; food and catering, including the ship's cook
Title 4Health protection, medical care, welfare and social security protectionMedical care on board and ashore; shipowners' liability; health and safety protection and accident prevention; access to shore-based welfare facilities; social security
Title 5Compliance and enforcementFlag State responsibilities including inspection and certification; port State responsibilities; and labour-supplying responsibilities

A useful way to hold them: Titles 1 to 4 run in the order a seafarer meets them - getting the job, the terms of the job, living on board, staying well - and Title 5 is how all of it is enforced. Limb (b) of this question lives entirely in Title 5.

4. The amendment position at this sitting

The Convention is "as amended", and stating that accurately is worth a mark because it shows the instrument is being treated as live:

  • 2014 amendments - in force 18 January 2017. Financial security for repatriation and abandonment (Regulation 2.5, Standard A2.5.2) and for shipowners' liability for death or long-term disability (Regulation 4.2, Standard A4.2.1). These also added two matters to Appendix A5-I.
  • 2016 amendments - in force 8 January 2019. Harassment and bullying brought into Guidelines B4.3.1 and B4.3.6.
  • 2018 amendments - in force 26 December 2020. The seafarer employment agreement, wages and entitlements continue during captivity following piracy or armed robbery.
  • 2022 amendments - approved at the 110th session of the International Labour Conference in June 2022 and entering into force on 23 December 2024. Among them: seafarers to be informed of their rights under the financial-security system operated by private recruitment and placement services; facilitation of prompt repatriation, with emphasis on abandonment; recreational facilities to include social connectivity, with reasonable access to ship-to-shore telephone and internet access; and food and drinking water free of charge, with meals nutritious, balanced and of sufficient quantity and quality.

The 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024 - that is, in the very month of this examination. The precise and safe way to state it is by the date: "the 2022 amendments were approved in June 2022 and enter into force on 23 December 2024". That is correct whether the paper is sat before or after that day, and it demonstrates the distinction between approved and in force which is exactly what a Class I examiner is testing.

5. The certification chain - where the DMLC sits

Under Regulation 5.1.3, certification is mandatory for ships of 500 GT or over engaged in international voyages, and ships of 500 GT or over flying the flag of a Member and operating from a port, or between ports, in another country. A shipowner whose ship falls outside those categories may request certification. But note Regulation 5.1.4: all ships covered by the Convention are subject to inspection for all its requirements, whether or not they are certified - certification and inspection are not the same duty.

Two documents are produced, and they do different jobs:

  • the Maritime Labour Certificate - evidence that the ship has been inspected and found to meet the national requirements implementing the Convention. Maximum validity five years (Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 1), subject to an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary dates. An interim certificate may be issued on delivery of a new ship, on change of flag, or when a shipowner assumes responsibility for a ship new to it - for a period not exceeding six months, and it is not renewable;
  • the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance (DMLC) - the document that says what the ship was measured against and how compliance is maintained. It is attached to the Certificate.

Both the Certificate and the DMLC must be carried on board, with a copy posted in a conspicuous place where it is available to the seafarers, and a copy made available to seafarers on request (paragraph 12).

6. DMLC Part I - the flag State's declaration

Part I is drawn up by the competent authority - that is, by the flag State, not by the shipowner - in the form corresponding to the model in Appendix A5-II. Under Standard A5.1.3 paragraphs 9 and 10(a) it must:

  1. identify the list of matters to be inspected in accordance with the Convention - the Appendix A5-I list;
  2. identify, in each of those areas, the national requirements embodying the relevant provisions of the Convention, by reference to the national legal provisions and, to the extent necessary, concise information on their main content;
  3. refer to any ship-type specific requirements under national legislation;
  4. record any substantially equivalent provisions adopted under Article VI paragraph 3; and
  5. clearly indicate any exemption granted by the competent authority as provided in Title 3.

Appendix A5-I lists the matters that must be inspected and found to meet national requirements before a certificate is issued. At this sitting there are SIXTEEN of them - the original fourteen, plus the two added by the 2014 amendments:

  1. minimum age;
  2. medical certification;
  3. qualifications of seafarers;
  4. seafarers' employment agreements;
  5. use of any licensed or certified or regulated private recruitment and placement service;
  6. hours of work or rest;
  7. manning levels for the ship;
  8. accommodation;
  9. on-board recreational facilities;
  10. food and catering;
  11. health and safety and accident prevention;
  12. on-board medical care;
  13. on-board complaint procedures;
  14. payment of wages;
  15. financial security for repatriation; and
  16. financial security for shipowners' liability.

The last two were added by the 2014 amendments, in force 18 January 2017. A great deal of material - including the ILO's own 2015 FAQ - still refers to "the 14 areas", because it predates that amendment. At this sitting the correct figure is sixteen, and a candidate who says "fourteen, plus two added by the 2014 amendments, so sixteen" has demonstrated command of the instrument rather than recall of a slogan.

7. DMLC Part II - the shipowner's declaration

Part II is drawn up by the shipowner and is certified by the competent authority or by a recognized organization duly authorized for the purpose. Under Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 10(b) it must identify:

  • the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance, between inspections, with the national requirements stated in Part I; and
  • the measures proposed to ensure that there is continuous improvement.

So the two Parts answer two different questions, and this symmetry is the cleanest way to hold the whole of limb (b):

DMLC Part IDMLC Part II
Drawn up byThe competent authority (flag State)The shipowner
Certified / issued byIssued under the authority of the competent authority; a duly authorized RO may issue it with the CertificateCertified by the competent authority or a duly authorized recognized organization
Answers the questionWHAT must be complied with - the national law implementing the sixteen A5-I mattersHOW this ship complies, and keeps complying, day to day
ContentNational legal provisions per A5-I area; ship-type specific requirements; substantial equivalences; Title 3 exemptionsMeasures for ongoing compliance between inspections; measures for continuous improvement
CharacterGeneric to the flag, with ship particulars addedShip-specific and operational

Guideline B5.1.3 paragraph 4 states the purpose of the whole document, and it is worth quoting in substance: the declaration should above all be drafted in clear terms designed to help all persons concerned - flag State inspectors, authorized officers in port States, and seafarers - to check that the requirements are being properly implemented. Appendix B5-I gives an example of both Parts.

8. The welfare measures covered, and how they bite

Reading the sixteen A5-I matters as welfare provision rather than as a list, the DMLC covers four groups:

  • Who may be employed and on what terms - minimum age, medical certification, qualifications, employment agreements, regulated recruitment and placement, payment of wages;
  • Fatigue and manning - hours of work or rest, and manning levels for the ship;
  • Living conditions - accommodation, on-board recreational facilities, food and catering. This is the group the 2022 amendments reach, adding social connectivity including reasonable internet access to recreational facilities and requiring food and drinking water free of charge;
  • Health, redress and protection against destitution - health and safety and accident prevention, on-board medical care, on-board complaint procedures, and the two financial security matters covering abandonment and repatriation and death or long-term disability.

The enforcement chain in Title 5 is what makes these real. The flag State inspects and certifies, directly or through an authorized recognized organization. The port State may inspect under Regulation 5.2.1: the Certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence of compliance, but where there are clear grounds - or a seafarer complaint - a more detailed inspection follows, and deficiencies may lead to detention. The on-board complaint procedure, itself one of the sixteen matters, gives the seafarer a route that does not depend on a port call. And under Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 14 the Certificate ceases to be valid if the inspections are not completed, if it is not endorsed after the intermediate inspection, on change of flag, when the shipowner ceases to assume responsibility for the ship, or when substantial changes are made to the structure or equipment covered in Title 3.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight marks each, and the two limbs reward completely different things. Limb (a) is a structure question - it wants the three levels, the Part A/Part B distinction and the five Titles by name, and it is answered fastest as a short hierarchy plus a table. Limb (b) is a function question: not "what is the DMLC" but what each Part contains and who produces it, read against welfare. The instruction "briefly" in limb (b) is not an invitation to write less - it is the examiner saying do not narrate the whole of Title 5.

The highest-value single sentence in the whole answer is the one that says Part I is drawn up by the competent authority and Part II by the shipowner. It is the fact most often stated backwards, and it is the fact that proves the candidate has handled a real DMLC.

The date this question turns on

The 2022 amendments to the MLC entered into force on 23 December 2024 - in the month of this examination. That is not a coincidence you can ignore, and it is not a difficulty either, provided you handle it the way an examiner wants: by the date, not by the tense.

Write "the 2022 amendments were approved at the 110th session of the International Labour Conference in June 2022 and enter into force on 23 December 2024". That sentence is correct whichever side of 23 December the paper is sat, and it displays the approved / adopted / in force distinction that Class I examiners test repeatedly. What you must not write is a bare "the 2022 amendments are now in force" or "are not yet in force" - either may be wrong by a matter of days, and both look like guesswork.

The same discipline explains why the amendment list is worth carrying in full. An answer that describes the MLC as adopted in 2006 and stops is describing an instrument that has moved four times since.

Fourteen or sixteen? The A5-I trap

Almost every revision source says the DMLC covers "the 14 areas". That was true when the Convention entered into force in 2013, and the ILO's own FAQ, published in 2015, still says fourteen - while noting that if the 2014 amendments entered into force, two more would be added.

They did, on 18 January 2017. Appendix A5-I has carried sixteen matters ever since, the two additions being financial security for repatriation and financial security for shipowners' liability. At a December 2024 sitting the answer is sixteen. The safest form of words is "the original fourteen matters, plus the two financial security matters added by the 2014 amendments, so sixteen at present" - which is right, shows the working, and cannot be marked down.

The vertical structure is the thing to hold, not the list

Candidates memorise the five Titles and then cannot say how the Convention actually works. The mechanism is the vertical stack on a shared numbering scheme: for any subject there is a Regulation x.y stating the purpose and obligation, a Standard Ax.y which is mandatory, and a Guideline Bx.y which is not mandatory but must be given due consideration.

Once you have that, everything else follows. You can locate any provision from its number. You can explain why Part B matters despite not being mandatory - a Member that departs from it must be able to justify how it still gives effect to the Part A Standard. And you can explain substantial equivalence under Article VI paragraph 3 as the deliberate flexibility that let so many States ratify, with the DMLC Part I as the place it must be declared so the flexibility is transparent rather than hidden.

Common mistakes

  • Inverting the DMLC Parts. Part I is the competent authority's; Part II is the shipowner's. This is the single most common error on this question.
  • Saying the Maritime Labour Certificate is valid for four years. It is a maximum of five, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary.
  • Saying the DMLC covers fourteen areas. Sixteen since 18 January 2017.
  • Saying Part B is optional and can be ignored. It is not mandatory, but a Member must give due consideration to it.
  • Describing the MLC as adopted in 2006 with no mention of amendments. It has been amended in 2014, 2016, 2018 and 2022.
  • Writing flatly that the 2022 amendments 'are in force' or 'are not yet in force' at this sitting. Give the date - 23 December 2024.
  • Saying only certified ships are inspected. All ships covered are subject to inspection for all requirements under Regulation 5.1.4; certification is mandatory only for ships of 500 GT and over in the two categories of Regulation 5.1.3.
  • Calling the interim certificate renewable. It is issued once, for a maximum of six months, and no further interim certificate follows.
  • Listing four Titles, or naming Title 5 as something other than compliance and enforcement.

Examiner traps

  • "Who signs DMLC Part I?" The competent authority, or someone directly empowered by it; it is issued under the authority of the competent authority, and a duly authorized RO may issue it together with the Certificate.
  • "What is a substantially equivalent provision, and where would you find one?" A national provision giving full effect to the general object and purpose of a Part A Standard, adopted under Article VI paragraph 3 - and recorded in DMLC Part I.
  • "Your ship has just changed flag. What happens to the certificate?" It ceases to be valid on change of flag (Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 14); an interim certificate may be issued for up to six months.
  • "A seafarer asks to see the DMLC. Must you show it?" Yes - a copy must be posted conspicuously where it is available to seafarers, and made available on request.
  • "What does the port State do with the DMLC?" The Certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence of compliance; with clear grounds or on a complaint, a more detailed inspection follows and deficiencies may lead to detention.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Name the three parts of the Convention. Articles, Regulations, Code (Part A Standards, Part B Guidelines).
  • Name the five Titles. Minimum requirements; conditions of employment; accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering; health protection, medical care, welfare and social security; compliance and enforcement.
  • Which ships must be certified? 500 GT and over on international voyages, and 500 GT and over operating from or between ports in another country.
  • What is the certificate's validity and what happens mid-term? Maximum five years, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary.
  • How many matters are in Appendix A5-I? Sixteen - the original fourteen plus two financial security matters added by the 2014 amendments.
  • Which amendments deal with abandonment? The 2014 amendments, financial security under Regulation 2.5 and Standard A2.5.2; the 2022 amendments strengthen prompt repatriation.
  • Which amendments cover captivity after piracy? The 2018 amendments, in force 26 December 2020.
  • What did the 2022 amendments add about connectivity? Recreational facilities should include social connectivity, with reasonable access to ship-to-shore telephone and internet.

Memory framework

For the structure: A-R-C - Articles, Regulations, Code; and the Code is A is mandatory, B is not. Numbering: x.y, Ax.y, Bx.y on the same subject.

For the five Titles, follow the seafarer: GET the job (1), the TERMS of the job (2), LIVE on board (3), STAY WELL (4), and who CHECKS (5).

For the DMLC: Part I is WHAT (the State says what the law is); Part II is HOW (the owner says how this ship keeps to it). Or shorter - I is the law, II is the ship.

Regulation and source map

  • Article VI - Part A mandatory, Part B due consideration; Article VI(3) substantial equivalence
  • Regulation 5.1.1 and Standard A5.1.2 - flag State inspection and certification system; authorization of recognized organizations
  • Regulation 5.1.3 - mandatory certification: 500 GT and over on international voyages, and 500 GT and over operating from or between ports in another country
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 1 - maximum five-year validity; Appendix A5-I list
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraphs 5-8 - interim certificate, maximum six months, not renewable, no DMLC required for it
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraphs 9 and 10(a) - DMLC Part I, drawn up by the competent authority
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 10(b) - DMLC Part II, drawn up by the shipowner, certified by the competent authority or an authorized RO
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 12 - carried on board, copy posted conspicuously
  • Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 14 - when the certificate ceases to be valid; 16 and 17 withdrawal
  • Regulation 5.1.4 - ALL ships covered are subject to inspection for ALL requirements
  • Regulation 5.2.1 - port State inspection; certificate and DMLC as prima facie evidence
  • Appendix A5-I - the sixteen matters; Appendix A5-II - the model forms; Appendix B5-I - the example DMLC
  • Guideline B5.1.3 - how detailed each Part should be, and the purpose of the declaration
  • Regulation 2.5 / Standard A2.5.2 - financial security for abandonment; Regulation 4.2 / Standard A4.2.1 - shipowners' liability

Uncertainty and currency

Three limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed consolidated MLC, 2006 as amended, so the Articles, Regulations, Standards and Appendices rest on the ILO's published material rather than a licensed consolidated text, and nothing is quoted verbatim at length. The detailed content of the individual 2022 amendments is stated at the level recorded in flag State and industry circulars, and no paragraph-level citation of the amending instrument is made. And no ratification count or tonnage figure is asserted - those move continuously and none was verified for this sitting.

One correction is recorded because it is instructive. An automated summary of the ILO source used during research reported the certificate validity as four years and reversed who draws up DMLC Parts I and II. Both were wrong; both were caught against the primary text. It is a clean illustration of why the standing rule is to read the instrument rather than a summary of it - the summary was fluent, plausible and wrong on the two highest-value facts in the question.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) What the MLC is, and why its structure is unusual
  2. __________a) The three-level structure
  3. __________a) The five Titles
  4. __________a) The amendment position at this sitting
  5. __________b) The certification chain - where the DMLC sits
  6. __________b) DMLC Part I - the flag State's declaration
  7. __________b) DMLC Part II - the shipowner's declaration
  8. __________b) The welfare measures covered, and how they bite
Flashcards 12

1 What the MLC is and why its structure is unusual. 2 The three levels - Articles, Regulations, Code. 3 The five Titles. 4 The amendment position at this sitting. 5 The certification chain. 6 DMLC Part I. 7 DMLC Part II. 8 The welfare measures and how they are enforced.

Steps 1 to 4 are the eight-mark limb (a); steps 5 to 8 are the eight-mark limb (b).

The Articles, the Regulations and the Code. The Code is in Part A, containing mandatory Standards, and Part B, containing Guidelines which are not mandatory but to which a Member must give due consideration. They run vertically on a shared numbering scheme - Regulation x.y, Standard Ax.y, Guideline Bx.y on the same subject.

The vertical stack is the mechanism; the Titles are only the index.

Title 1 Minimum requirements for seafarers to work on a ship; Title 2 Conditions of employment; Title 3 Accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering; Title 4 Health protection, medical care, welfare and social security protection; Title 5 Compliance and enforcement.

Follow the seafarer - get the job, the terms, live aboard, stay well, who checks.

Part I is drawn up by the COMPETENT AUTHORITY - the flag State - in the form of the model in Appendix A5-II. Part II is drawn up by the SHIPOWNER and certified by the competent authority or a duly authorized recognized organization. Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 10.

The single most commonly inverted fact on this question.

The list of matters to be inspected under Appendix A5-I; in each of those areas, the national requirements implementing the Convention, by reference to the national provisions plus concise content where necessary; ship-type specific requirements; any substantially equivalent provisions adopted under Article VI paragraph 3; and any exemption granted by the competent authority under Title 3.

Substantial equivalences and exemptions are declared here - it is where the Convention's flexibility becomes transparent.

The measures adopted by the shipowner to ensure ongoing compliance, between inspections, with the national requirements stated in Part I; and the measures proposed to ensure that there is continuous improvement.

'Between inspections' and 'continuous improvement' are the two phrases that earn the mark.

Sixteen. The original fourteen, plus financial security for repatriation and financial security for shipowners' liability, added by the 2014 amendments which entered into force on 18 January 2017. Sources written before 2017 - including the ILO's own 2015 FAQ - say fourteen.

A date-sensitive number that almost all revision material gets wrong.

Ships of 500 GT and over engaged in international voyages, and ships of 500 GT and over operating from a port, or between ports, in another country - Regulation 5.1.3. Maximum validity five years, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary dates. An interim certificate may be issued on delivery of a new ship, on change of flag, or when a shipowner takes on a ship new to it, for not more than six months, and it is not renewable.

Five years and six months are both commonly misremembered, and the interim's non-renewability is a favourite oral question.

Yes. Regulation 5.1.4 provides that all ships covered by the Convention are subject to inspection for all its requirements, whether or not they are certified. Certification under Regulation 5.1.3 is an additional duty for the ships that fall within it.

Candidates routinely equate the scope of certification with the scope of the Convention.

2014 amendments - 18 January 2017, financial security for repatriation and for shipowners' liability. 2016 amendments - 8 January 2019, harassment and bullying. 2018 amendments - 26 December 2020, employment agreement and wages continue during captivity after piracy or armed robbery. 2022 amendments - approved June 2022 at the 110th session of the International Labour Conference, entering into force 23 December 2024.

Describing the MLC as a 2006 instrument and stopping there ignores four sets of amendments.

By date: approved at the 110th session of the International Labour Conference in June 2022 and entering into force on 23 December 2024. Do not write a bare 'now in force' or 'not yet in force' - the examination day is not printed on the paper, and the date formulation is correct either way.

The sitting falls on the entry-into-force boundary, which makes it the cleanest possible illustration of approved versus in force.

Under Article VI paragraph 3, a national law or measure that gives full effect to the general object and purpose of a Part A Standard, adopted where a Member is not in a position to implement that Standard in the manner set out. It must be recorded in DMLC Part I.

It explains how so many States with differing legal systems were able to ratify, and it links limb (a) to limb (b).

Quick revision
15-second recall
MLC, 2006 - ILO, 94th (Maritime) Session; consolidated some 68 earlier instruments; the fourth pillar with SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW. Three parts: ARTICLES (treaty framework, obligations, definitions, amendment), REGULATIONS (core requirements, each with a purpose), CODE - Part A STANDARDS, MANDATORY; Part B GUIDELINES, not mandatory but must be given DUE CONSIDERATION. Shared numbering: Reg x.y / Std Ax.y / Gdl Bx.y on one subject. Article VI(3) substantial equivalence; Title 3 exemptions - both declared in DMLC Part I. FIVE TITLES: 1 minimum requirements to work on a ship (age, medical, qualifications, recruitment and placement); 2 conditions of employment (SEA, wages, hours of work/rest, leave, repatriation, loss of ship, manning, career development); 3 accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering; 4 health protection, medical care, welfare and social security; 5 compliance and enforcement (flag State, port State, labour-supplying). AMENDMENTS: 2014 in force 18 Jan 2017 (financial security for repatriation and shipowners' liability; added 2 matters to A5-I); 2016 in force 8 Jan 2019 (harassment and bullying); 2018 in force 26 Dec 2020 (SEA and wages continue during captivity after piracy); 2022 approved June 2022, IN FORCE 23 DECEMBER 2024 (recruitment financial-security information, prompt repatriation/abandonment, social connectivity and internet access, free food and drinking water). CERTIFICATION (Reg 5.1.3): mandatory at 500 GT+ on international voyages, and 500 GT+ operating from or between ports in another country; others on request. Reg 5.1.4 - ALL covered ships are inspected for ALL requirements, certified or not. Certificate: max FIVE years, intermediate inspection between the 2nd and 3rd anniversary. Interim: max SIX months, NOT renewable, no DMLC needed for it. DMLC PART I - drawn up by the COMPETENT AUTHORITY: identifies the A5-I list, the national requirements in each area, ship-type specific requirements, substantial equivalences, and any Title 3 exemption. DMLC PART II - drawn up by the SHIPOWNER, certified by the competent authority or an authorized RO: measures for ongoing compliance between inspections, and measures for continuous improvement. Part I = WHAT; Part II = HOW. Appendix A5-I: SIXTEEN matters - age, medical, qualifications, SEA, recruitment and placement, hours of work/rest, manning, accommodation, recreational facilities, food and catering, health and safety, medical care, complaint procedures, wages, + financial security for repatriation, + financial security for shipowners' liability. Both documents carried on board, copy posted conspicuously. Port State (5.2.1): certificate and DMLC are prima facie evidence; clear grounds or complaint -> more detailed inspection -> possible detention.

  • Certification mandatory at 500 GT and over - Regulation 5.1.3
  • Maritime Labour Certificate - maximum five years, Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 1

  • Interim certificate - maximum six months, not renewable

Critical regulation
Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 10 - Part I of the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance is drawn up by the COMPETENT AUTHORITY and identifies the Appendix A5-I matters, the national requirements implementing them, ship-type specific requirements, any substantially equivalent provisions and any Title 3 exemption; Part II is drawn up by the SHIPOWNER and certified by the competent authority or a duly authorized recognized organization, identifying the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance between inspections and the measures proposed to ensure continuous improvement.
Major trap
Inverting the two Parts of the DMLC. Part I is the competent authority's statement of national law; Part II is the shipowner's statement of how the ship complies. Two further traps sit alongside it. Appendix A5-I now contains SIXTEEN matters, not fourteen - the two financial security matters were added by the 2014 amendments, in force 18 January 2017, and most revision material (including the ILO's own 2015 FAQ) predates that. And the 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024, the month of this examination: state the date rather than writing 'now in force' or 'not yet in force', because the examination day is not printed on the paper.

Understand this first

Two limbs that are really one subject. Limb (a) asks why it matters WHEN the carrier's responsibility starts and stops, and the answer is that the period decides which regime applies and therefore who bears the loss - because outside the period the carrier can lawfully exclude liability, while inside it any such clause is void. Each successive convention extends the period precisely because the last one left a gap: tackle to tackle, then port to port, then door to door. The trap is that the widest of the three, Rotterdam, is not in force. Limb (b) then puts you inside the period with a real claim, and the shape of a cargo claim is what matters: a clean bill of lading proves the goods were good in, damaged out-turn proves they were bad out, and the burden is on the owner to name a defence. For grain that defence is inherent vice - but it is conditional, because it fails if the holds were not fit or the ventilation was not properly managed. Which is why a grain claim is usually decided by three documents: moisture content at loading, hatch cover testing, and ventilation records.

Knowledge map
Carrier Responsibility under the Cargo Conventions, and Defences
