Model written answer

1. Why the duration of responsibility matters

The period of responsibility decides which legal regime applies to a loss, and therefore who bears it. If the cargo is damaged at a moment falling outside the convention period, the Rules do not govern; liability is then determined by the contract of carriage and by general law, where the carrier may lawfully exclude liability altogether. If the damage occurs inside the period, the Rules apply compulsorily - and under Article III rule 8 of the Hague and Hague-Visby Rules any clause reducing the carrier's liability below the Rules' standard is null and void.

So the same physical damage, to the same cargo, on the same voyage, can produce entirely different outcomes depending on when it happened. That is the statement the question asks to be explained with examples.

2. Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle

Under Article I(e), "carriage of goods" covers the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time when they are discharged from the ship. This is the classic "tackle to tackle" period.

Two consequences follow, and they are the heart of limb (a):

  • the period covers only the sea carriage stage. Anything happening before loading - while the goods sit in a shed, on the quay or in the carrier's inland custody - and anything after discharge - in the terminal, in a warehouse, awaiting collection - falls outside the compulsory regime;
  • in those gaps the carrier may, and in practice does, contract out of liability by a "before and after" clause. The Rules do not prohibit it, because Article III rule 8 only strikes down clauses derogating from liability under the Rules, and outside the period there is no liability under the Rules to derogate from.

The Rules also apply only where a bill of lading or similar document of title is issued (Article I(b)), and "goods" excludes live animals and deck cargo stated as carried on deck and so carried (Article I(c)) - two further gaps in the compulsory cover.

Example. A consignment is damaged by rain while lying on the quay at the load port awaiting loading. Under Hague-Visby that damage falls outside the period of responsibility, and the carrier can rely on an exclusion clause in the bill of lading. Had the identical damage occurred an hour later, with the goods already across the ship's rail, the carrier would be answerable under Article III rule 2 unless it could bring itself within an Article IV exception.

3. Hamburg - port to port

The Hamburg Rules 1978 were written to close exactly that gap. Under Article 4, the carrier's period of responsibility covers the goods for the whole time the carrier is in charge of them at the port of loading, during the carriage, and at the port of discharge - the "port to port" period.

The Article then defines the boundaries precisely: the carrier is deemed to be in charge from the time it has taken over the goods from the shipper, or from an authority or third party to whom the goods must be handed over for shipment under law or regulation at the port of loading; and it remains in charge until it has delivered the goods to the consignee, or placed them at the consignee's disposal in accordance with the contract or local usage, or handed them over to an authority or third party to whom they must be handed over under law or regulation at the port of discharge.

The regime is materially harsher on the carrier in other respects too, and the period is only part of it: Article 5 imposes presumed fault - the carrier is liable for loss occurring while the goods were in its charge unless it proves that it and its servants took all measures that could reasonably be required to avoid the occurrence and its consequences - and there is no nautical fault exception and no long catalogue of excepted perils. The time bar is two years (Article 20) rather than one.

Example. The same rain damage on the quay at the load port, after the carrier has taken the goods into its charge, now falls inside the period. The carrier is liable unless it can show it took all measures reasonably required.

4. Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force

The Rotterdam Rules 2008 go further again. Under Article 12 the period of responsibility begins when the carrier or a performing party receives the goods for carriage and ends when the goods are delivered - a "door to door" period which is not tied to the ports at all, and which can include inland road, rail or inland waterway legs of a contract that is wholly or partly by sea.

This is the point on which candidates most often go wrong: the Rotterdam Rules are NOT IN FORCE. Adopted in 2008 and opened for signature in Rotterdam in 2009, they require twenty ratifications before entering into force under Article 94, and that threshold had not been reached at this sitting. They are therefore a proposed regime and must be described as such - their significance is as the direction of travel, not as applicable law.

Article 12 does allow the parties to agree the time and location of receipt and delivery, subject to limits, and Article 13(2) permits an agreement that the shipper or consignee performs loading, handling, stowing or discharging - the convention's recognition of FIOS terms.

5. The comparison, and the Indian position

Hague / Hague-VisbyHamburgRotterdam
PeriodTackle to tackle - loading to discharge (Art I(e))Port to port - in charge at load port, carriage, discharge port (Art 4)Door to door - receipt to delivery (Art 12)
Basis of liabilityDue diligence for seaworthiness; proper care of cargo; long list of exceptionsPresumed fault; no nautical fault exceptionPresumed fault with a modified list of exonerations; no nautical fault exception
Time barOne year (Art III r 6)Two years (Art 20)Two years (Art 62)
StatusIn force and dominant in world tradeIn force, but not adopted by the major maritime trading nationsNOT in force
IndiaApplies - Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925 as amended in 1993Not a partyNot a party

The Indian position at this sitting. The governing statute is the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, which was amended in 1993 to give effect to the Hague-Visby Rules including the Visby and SDR Protocol amendments. It operates alongside the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856, which governs the transfer of rights of suit to the consignee or endorsee of a bill of lading. India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules or the Rotterdam Rules. So for an Indian contract of carriage the period of responsibility is the tackle-to-tackle period.

Worked comparison on one set of facts. A consignment is taken into the carrier's custody at the load port terminal on the 1st, loaded on the 3rd, discharged on the 20th, and remains in the discharge port terminal until collected on the 25th. Damage occurs on the 2nd and again on the 22nd.

  • Hague-Visby - neither loss is inside the period. Both fall to the contract terms, and a "before and after" clause will usually defeat both claims.
  • Hamburg - both losses are inside the period, because the carrier was in charge of the goods at the load port on the 2nd and at the discharge port on the 22nd. The carrier is liable for both unless it discharges the Article 5 burden.
  • Rotterdam - both would be inside the period, and had the carrier also received the goods at an inland point the cover would extend there too. But the Rules are not in force, so this outcome is hypothetical.

That single set of facts is the clearest way to "explain this statement with examples", because it produces three different answers from one fact pattern.

6. The grain claim - how it is framed

The facts are: a bulk carrier discharged a cargo of grain; the consignee received some cargo in damaged condition; the consignee claims against the shipowner.

The consignee's route is short. If the bill of lading was clean, it is prima facie evidence under Article III rule 3 that the goods were shipped in apparent good order and condition - and conclusive evidence in the hands of a good-faith third-party transferee under the Visby amendment. If the cargo is out-turned damaged, the consignee has established, on the face of it, that the goods were good in and bad out, and the burden shifts to the carrier to bring itself within a defence.

So the shipowner is not asked to prove it was careful in the abstract. It must point to a specific defence.

7. The excepted perils that apply to grain

The catalogue is in Article IV rule 2. The ones that matter on these facts are:

  1. Inherent defect, quality or vice of the goods - rule 2(m). This is the shipowner's principal defence in a grain claim, and it also covers wastage in bulk or weight. Grain is a hygroscopic organic cargo: if it is loaded at a moisture content above the safe limit, it will self-heat, sweat, cake, mould and discolour in the hold no matter how carefully the ship is operated. Damage that is the working out of the cargo's own condition at shipment is not the carrier's responsibility.
  2. Act or omission of the shipper or owner of the goods - rule 2(i). Where the shipper loaded wet, heated or contaminated grain, or misdescribed it, or where damage arises from the shipper's own loading arrangements.
  3. Insufficiency of packing - rule 2(n). Less obvious for a bulk cargo, but it can bite where the shipper was responsible for a protective arrangement that failed.
  4. Perils, dangers and accidents of the sea - rule 2(c). Available where heavy weather of a severity that could not reasonably be guarded against caused water ingress or shifting - but this requires real evidence of exceptional conditions, not merely a rough passage, and the ship must have been seaworthy for the voyage contemplated.
  5. Act, neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or MANAGEMENT of the ship - rule 2(a). The classic and difficult distinction: an error in the management of the ship is excepted, but a failure in the care of the cargo is not. Ventilation is the battleground for grain - if the ventilation failure is characterised as care of cargo, the exception does not help.
  6. Latent defects not discoverable by due diligence - rule 2(p), for example a hatch cover or pipe defect that reasonable inspection would not have revealed.
  7. Fire - rule 2(b), unless caused by the actual fault or privity of the carrier - relevant where self-heating has progressed to combustion.
  8. The catch-all - rule 2(q): any other cause arising without the actual fault or privity of the carrier and without the fault or neglect of its agents or servants. Note that here the burden of proof is expressly on the carrier, so it is a defence of last resort.

8. The overriding obligations that can defeat every defence

The exceptions are not free-standing. Two obligations sit above them, and a shipowner who has breached either will usually lose despite an otherwise good exception:

  • Seaworthiness - Article III rule 1. The carrier must exercise due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage to make the ship seaworthy, properly man, equip and supply her, and make the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the goods. That last limb is cargoworthiness and is where grain claims are won and lost: holds must be clean, dry, free of previous cargo residues and infestation, and the hatch covers weathertight. Under Article IV rule 1, if the loss arises from unseaworthiness the carrier is liable unless it proves due diligence - and the burden of proving due diligence is on the carrier.
  • Care of the cargo - Article III rule 2. The carrier must properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge the goods. For grain this means the ventilation regime above all - ventilating according to an accepted rule when the dew point conditions require it, and not ventilating when it would cause condensation - together with temperature monitoring, hold bilge soundings and pumping, and protection during rain.

The practical position is therefore this: inherent vice is a complete answer to a grain claim only if the ship was cargoworthy and the cargo was properly cared for. If the hatch covers leaked, or the ventilation records are missing or show a regime that made condensation inevitable, the owner will be held to have caused or contributed to the loss and the exception will fail.

9. Procedural and quantum defences

Even where liability cannot be avoided altogether, three further defences reduce or extinguish the claim:

  1. Notice of loss - Article III rule 6. Notice in writing must be given before or at the time of removal of the goods, or within three days where the loss or damage is not apparent. Absence of notice is prima facie evidence of delivery in accordance with the bill of lading, though it does not by itself bar the claim.
  2. Time bar - Article III rule 6. The carrier and the ship are discharged from all liability unless suit is brought within one year of delivery or of the date when the goods should have been delivered. This is a hard defence and defeats many claims outright. (Under Hamburg and Rotterdam the period is two years.)
  3. Limitation of liability - Article IV rule 5, as amended by the Visby and SDR Protocols: the package or unit limit or the weight-based limit, whichever is the higher. For a bulk cargo such as grain there are no packages, so the weight-based limit is the operative one - a point specific to these facts and worth making. The right to limit is lost only where the damage resulted from an act or omission done with intent to cause damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that damage would probably result.

Two further points complete the picture. Under the Article 4 bis Himalaya provision the carrier's defences and limits extend to its servants and agents, though not to independent contractors. And a claim must be brought by a person with title to sue - under the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 the rights of suit pass to the consignee or endorsee to whom property in the goods passes upon or by reason of the consignment or endorsement, so a consignee without title cannot maintain the claim at all.

10. The evidence that makes the defence work

A defence of inherent vice is only as good as the record supporting it. In practice the shipowner needs:

  • the pre-loading hold inspection and cleanliness certificate, and a hatch cover weathertightness test record - ultrasonic or hose test - to establish cargoworthiness;
  • the pre-shipment cargo survey, and above all the moisture content and temperature of the grain at loading, which is the single most valuable document in the case;
  • mate's receipts and any clausing - if the grain was visibly wet, heated or contaminated at loading, the mate's receipt and the bill should have been claused, and a clean bill issued over a claused mate's receipt is a serious problem for the owner;
  • complete ventilation records showing the rule applied and the dew point readings on which each decision was based, plus hold temperature logs and bilge sounding and pumping records;
  • weather records and the deck log for any heavy weather relied on, and the draught survey figures at load and discharge port for any shortage element;
  • prompt appointment of a surveyor at the discharge port, jointly with the cargo interests where possible, and preservation of samples;
  • notification of the P&I Club at once, since the Club will appoint surveyors and lawyers and the owner's cover depends on cooperation.

Conclusion on the facts. The shipowner's best defence is inherent vice under Article IV rule 2(m), supported where the evidence allows by act or omission of the shipper under rule 2(i), and backed by the one-year time bar and the weight-based limitation as fallbacks. But that defence stands or falls on the owner being able to prove it exercised due diligence to make the holds fit for the grain and that it properly ventilated and cared for the cargo throughout - because if either fails, the exception fails with it.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight marks each on two limbs that look unrelated and are in fact the same subject seen from two sides. Limb (a) is comparative law - three conventions, one variable. Limb (b) is applied law - one fact pattern, and the question is what the owner can actually say.

The printed instruction in limb (a) is "explain this statement with examples", and that is what most answers omit. Three convention definitions recited in sequence is a description, not an explanation. The efficient way to earn the explanation marks is a single fact pattern run through all three regimes - the model answer uses damage on day 2 in the load port terminal and day 22 in the discharge port terminal, which produces three different results from one set of facts and demonstrates exactly why the duration matters.

Limb (b) prints "what defence is available to the ship owner?" - singular in form, plural in substance. Give the excepted perils, the overriding obligations that can defeat them, and the procedural and quantum defences, then land on which one actually wins on these facts.

The one sentence that makes limb (a) an explanation

The period of responsibility decides which regime applies, and therefore who bears the loss - because outside the period the carrier can lawfully exclude liability, and inside it Article III rule 8 makes any such clause null and void.

State that at the outset and everything after it is illustration rather than recitation. It also gives you the reason the Hamburg and Rotterdam Rules were drafted at all: each successive convention extends the period because the previous one left a gap in which the carrier could contract out.

Three periods, three phrases

  • Hague / Hague-Visby - TACKLE TO TACKLE. Article I(e): from the time the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship.
  • Hamburg - PORT TO PORT. Article 4: the whole time the carrier is in charge of the goods at the port of loading, during the carriage and at the port of discharge.
  • Rotterdam - DOOR TO DOOR. Article 12: from receipt of the goods for carriage to delivery - not tied to ports, and capable of covering inland legs.

Three phrases, three Articles. If time is very short, those three lines plus the one-sentence reason plus the Rotterdam status point will carry most of limb (a).

The Rotterdam trap

The Rotterdam Rules are not in force. They were adopted in 2008, opened for signature in 2009, and require twenty ratifications under Article 94. That threshold was not met at this sitting and the Convention is not applicable law anywhere.

Writing about them in the present tense as though they governed is a straightforward and highly visible error, and it is exactly the adopted versus in force distinction that Class I examiners test across every subject. Describe them as the intended successor regime, explain what they would change, and say plainly that they are not in force. Note also that the Hamburg Rules, while genuinely in force, have not been adopted by the major maritime trading nations - so "in force" and "commercially dominant" are two different things, and saying so shows real command.

The Indian position - state it, and state it as at the sitting

For an Indian examination this must appear. At this sitting the governing statute is the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, amended in 1993 to give effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, operating alongside the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 for the transfer of rights of suit. India is not a party to the Hamburg or Rotterdam Rules.

So the Indian period of responsibility is tackle to tackle. A candidate who explains the three regimes and then says which one actually governs an Indian carriage has answered a question about law rather than about conventions.

Limb (b): the shape of a cargo claim

Understand the burden and limb (b) writes itself. The consignee proves good in, bad out - and a clean bill of lading does the first half for it, being prima facie evidence under Article III rule 3 and conclusive in the hands of a good-faith transferee under Visby. The burden then shifts to the carrier to bring itself within a defence.

So the answer must name defences, not assert care. And the defences come in three layers:

  1. The excepted perils - Article IV rule 2, of which (m) inherent defect, quality or vice is the grain defence.
  2. The overriding obligations that can defeat them - seaworthiness and cargoworthiness under Article III rule 1, and care of cargo under Article III rule 2.
  3. The procedural and quantum defences - notice, the one-year time bar, and limitation.

Why inherent vice is the right answer for grain - and why it is fragile

Grain is hygroscopic and organic. Loaded above its safe moisture content it will self-heat, sweat, cake, mould and discolour whatever the ship does. Article IV rule 2(m) exists precisely for damage that is the working out of the cargo's own condition at shipment - and it expressly covers wastage in bulk or weight as well, which handles any shortage element of the claim.

But the defence is conditional, and this is the point that separates a good answer. Under Article IV rule 1, where loss arises from unseaworthiness the carrier is liable unless it proves due diligence, and the burden of proving due diligence is on the carrier. So the owner must first show the holds were fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of grain - clean, dry, free of residues and infestation, hatch covers weathertight - and then show it properly ventilated and cared for the cargo under Article III rule 2. Leaking hatch covers or missing ventilation records will destroy an inherent vice defence, because the damage will be attributed to the ship rather than to the cargo.

That is why the model answer ends on evidence. In a real grain claim the case is usually decided by three documents: the moisture content at loading, the hatch cover weathertightness test, and the ventilation records.

The bulk cargo limitation point

A small point that is specific to these facts and therefore valuable. Article IV rule 5 as amended gives the package or unit limit or the weight-based limit, whichever is higher. A bulk grain cargo has no packages or units, so the weight-based limit is the operative one. Candidates who quote a per-package figure for a bulk cargo have not thought about the facts they were given.

The navigation-versus-cargo-care distinction

Article IV rule 2(a) excepts neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or in the management of the ship. It does not except failures in the care of the cargo, which is Article III rule 2 territory. The line between "management of the ship" and "care of the cargo" has generated more litigation than any other provision in the Rules, and ventilation is the classic battleground for grain: the owner will argue the decision was ship management; the cargo interest will argue it was cargo care. Flagging that contest, rather than reciting the exception, is what a management-level answer does.

Common mistakes

  • Describing the Rotterdam Rules as in force. They are not, and require twenty ratifications under Article 94.
  • Reciting three definitions without examples, when the stem prints "explain this statement with examples".
  • Omitting the Indian position entirely in an Indian examination.
  • Saying Hague-Visby covers the whole voyage. It covers loading to discharge only.
  • Treating inherent vice as an unconditional defence. It fails if the ship was uncargoworthy or the cargo was not properly cared for.
  • Forgetting that under Article IV rule 1 the burden of proving due diligence is on the carrier.
  • Quoting a per-package limit for a bulk cargo that has no packages.
  • Omitting the one-year time bar, which is often the most effective defence of all.
  • Confusing the nautical fault exception with a general excuse - it covers navigation and management of the ship, not care of the cargo.
  • Forgetting title to sue under the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856.
  • Ignoring the effect of a clean bill of lading on the burden of proof.

Examiner traps

  • "When does the carrier's responsibility begin under Hague-Visby?" When the goods are loaded on - not when they enter the carrier's custody.
  • "Are the Rotterdam Rules the current law?" No. Adopted 2008, not in force; twenty ratifications required.
  • "Which Rules apply to your Indian voyage charter with a bill of lading?" Hague-Visby, through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925 as amended in 1993.
  • "The grain was wet when loaded but you issued a clean bill. Where does that leave you?" In serious difficulty - the clean bill is prima facie, and conclusive against a good-faith transferee, so the owner may be estopped from proving the true condition at shipment.
  • "Your ventilation records are missing. Does inherent vice still help?" Probably not - the owner must show it properly cared for the cargo under Article III rule 2, and without records it cannot.
  • "How long does the consignee have to sue?" One year under Hague-Visby; two years under Hamburg and Rotterdam.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Give the three periods of responsibility in one phrase each. Tackle to tackle; port to port; door to door.
  • Which Article gives each? Hague-Visby Article I(e); Hamburg Article 4; Rotterdam Article 12.
  • What is the Indian governing statute? The Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, amended 1993 for Hague-Visby.
  • What is the carrier's seaworthiness obligation? Due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage, including making the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the goods.
  • Name five excepted perils. Nautical fault; fire without actual fault or privity; perils of the sea; inherent vice; act or omission of the shipper.
  • Which exception applies to grain? Inherent defect, quality or vice - Article IV rule 2(m), which also covers wastage in bulk or weight.
  • Where does the burden of proof sit on due diligence? On the carrier - Article IV rule 1.
  • What is the time bar? One year from delivery or from when the goods should have been delivered.
  • Which limitation applies to bulk grain? The weight-based limit, because there are no packages.
  • What three documents decide a grain claim? Moisture content at loading, hatch cover weathertightness test, and ventilation records.

Memory framework

For limb (a): TACKLE - PORT - DOOR, and each convention extends the period because the last one left a gap. Add the status line: Hague-Visby governs, Hamburg is in force but marginal, Rotterdam is not in force at all. Then India: 1925 Act as amended in 1993 = Hague-Visby = tackle to tackle.

For limb (b), three layers: EXCEPTIONS - OBLIGATIONS - LIMITS. What I can rely on (Art IV r 2, especially inherent vice); what can defeat it (Art III r 1 seaworthiness and r 2 cargo care); and what caps it anyway (notice, one-year bar, weight-based limitation). And the evidence triad: MOISTURE, HATCHES, VENTILATION.

Regulation and source map

  • Hague-Visby Article I(e) - period of responsibility, loaded on to discharged from; Article I(b) bill of lading; Article I(c) goods, excluding live animals and deck cargo
  • Article III rule 1 - due diligence for seaworthiness and cargoworthy holds; rule 2 - properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge
  • Article III rules 3 and 4 - the bill as prima facie and (Visby) conclusive evidence; rule 6 - notice, three days, and the ONE-YEAR time bar; rule 8 - derogating clauses null and void
  • Article IV rule 1 - unseaworthiness, with the burden of proving due diligence ON THE CARRIER
  • Article IV rule 2(a) to (q) - the excepted perils; (m) inherent defect, quality or vice and wastage in bulk or weight; (i) act or omission of the shipper; (n) insufficiency of packing; (q) catch-all with burden on the carrier
  • Article IV rule 5 (Visby and SDR Protocols) - package or unit limit or weight-based limit, whichever higher; loss of the right to limit
  • Article 4 bis (Visby) - Himalaya protection for servants and agents, not independent contractors
  • Hamburg Article 4 - port to port; Article 5 - presumed fault, all measures reasonably required, no nautical fault exception; Article 19 notice; Article 20 two-year bar
  • Rotterdam Article 12 - receipt to delivery; Article 13(2) FIOS; Article 17 basis of liability; Article 62 two-year bar; Article 94 entry into force on twenty ratifications
  • Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, as amended 1993 - Hague-Visby in Indian law; Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 - transfer of rights of suit

Uncertainty and currency

Four limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed consolidated text of the Hague-Visby, Hamburg or Rotterdam Rules, nor of the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925; the Articles relied on rest on the conventions as adopted and on authoritative reproduction, and nothing is quoted verbatim at length. No ratification count is asserted for either the Rotterdam or the Hamburg Rules - those figures move, and what matters and is certain is the status: Rotterdam not in force, Hamburg in force but not adopted by the major maritime trading nations. And the printed facts do not say whether the bill was shipped or received-for-shipment, or whether the charter was on FIOST terms; both would materially change the analysis, so both are flagged rather than assumed.

One currency instruction for a later editor: the Indian statutes stated here are those in force at December 2024 - the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925 as amended in 1993, and the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856. India's carriage and bills of lading legislation was subsequently replaced, and the replacement statutes must not be read back into a December 2024 answer. This is the same discipline that keeps the Merchant Shipping Act 1958 rather than the 2025 Act throughout this paper.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 10 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) Why the duration of responsibility matters
  2. __________a) Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle
  3. __________a) Hamburg - port to port
  4. __________a) Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force
  5. __________a) The comparison, and the Indian position
  6. __________b) The grain claim - how it is framed
  7. __________b) The excepted perils that apply to grain
  8. __________b) The overriding obligations that can defeat every defence
  9. __________b) Procedural and quantum defences
  10. __________b) The evidence that makes the defence work
Flashcards 13

(a) 1 Why the duration matters. 2 Hague-Visby, tackle to tackle. 3 Hamburg, port to port. 4 Rotterdam, door to door and not in force. 5 The comparison and the Indian position. (b) 6 How the claim is framed. 7 The excepted perils. 8 The overriding obligations that defeat them. 9 Procedural and quantum defences. 10 The evidence needed.

Step 1 converts limb (a) from recitation into explanation, and step 8 is what stops limb (b) being a bare list of exceptions.

Because it decides which regime governs the loss, and therefore who bears it. Outside the period the Rules do not apply and the carrier may lawfully exclude liability by contract; inside the period Article III rule 8 makes any clause relieving the carrier below the Rules' standard null and void. The same damage produces different outcomes depending on when it occurred.

This one sentence is what the printed word 'explain' is asking for.

Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle, from when the goods are loaded on to when they are discharged from the ship, Article I(e). Hamburg - port to port, the whole time the carrier is in charge of the goods at the port of loading, during the carriage and at the port of discharge, Article 4. Rotterdam - door to door, from receipt of the goods for carriage to delivery, Article 12.

Three phrases and three Articles carry the core of limb (a).

Adopted in 2008 and opened for signature in 2009, but NOT IN FORCE. Article 94 requires twenty ratifications before entry into force and that threshold was not reached. They are the intended successor regime, not applicable law anywhere.

Writing about them in the present tense as governing law is the most visible error available on this question.

The Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, amended in 1993 to give effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, together with the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 for the transfer of rights of suit. India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules or the Rotterdam Rules, so the Indian period of responsibility is tackle to tackle.

An Indian examination expects the Indian position, and it must be the position as at the sitting.

Goods into the carrier's custody at the load port on day 1, loaded day 3, discharged day 20, collected day 25; damage on day 2 and day 22. Hague-Visby - neither loss is inside the period, so both fall to the contract terms and a 'before and after' clause usually defeats them. Hamburg - both are inside, and the carrier is liable unless it discharges the Article 5 burden. Rotterdam - both inside, and inland legs would be covered too, but the Rules are not in force.

One fact pattern giving three different answers is the most efficient way to earn the 'with examples' marks.

The consignee must show the goods were good in and bad out. A clean bill of lading is prima facie evidence under Article III rule 3 that the goods were shipped in apparent good order and condition, and conclusive evidence in the hands of a good-faith third-party transferee under the Visby amendment. Damaged out-turn then shifts the burden to the carrier to bring itself within a defence.

Understanding where the burden sits is what makes limb (b) a legal answer rather than an assertion of care.

Article IV rule 2(m) - wastage in bulk or weight, or any other loss or damage arising from inherent defect, quality or vice of the goods. Grain is hygroscopic and organic: loaded above its safe moisture content it will self-heat, sweat, cake, mould and discolour however carefully the ship is operated, and that damage is the working out of the cargo's own condition at shipment.

It also covers any shortage element through the wastage-in-bulk limb.

No. Article III rule 1 requires due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage to make the ship seaworthy and the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the goods, and Article IV rule 1 puts the burden of proving due diligence on the carrier. Article III rule 2 requires the cargo to be properly and carefully cared for. Leaking hatch covers or missing ventilation records will defeat the defence, because the damage is then attributed to the ship rather than to the cargo.

The conditionality is the point that separates a good answer from a list of exceptions.

Article IV rule 2(a) excepts neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or in the management of the ship, but not failures in the care of the cargo, which is Article III rule 2 territory. Ventilation of a grain cargo is the classic battleground: the owner argues the decision was ship management and excepted, the cargo interest argues it was cargo care and not.

It has generated more litigation than any other provision in the Rules, and flagging the contest shows real understanding.

Notice of loss in writing before or at the time of removal, or within three days where the damage is not apparent - absence of notice is prima facie evidence of delivery as per the bill. A one-year time bar from delivery or from when the goods should have been delivered, after which the carrier and ship are discharged from all liability. And limitation under Article IV rule 5 - the package or unit limit or the weight-based limit, whichever is higher.

The one-year time bar is often the most effective defence of all and is regularly omitted.

The weight-based limit. Article IV rule 5 as amended gives the package or unit limit or the weight-based limit, whichever is higher - and a bulk cargo has no packages or units, so only the weight limb can operate.

Quoting a per-package figure for a bulk cargo shows the facts were not read.

The moisture content and temperature of the grain recorded at loading; the hatch cover weathertightness test record, ultrasonic or hose test; and the complete ventilation records showing the rule applied and the dew point readings behind each decision. Supported by hold inspection and cleanliness certificates, mate's receipts and any clausing, hold temperature logs, bilge soundings, weather records and draught surveys.

A defence of inherent vice is only as good as the record supporting it.

Quick revision
15-second recall
WHY THE PERIOD MATTERS: it decides which regime applies and so who bears the loss - outside it the carrier may lawfully exclude liability; inside it Art III r 8 makes any derogating clause null and void. Each convention extends the period because the last left a gap. HAGUE / HAGUE-VISBY - TACKLE TO TACKLE, Art I(e): from when goods are loaded on to when discharged from the ship. Only where a bill of lading is issued (I(b)); excludes live animals and deck cargo (I(c)). Gaps before loading and after discharge - carriers contract out by 'before and after' clauses. HAMBURG - PORT TO PORT, Art 4: the whole time the carrier is in charge at the load port, during carriage, and at the discharge port. Art 5 - presumed fault: liable unless it proves all measures reasonably required were taken; no nautical fault exception. Art 20 - two-year bar. ROTTERDAM - DOOR TO DOOR, Art 12: from receipt to delivery, not tied to ports, covering inland legs; Art 13(2) FIOS. ADOPTED 2008 - NOT IN FORCE; Art 94 needs twenty ratifications. Time bars: 1 / 2 / 2 years. INDIA at this sitting: Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925 as amended 1993 = Hague-Visby = TACKLE TO TACKLE, with the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 for title to sue; India is NOT party to Hamburg or Rotterdam. (b) GRAIN CLAIM. Consignee proves good in, bad out - a clean bill is prima facie evidence (III r 3), conclusive against a good-faith transferee under Visby; burden shifts to the carrier. DEFENCES - Art IV r 2: (m) INHERENT DEFECT, QUALITY OR VICE and wastage in bulk or weight - the grain defence, since grain is hygroscopic and self-heats, sweats, cakes and moulds if loaded above safe moisture; (i) act or omission of the shipper; (n) insufficiency of packing; (c) perils of the sea; (a) neglect in navigation or management of the ship - but NOT care of cargo, and ventilation is the battleground; (p) latent defect; (b) fire without actual fault or privity; (q) catch-all, burden on the carrier. BUT TWO OVERRIDING DUTIES: Art III r 1 due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage to make the ship seaworthy and the holds fit and safe for reception, carriage and preservation - cargoworthiness; and Art IV r 1 puts the burden of proving due diligence ON THE CARRIER. Art III r 2 - properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge. So inherent vice succeeds only if the ship was cargoworthy and the cargo properly ventilated and cared for. PROCEDURAL/QUANTUM: notice before or at removal, or within three days if not apparent; ONE-YEAR time bar (III r 6); Art IV r 5 package/unit or weight-based limit, whichever higher - for bulk grain there are no packages, so the WEIGHT limit applies; limit lost only on intent or recklessness with knowledge. Art 4 bis Himalaya protection for servants and agents. EVIDENCE: hold inspection, hatch cover weathertightness test, moisture content and temperature at loading, mate's receipts and clausing, ventilation records with dew points, hold temperatures, bilge soundings, weather records, draught surveys, discharge-port survey, notify the P&I Club.

Critical numbers
  • Hague-Visby Article I(e) - loaded on to discharged from the ship
  • Hamburg Article 4 - in charge at load port, during carriage, at discharge port
  • Rotterdam Article 12 - receipt to delivery; Article 94 - twenty ratifications for entry into force
  • Hague-Visby time bar - ONE year (Article III rule 6)
  • Hamburg time bar - TWO years (Article 20); Rotterdam - TWO years (Article 62)
  • Notice of non-apparent damage - within THREE days (Article III rule 6)
  • Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, amended 1993 for Hague-Visby
  • Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 - transfer of rights of suit
  • Article IV rule 2(m) - inherent defect, quality or vice, and wastage in bulk or weight
  • Article IV rule 5 - package or unit limit or weight-based limit, whichever is higher
Critical regulation
Hague-Visby Article I(e) defines 'carriage of goods' as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time when they are discharged from the ship - the tackle-to-tackle period; read with Article III rule 8, under which any clause relieving the carrier from liability otherwise than as provided in the Rules is null and void, so that the period determines both which regime applies and whether an exclusion clause is effective.
Major trap
Describing the Rotterdam Rules as if they were in force. They were adopted in 2008 and require twenty ratifications under Article 94, which had not been achieved at this sitting - so they are a proposed regime, not applicable law. In limb (b) the corresponding trap is treating inherent vice as an unconditional defence: under Article IV rule 1 the burden of proving due diligence is on the carrier, so the defence fails if the holds were not fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the grain, or if the cargo was not properly ventilated and cared for under Article III rule 2. Two smaller but visible errors: quoting a per-package limitation for a bulk cargo that has no packages, and omitting the Indian statutory position in an Indian examination.

Production metadata (review build only)
Reuse tier C · Verification Verified against the conventions and the Indian implementing statutes as at the December 2024 sitting. The Hague Rules 1924 and the Hague-Visby Rules (the 1968 Visby Protocol and the 1979 SDR Protocol) were used for the definition of 'carriage of goods' as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship (Article I(e)); the carrier's obligations of due diligence to make the ship seaworthy before and at the beginning of the voyage and properly and carefully to load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge the goods (Article III rules 1 and 2); the catalogue of exceptions (Article IV rule 2(a) to (q)) including the nautical fault and fire exceptions, perils of the sea, inherent defect quality or vice of the goods, insufficiency of packing, latent defects not discoverable by due diligence, and the catch-all at (q) with its burden of proof on the carrier; the package or unit limitation as amended by the Visby and SDR Protocols; and the one-year time bar under Article III rule 6. The Hamburg Rules 1978 were used for the port-to-port period of responsibility (Article 4) and the presumed-fault basis of liability (Article 5). The Rotterdam Rules 2008 were used for the door-to-door period (Articles 11 and 12) and are recorded as ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE at this sitting. The Indian position at this sitting was established as: the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, amended in 1993 to give effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, together with the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856; India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules or the Rotterdam Rules. No post-2024 Indian carriage legislation appears anywhere in this object.

Sources
  • PRIMARY: International Convention for the Unification of Certain Rules of Law relating to Bills of Lading, Brussels 1924 (the Hague Rules), Article I(e) - 'carriage of goods' covers the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article I(b) - 'contract of carriage' applies only to contracts covered by a bill of lading or similar document of title; Article I(c) - 'goods' excludes live animals and deck cargo stated as carried on deck and so carried
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article II - the carrier's responsibilities and liabilities, and rights and immunities, in relation to the loading, handling, stowage, carriage, custody, care and discharge of goods
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article III rule 1 - the carrier shall be bound, before and at the beginning of the voyage, to exercise due diligence to make the ship seaworthy, properly man, equip and supply the ship, and make the holds and all other parts in which goods are carried fit and safe for their reception, carriage and preservation
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article III rule 2 - the carrier shall properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge the goods carried
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article III rules 3 and 4 - the bill of lading as prima facie evidence of receipt of the goods as described; conclusive evidence in the hands of a third party transferee under the Visby amendment
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article III rule 6 - notice of loss or damage in writing before or at the time of removal, or within three days where the loss is not apparent; and discharge from liability unless suit is brought within ONE YEAR of delivery or of the date when the goods should have been delivered
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article III rule 8 - any clause relieving the carrier from liability otherwise than as provided in the Rules is null and void
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article IV rule 1 - neither the carrier nor the ship is liable for loss or damage arising from unseaworthiness unless caused by want of due diligence; the burden of proving the exercise of due diligence is on the carrier
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article IV rule 2(a) to (q) - the catalogue of excepted perils, including act, neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or management of the ship; fire unless caused by the actual fault or privity of the carrier; perils, dangers and accidents of the sea; act of God; act of war; act of public enemies; arrest or restraint; quarantine restrictions; act or omission of the shipper or owner of the goods; strikes or lockouts; riots and civil commotions; saving or attempting to save life or property at sea; WASTAGE IN BULK OR WEIGHT OR ANY OTHER LOSS OR DAMAGE ARISING FROM INHERENT DEFECT, QUALITY OR VICE OF THE GOODS; INSUFFICIENCY OF PACKING; insufficiency or inadequacy of marks; latent defects not discoverable by due diligence; and (q) any other cause arising without the actual fault or privity of the carrier and without the fault or neglect of its agents or servants, the burden of proof being on the person claiming the benefit of the exception
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article IV rule 4 - deviation in saving or attempting to save life or property at sea, or any reasonable deviation, is not an infringement of the Rules
  • PRIMARY: Hague Rules Article IV rule 5, as amended by the 1968 Visby Protocol and the 1979 SDR Protocol - the package or unit limitation, and the alternative weight-based limit; the container clause treating enumerated packages in a container as separate packages; and the loss of the right to limit where damage results from an act or omission done with intent to cause damage or recklessly and with knowledge that damage would probably result
  • PRIMARY: Visby Protocol 1968 Article 4 bis - the Himalaya provision extending the carrier's defences and limits to servants and agents, but not to independent contractors
  • PRIMARY: United Nations Convention on the Carriage of Goods by Sea, Hamburg 1978 (the Hamburg Rules), Article 4 - the period of responsibility covers the goods for the whole time the carrier is in charge of them AT THE PORT OF LOADING, DURING THE CARRIAGE AND AT THE PORT OF DISCHARGE, with detailed provisions on when the carrier is deemed to have taken over and delivered the goods
  • PRIMARY: Hamburg Rules Article 5 - basis of liability: the carrier is liable if the occurrence which caused the loss, damage or delay took place while the goods were in its charge, UNLESS it proves that it, its servants and agents took all measures that could reasonably be required to avoid the occurrence and its consequences - a presumed-fault regime with no nautical fault exception; and Article 5(4) on fire
  • PRIMARY: Hamburg Rules Article 6 - limits of liability by package or shipping unit or by weight; Article 19 - notice of loss and the extended notice periods; Article 20 - a TWO-YEAR time bar
  • PRIMARY: United Nations Convention on Contracts for the International Carriage of Goods Wholly or Partly by Sea, 2008 (the Rotterdam Rules), Article 12 - the period of responsibility begins when the carrier or a performing party RECEIVES the goods for carriage and ends when the goods are DELIVERED; Article 11 - carriage and delivery of the goods; and Article 13 with the FIOS provision at Article 13(2)
  • PRIMARY: Rotterdam Rules Article 17 - basis of liability; Article 62 - a two-year time bar; and Article 94 - entry into force twelve months after twenty ratifications, which had NOT been achieved at this sitting
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925, as amended in 1993 to give effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, being the statute in force at this sitting; and the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856
  • AUTHORITATIVE SECONDARY: India is a party to the Hague-Visby regime through the 1925 Act as amended, and is NOT a party to the Hamburg Rules or the Rotterdam Rules
  • INDUSTRY GUIDANCE: the standard cargo-claim evidence chain for bulk grain - draught surveys and shore figures, hold inspection and hatch cover weathertightness testing, ventilation records, moisture content and temperature records, the pre-loading survey certificate and the mate's receipts and any clausing

Understand this first

This question hands you its own structure, which is why it is easier to answer adequately than to answer well. The four steps are printed in the stem, so describing them earns only part of the marks; the printed command asks for the CRITICAL ROLE of each, WITH EXAMPLES. So the real task is to show that the four steps are a closed loop in which each stage is load-bearing - plan without monitoring and you cannot prove a saving; monitor without self-evaluation and the cycle never closes. Two framing facts separate a current answer from a stale one: the four steps are the framework of PART I of the SEEMP, not of the whole plan, and the guidelines that describe them were re-adopted as MEPC.395(82) on 4 October 2024, two months before this examination, revoking the 2022 edition.

Knowledge map
SEEMP - The Four-Step Energy Efficiency Cycle
  1. framing
    1. 1What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live
      • required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - NOT regulation 22, renumbered by MEPC.328(76) from 1 November 2022
      • ship-specific, operational, kept on board, developed taking account of the Organization's guidelines
      • operative guidelines at this sitting - MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, revoking MEPC.346(78)
  2. main
    1. 2Step 1 - Planning
      • 4.1.1 - the MOST CRUCIAL stage; devote sufficient time to it
      • determines current status of energy use and carbon intensity, and the improvement achievable
      • ship-specific measures - identify what has been done, how effective, what more can be adopted
    2. 3Step 2 - Implementation
      • 4.2.1 - establish a SYSTEM: procedures, defined tasks, assigned responsible personnel
      • defined levels of authority and lines of communication; internal audit and management review where relevant
      • state how each measure is implemented, who is responsible, and start and end dates
    3. 4Step 3 - Monitoring
      • 4.3.1 - monitor QUANTITATIVELY by an established method, preferably an international standard
      • tool should target the goal indicator - AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII
      • a ship subject to regulation 28 is likely to use the CII itself
    4. 5Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement
      • 4.4.1 - the FINAL phase; produces feedback for the planning stage of the NEXT cycle
      • 4.4.2.1 - evaluate effectiveness of the measures and of their implementation
      • 4.4.2.2 - deepen understanding of what works, how and why
    5. 6Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative
      • planning without monitoring - savings cannot be claimed against any baseline
      • implementation without assignment - the plan is carried for inspection, not used
      • monitoring without consistency - effectiveness becomes opinion and weather cannot be separated from operation
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

framing
  1. What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live
main
  1. Step 1 - Planning
  2. Step 2 - Implementation
  3. Step 3 - Monitoring
  4. Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement
  5. Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative

Memory cue. PIMS - Plan, Implement, Monitor, Self-evaluate. It is Plan-Do-Check-Act in IMO clothes. Inside planning: SHIP, COMPANY, PEOPLE, GOAL (2). Implementation is who, what, by when (3). Monitoring is quantitative, continuous, shore-side (4). Self-evaluation asks did it work, why, what trend, what next (5). Then close with break one link and the loop fails (6).

Remember6 route headings
Cover46 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 46 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover

Model written answer

1. What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live

The Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan (SEEMP) is required by regulation 26 of MARPOL Annex VI. It is a ship-specific, operational management document that must be kept on board, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization. The guidelines operative at this sitting are the 2024 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted on 4 October 2024, which revoke the 2022 Guidelines, MEPC.346(78).

Note the numbering. The SEEMP requirement sits at regulation 26, not regulation 22 - Annex VI chapter 4 was renumbered by MEPC.328(76) with effect from 1 November 2022. Within chapter 4 the goal is stated at regulation 20, the fuel oil data collection system at regulation 27 and the operational carbon intensity indicator at regulation 28.

The plan has three Parts, and it matters for this question that the four steps belong to Part I:

  • Part I - Ship Management Plan to Improve Energy Efficiency, required by regulation 26.1, applying to any ship of 400 GT and above. This is the part built on the four-step cycle.
  • Part II - Ship Fuel Oil Consumption Data Collection Plan, required by regulation 26.2, applying to any ship of 5,000 GT and above. It describes the methodology for collecting the data required by regulation 27 and reporting it to the Administration.
  • Part III - Ship Operational Carbon Intensity Plan, required by regulations 26.3 and 28.8. It carries the attained annual operational CII methodology, the required CII for the next three years, a three-year implementation plan, and its own process for self-evaluation and improvement.

Section 3.6 of the Guidelines states the cycle directly: Part I should be developed as a ship-specific plan by the company and should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency and reduce carbon intensity through four steps - planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement - and "these components play a critical role in the continuous cycle". Section 3.7 adds the standing qualification that safety considerations are paramount at all times.

2. Step 1 - Planning

The Guidelines are unusually emphatic here. Section 4.1.1: planning is the most crucial stage, because it determines both the ship's current energy and carbon-intensity status and the improvement that can be expected - so sufficient time should be devoted to it. Planning has four components:

  1. Ship-specific measures. Establish what the ship actually does now, identify measures already taken and how effective they have been, and identify what more can be adopted. The Guidelines warn that not all measures suit all ships, or the same ship under different operating conditions, and that some are mutually exclusive.
  2. Company-specific measures. Efficiency does not depend on single-ship management alone: repair yards, owners, operators, charterers, cargo owners, fuel suppliers, ports and traffic management all bear on it, so a company-level plan and coordination is recommended.
  3. Human resource development. Awareness-raising and training for personnel ashore and on board - the Guidelines call this both a planning component and a critical element of implementation.
  4. Goal setting. For ships subject to regulation 28 the goal should be consistent with the continuous CII improvement required by that regulation. Whatever is chosen, the goal should be measurable and easy to understand.

Examples. Ship-specific: speed optimisation; weather routeing; hull and propeller condition management with a coating and polishing regime; trim optimisation; confirming berth availability and arrival time with the destination port so the ship is not steaming hard to wait at anchor. Company-specific: negotiating "just-in-time" arrival with terminals, which needs early communication between operator, port and traffic management and cannot be delivered from the bridge alone. Mutually exclusive: a fixed-pitch propeller re-pitched for a lower service speed rules out later gains from speed increase; slow steaming and main-engine derating interact with waste-heat recovery output. Goal: "reduce AER by 5% year on year, consistent with the required CII for the next three years" - measurable, and tied to the regulation.

3. Step 2 - Implementation

Section 4.2.1: once the measures are identified it is essential to establish a system for carrying them out. That means developing procedures for energy management, defining the tasks associated with them and assigning those tasks to responsible personnel, with defined levels of authority and lines of communication, and procedures for internal audit and management review where relevant. Part I should say how each measure is to be implemented, who is responsible, and the start and end dates of each. The Guidelines note that building this system can be treated as part of planning.

Section 4.2.2 adds record-keeping, and two points that are easy marks because they are counter-intuitive:

  • if an identified measure cannot be implemented, the reason should be recorded for internal use - a plan that quietly drops a measure has lost the evidence its next self-evaluation needs;
  • events and operational conditions outside the crew's control - waiting for berths, extended port dwell times, operation in severe adverse weather - should be documented, because they affect the ship's rating and cannot be reconstructed later.

Examples. The Chief Engineer is named as responsible for the hull and propeller condition regime, with an inspection at each dry-docking and in-water polishing at defined fouling thresholds; the Master is named for trim and speed compliance against the passage plan; the shore superintendent for retrofit scheduling. A measure such as a shaft generator retrofit that is deferred because the yard slot moved is recorded, with the reason, so that next year's evaluation does not read it as a failure of the measure itself.

4. Step 3 - Monitoring

Section 4.3.1: energy efficiency should be monitored quantitatively, by an established method and preferably by an international standard. The monitoring tool should target the goal indicator set at planning - AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII - and a ship subject to regulation 28 is likely to use the CII itself, calculated in accordance with the Guidelines developed by the Organization.

  • 4.3.4 - monitoring at regular intervals for consistency of data and to assist verification; fuel oil consumption monitored using daily reporting, such as noon reports, or at higher frequency.
  • 4.3.5 - continuous, consistent and reliable data collection is the foundation of monitoring, so a monitoring system with data-collection procedures and assigned personnel must be developed - again, at the planning stage.
  • 4.3.6 - to avoid unnecessary administrative burden, monitoring should be done as far as possible by shore staff where data can be transferred automatically, using existing required records such as the official and engineering logbooks and the oil record book.
  • 4.3.7 - where a ship diverts to engage in search and rescue, that data should not be used in energy efficiency monitoring and should be recorded separately.

Examples. Noon reports feeding an automated performance system that separates hull-fouling degradation from weather effect; a shaft power meter and mass flow meters giving fuel consumption independent of tank soundings; the CII tracked monthly against the required value so the corrective action is taken in month seven, not discovered in the annual return.

5. Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement

Section 4.4.1: this is the final phase of the management cycle, and its purpose is to produce meaningful feedback for the planning stage of the next cycle. Section 4.4.2 gives four purposes:

  1. evaluate the effectiveness of the planned measures and of their implementation;
  2. deepen understanding of the ship's operation - what types of measure do and do not function effectively, and how and why;
  3. comprehend the trend of efficiency improvement for that ship; and
  4. develop the improved plan for the next cycle by identifying further opportunities.

Note the second and third. The Guidelines do not ask only "did it work?" but "why did it work, and what is the trend" - which is what converts a year's data into a better plan rather than a score.

Examples. Evaluation shows weather routeing delivered most of the claimed saving on North Atlantic legs but almost none on short coastal legs, so the next cycle applies it selectively and reinvests the effort in trim optimisation. Hull coating performance is found to degrade faster on a trade with long idle periods in warm water, so the next cycle shortens the polishing interval rather than blaming the coating. For a ship subject to regulation 28, Part III carries this further: where the ship is rated D for three consecutive years or E in a single year, a plan of corrective actions must be developed and included in the SEEMP - self-evaluation with a statutory consequence.

6. Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative

The question asks for the critical role of each component, so the answer must say why removing any one of them breaks the whole:

StepWhat it contributesWhat happens without it
PlanningEstablishes the baseline and selects measures that suit this ship and this tradeMeasures are adopted by fashion rather than fit; savings cannot be claimed because there is no baseline to claim them against
ImplementationConverts intention into assigned, dated, auditable actionThe plan becomes a document carried for inspection rather than a way the ship is run
MonitoringSupplies the quantitative evidence, consistently and continuouslyEffectiveness is a matter of opinion; the CII return is unverifiable and adverse weather cannot be distinguished from poor operation
Self-evaluationTurns the data into a better plan and explains why measures workedThe cycle does not close - the same plan is re-issued each year and improvement stops

The cycle is also what makes the plan ship-specific and living rather than generic: section 3.6 notes that with each iteration some elements of Part I will necessarily change while others may remain. And it is what connects an operational management document to a regulatory outcome - the same four beats reappear in Part III, where planning becomes the required CII for the next three years and the three-year implementation plan, monitoring becomes the attained annual operational CII, and self-evaluation becomes the plan of corrective actions.

Finally, note what the SEEMP is not. It is operational, not technical: the EEDI and EEXI fix what the ship is, once. The SEEMP governs how it is operated, continuously - which is why it is a cycle and they are not.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, one limb, and the question names its own four sections. That is a gift and a trap. The gift is that the structure is handed to you. The trap is that four equal paragraphs of description will not score sixteen marks, because the printed command is "describe with suitable examples, the critical role of each of these components" - three separate demands. You must (i) describe each step, (ii) give examples, and (iii) say why each is critical. An answer that does only the first has answered roughly a third of the question.

The route therefore spends one step per component, attaches examples inside each, and then adds a dedicated step on criticality - the table at step 6 - which is where the "critical role" marks are actually banked. Framing at step 1 is worth the space because it lets you say something most candidates cannot: that the four steps are the framework of Part I specifically, not of the whole plan.

Which guidelines - and why this sitting is different from earlier 2024 sittings

The SEEMP Guidelines are re-adopted periodically and each edition revokes the last. At this examination the operative edition is MEPC.395(82), the 2024 Guidelines, adopted 4 October 2024 - only two months before the paper was sat - which revoked MEPC.346(78), the 2022 Guidelines.

That is a real discriminator between sittings of the same year. A candidate revising from a January, February or March 2024 paper would cite MEPC.346(78), and by December that citation is superseded. The intermediate step is worth knowing because it shows the pace: MEPC.388(81) amended the 2022 Guidelines at MEPC 81 in the spring, and MEPC.395(82) then replaced them outright in the autumn. Two changes inside one calendar year, on a paper the intake had flagged as carrying no temporal risk at all.

The regulation number is the other easy mark to lose

A great deal of older material - textbooks, circulars and notes written before 2023 - cites the SEEMP as MARPOL Annex VI regulation 22. That was correct until chapter 4 was renumbered by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Since then the SEEMP is regulation 26, with Part I at 26.1, Part II at 26.2 and Part III at 26.3 (read with 28.8). Getting this right, and being able to say why the older number exists, is a visible sign of current reading.

The neighbouring numbers are worth carrying too: regulation 20 the goal of chapter 4, regulation 27 the data collection system, regulation 28 the CII.

Where the paper's decarbonisation position stood in December 2024

If the answer reaches for context, it must be the context of this sitting. EEXI and CII entered into force on 1 January 2023, so at this examination 2023 is the first full CII data year and the first ratings have been issued during 2024 on that data. The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, resolution MEPC.377(80) of 7 July 2023, is the operative strategy. And MEPC 82, held 30 September to 4 October 2024, began the review of the short-term measures - CII, SEEMP and EEXI - referring it to a correspondence group and an intersessional working group reporting to MEPC 83 in 2025. That review had produced no amendment at this sitting. Do not write that the short-term measures have been revised, and do not import any mid-term measure as adopted.

The five facts from section 4 that most answers miss

  • Planning is expressly the "most crucial stage" (4.1.1). Say so and attribute it - it converts a generic opening into a cited one.
  • Some measures are mutually exclusive (4.1.3). Almost nobody says this, and it demonstrates real engineering understanding rather than a list of green measures.
  • Reasons for non-implementation must be recorded (4.2.2). Counter-intuitive, and precise.
  • Conditions outside the crew's control should be documented (4.2.2) - waiting for berths, port dwell, severe weather - because they affect the rating.
  • Search and rescue data is excluded and recorded separately (4.3.7). A small, exact, memorable point that proves the Guidelines were opened.

Common mistakes

  • Citing the SEEMP as regulation 22. It has been regulation 26 since 1 November 2022.
  • Citing MEPC.346(78) as the operative guidelines. They were revoked by MEPC.395(82) on 4 October 2024, before this sitting.
  • Presenting the four steps as the structure of the whole SEEMP. They are the framework of Part I.
  • Describing the steps without giving examples, when the question prints "with suitable examples".
  • Describing the steps without saying why each is critical, when the question prints "the critical role of each".
  • Confusing the SEEMP with the EEDI or EEXI. Those are technical and one-off; the SEEMP is operational and continuous.
  • Writing that Part I applies to ships of 5,000 GT and above. That is Part II; Part I is 400 GT and above.
  • Treating monitoring as something done only for the annual return. The Guidelines require regular intervals and daily fuel reporting.
  • Asserting that MEPC 82 revised the CII. It opened a review; no amendment existed at this sitting.

Examiner traps

  • "Whose plan is it?" Part I is developed as a ship-specific plan by the company (3.6) - not written on board, though it is implemented on board.
  • "Is the SEEMP audited?" For ships not subject to regulation 28 the Guidelines say expressly there is no requirement to define a goal, publish it, or be subject to external inspection, survey or audit in respect of the SEEMP (4.1.7.2). For ships subject to regulation 28 the position is different, because Part III is verified. Knowing that the answer splits is worth a mark.
  • "What happens if the ship rates badly?" D for three consecutive years, or E in one year, triggers a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP.
  • "Why must adverse weather be recorded?" Because it affects the rating and cannot be reconstructed after the fact - 4.2.2.
  • "Where does safety come in?" Section 3.7 - safety considerations are paramount at all times, and the trade may determine which measures are feasible.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which regulation requires the SEEMP, and which guidelines are current? Regulation 26 of MARPOL Annex VI; the 2024 Guidelines, MEPC.395(82).
  • What are the three Parts and their thresholds? Part I 400 GT and above; Part II 5,000 GT and above; Part III with the CII regime under regulations 26.3 and 28.8.
  • Name the four steps. Planning, implementation, monitoring, self-evaluation and improvement.
  • Which step do the Guidelines call the most crucial, and why? Planning - it fixes the baseline and the achievable improvement.
  • Give two ship-specific and one company-specific measure. Speed optimisation and hull condition management; just-in-time arrival negotiated with the terminal.
  • What must be recorded when a measure is not implemented? The reason, for internal use.
  • What data is excluded from energy efficiency monitoring? Data from search and rescue diversions, recorded separately.
  • How does Part III mirror the cycle? Required CII and three-year implementation plan; attained annual CII; plan of corrective actions.

Memory framework

PIMS - Plan, Implement, Monitor, Self-evaluate. It is a Plan-Do-Check-Act cycle wearing IMO clothes, which is why section 3.3 says a company already running ISO 14001 should treat efficiency monitoring as part of its existing management system, and section 3.4 allows Part I to form part of the safety management system.

For the four sub-parts of planning: SHIP, COMPANY, PEOPLE, GOAL - ship-specific measures, company-specific measures, human resource development, goal setting. For the four purposes of self-evaluation: did it work, why, what is the trend, what next.

Regulation and source map

  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - the SEEMP. 26.1 Part I, 26.2 Part II, 26.3 Part III
  • Regulation 20 goal of chapter 4; regulation 27 data collection system; regulation 28 operational CII, with 28.8 for Part III
  • MEPC.328(76) - the 2021 revised Annex VI; renumbered the SEEMP from regulation 22 to 26 with effect from 1 November 2022
  • MEPC.395(82) - 2024 SEEMP Guidelines, adopted 4 October 2024; revokes MEPC.346(78). The operative edition at this sitting
  • MEPC.388(81) - amended the 2022 Guidelines at MEPC 81, before MEPC.395(82) replaced them
  • MEPC.395(82) section 3.6 - the four steps; 3.7 safety paramount
  • Section 4.1 planning (4.1.1 most crucial stage; 4.1.7 goal setting); 4.2 implementation; 4.3 monitoring; 4.4 self-evaluation and improvement
  • Sections 9 to 15 - Part III, including 13 self-evaluation and 15 plan of corrective actions
  • MEPC.352(78) CII Guidelines G1; MEPC.355(78) correction factors and voyage adjustments G5
  • MEPC.377(80) - 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023; the operative strategy at this sitting

Uncertainty and currency

The four steps, their content and every section number in this answer are taken from MEPC.395(82) read directly. Three limitations are recorded. MIW holds no licensed consolidated MARPOL Annex VI, so the regulation numbering and content are established from the recitals and cross-references inside MEPC.395(82) rather than from a licensed Annex VI text, and no regulation is quoted verbatim. No CII rating statistic is asserted - the first ratings were issued during 2024 on 2023 data, but no published distribution was verified for this sitting. And the MEPC 82 review of the short-term measures is recorded as in progress with no outcome, because that is its status at this sitting.

One currency instruction for any later editor: MEPC.395(82) is the correct edition here and must not be advanced. The guidelines are re-adopted periodically, and the review opened at MEPC 82 reported to MEPC 83 in 2025 - after this examination. Anything arising from that review belongs to a later paper, not this one.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.
