  5. __________main Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement
  6. __________main Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative
Flashcards 12

1 What the SEEMP is and where the four steps live. 2 Planning. 3 Implementation. 4 Monitoring. 5 Self-evaluation and improvement. 6 Why the cycle is critical rather than administrative.

Step 6 is where the 'critical role' marks are banked; steps 2 to 5 carry the examples inside them.

MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26. The operative guidelines are the 2024 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, which revoke the 2022 Guidelines MEPC.346(78).

Two citation traps in one card - the old regulation 22 numbering and the superseded 2022 guidelines.

Because that was the numbering before MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 was renumbered by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Since then the SEEMP is regulation 26, with Part I at 26.1, Part II at 26.2 and Part III at 26.3 read with 28.8.

Pre-2023 textbooks and notes are still in circulation and all carry the old number.

Part I, Ship Management Plan to Improve Energy Efficiency - any ship of 400 GT and above. Part II, Ship Fuel Oil Consumption Data Collection Plan - any ship of 5,000 GT and above. Part III, Ship Operational Carbon Intensity Plan - required by regulations 26.3 and 28.8 for ships subject to the CII.

The four steps belong to Part I only, and the two tonnage thresholds are commonly transposed.

Section 4.1.1 calls it the most crucial stage, because it determines both the current status of energy use and carbon intensity and the improvement that can be expected. It has four components: ship-specific measures, company-specific measures, human resource development, and goal setting - the goal to be measurable and easy to understand.

Attributing 'most crucial stage' to the Guidelines converts a generic opening into a cited one.

That not all measures can be applied to all ships, or even to the same ship under different operating conditions, and that some of them are mutually exclusive.

Almost no candidate says this, and it demonstrates engineering judgement rather than a list of green measures.

Procedures for energy management; defined tasks assigned to responsible personnel; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for internal audit and management review where relevant; and, for each measure, how it is to be implemented, who is responsible and the start and end dates.

'Who, what, by when' is the whole of step 2 and is what makes the plan auditable.

First, if an identified measure cannot be implemented, the reason should be recorded for internal use. Second, events and operational conditions outside the crew's control - waiting for berths, extended port dwell times, operation in severe adverse weather - should be documented, because they may affect the ship's rating.

Both are counter-intuitive, both are precise, and the second links implementation to the CII outcome.

Quantitative monitoring by an established method, preferably an international standard, using a tool targeting the goal indicator - AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII; at regular intervals, with fuel oil consumption monitored by daily reporting such as noon reports; supported by a monitoring system established at the planning stage and carried out as far as possible by shore staff. Data obtained during a search and rescue diversion is excluded and recorded separately.

The SAR exclusion is small, exact and memorable, and proves the Guidelines were opened.

To evaluate the effectiveness of the planned measures and their implementation; to deepen understanding of the ship's operation - what works, how and why; to comprehend the trend of efficiency improvement; and to develop the improved management plan for the next cycle.

The middle two are what convert data into a better plan rather than a score.

EEDI and EEXI are technical indices fixing what the ship is - design efficiency, assessed once. The SEEMP is an operational management plan governing how the ship is run, continuously, through a repeating four-step cycle.

It explains why the SEEMP is a cycle and the indices are not.

MEPC 82, held 30 September to 4 October 2024, OPENED a review of the short-term GHG measures - CII, SEEMP and EEXI - and referred it to a correspondence group and an intersessional working group reporting to MEPC 83 in 2025. No amendment existed at this examination.

Material written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting carries the outcome of that review; back-reading it into a December 2024 answer is future contamination.

Quick revision
15-second recall
SEEMP = MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 (NOT reg 22 - renumbered by MEPC.328(76) from 1 Nov 2022). Ship-specific, operational, kept on board. Guidelines at this sitting: MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024, revoking MEPC.346(78) (which MEPC.388(81) had amended earlier in 2024). Chapter 4: reg 20 goal, reg 27 data collection, reg 28 CII. Three Parts: Part I (reg 26.1, 400 GT+) - the four-step cycle; Part II (reg 26.2, 5,000 GT+) - fuel oil data collection plan; Part III (regs 26.3 and 28.8) - operational carbon intensity plan. Section 3.6 states the four steps and calls them critical to the continuous cycle; 3.7 safety paramount. 1 PLANNING (4.1) - the MOST CRUCIAL stage; ship-specific measures (some are mutually exclusive), company-specific measures, human resource development, goal setting - measurable and easy to understand, consistent with continuous CII improvement. 2 IMPLEMENTATION (4.2) - establish a system: procedures, defined tasks, responsible personnel, levels of authority, lines of communication, start and end dates; record-keeping; record the reason if a measure cannot be implemented; document conditions outside the crew's control (berth waiting, port dwell, severe weather) because they affect the rating. 3 MONITORING (4.3) - quantitative, by an established method; tool targets the goal indicator (AER, cgDIST, EEOI, CII); regular intervals, fuel by noon reports or better; continuous consistent reliable data is the foundation; do it shore-side where possible using logbooks and the oil record book; search and rescue data excluded and recorded separately. 4 SELF-EVALUATION (4.4) - final phase, feeds the next planning stage; four purposes: evaluate effectiveness, deepen understanding of what works and why, comprehend the trend, develop the improved plan. Why critical: no baseline means no provable saving; no assignment means a document not a practice; no consistent data means opinion; no evaluation means the loop never closes. Part III mirrors it - required CII and three-year implementation plan, attained annual CII, and a plan of corrective actions if rated D three years running or E once. Contrast: EEDI/EEXI are technical and one-off; the SEEMP is operational and continuous.

  • MEPC.395(82), adopted 4 October 2024 - the operative guidelines at this sitting
  • MEPC.346(78) - the 2022 Guidelines, revoked by MEPC.395(82)
  • MEPC.328(76) - renumbered the SEEMP from regulation 22 to 26, effective 1 November 2022
  • Part I applies to ships of 400 GT and above
  • Part II applies to ships of 5,000 GT and above
  • CII rating D for three consecutive years, or E in one year, triggers a plan of corrective actions
  • MEPC.377(80), 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023
Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 requires each ship to keep on board a Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization - which at this sitting are the 2024 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, revoking the 2022 Guidelines MEPC.346(78). Section 3.6 of those Guidelines states that Part I should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency through four steps: planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement.
Major trap
Answering the question as a description of four steps when the printed command asks for the CRITICAL ROLE of each, WITH SUITABLE EXAMPLES - three demands, of which description is only one. The citation traps run alongside it: the SEEMP is MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26, not regulation 22 (renumbered by MEPC.328(76) from 1 November 2022); and the operative guidelines at this sitting are MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, not MEPC.346(78), which they revoked two months before this paper was sat. Finally, the four steps are the framework of Part I only.

Also on the platform: QP2412 Q1 - the Sustainable Development Goals, where SDG 13 is the climate limb this question implements · QP2412 Q4 - the harmonized survey system under which the SEEMP is verified · QP2412 Q5 - self-evaluation and continuous improvement as a management discipline

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Operative paragraph 3 revokes MEPC.346(78), the 2022 Guidelines. The resolution's own preamble records the intermediate step: MEPC.388(81) amended the 2022 Guidelines at MEPC 81 before MEPC.395(82) replaced them outright. The regulation number was verified from the resolution text rather than from recall: the SEEMP is required by MARPOL Annex VI REGULATION 26 - Part I by regulation 26.1, Part II by 26.2, Part III by 26.3 and 28.8 - not by regulation 22, which is the pre-2021 numbering superseded by MEPC.328(76) with effect from 1 November 2022. Chapter 4's goal is at regulation 20, data collection at regulation 27 and the CII at regulation 28.

Understand this first

The examiner has named one instrument and is checking whether you have opened it. Every mark on this question is inside resolution A.1070(28). The Code is short - sixty-three paragraphs in four parts - and it is organised exactly the way the question is: common material first, then flag, coastal and port States in that order, each part ending in an evaluation and review section. Limb (b) IS that evaluation section of the flag State part. So if you can hold the Code's own structure, you can rebuild the whole answer from it. Two things are sharper than they look. The question asks "which all IMO instruments" - and the Code answers that with six SUBJECT AREAS, while the instruments it is mandatory under are a different list entirely. And this paper prints 6 + 5 + 5, not the 6 + 4 + 6 of the January and March 2024 sittings, so limb (b) needs more than the paragraph 43 list and limb (c) has to fit three States into five marks.

Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

framing
  1. The Code, and what made it mandatory
a)
  1. The objective of the Code
  2. The strategy recommended to the flag State
  3. Which IMO instruments the Code covers
b)
  1. Key performance indicators for flag State evaluation
c)
  1. Flag State responsibilities
  2. Coastal State and port State responsibilities

Memory cue. The Code's four parts give the answer its spine: COMMON, FLAG, COASTAL, PORT. Walk it as WHAT-WHY-HOW-WHICH-MEASURE-WHO: WHAT the Code is (1), WHY it exists - the objective (2), HOW a State meets it - the strategy (3), WHICH instruments it reaches (4), how performance is MEASURED (5), and WHO does what - flag (6), then coastal and port (7). Inside every State part the same three beats: IMPLEMENT, ENFORCE, EVALUATE.

Model written answer

1. The Code, and what made it mandatory

The IMO Instruments Implementation Code (III Code) is the annex to resolution A.1070(28), adopted by the Assembly on 4 December 2013. Operative paragraph 3 revoked resolution A.1054(27), the Code for the Implementation of Mandatory IMO Instruments, 2011, which had been the basis of the voluntary audit scheme.

The Code is recommendatory in its own drafting - it says what a State "should" do - but its use was made mandatory by amending the treaty instruments themselves: A.1083(28), A.1084(28) and A.1085(28) amended Load Lines 1966, Tonnage 1969 and COLREG 1972; MSC.366(93), MSC.373(93), MSC.374(93) and MSC.375(93) amended SOLAS 1974, STCW 1978, the STCW Code and the 1988 Load Lines Protocol; and MEPC.246(66) and MEPC.247(66) amended MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol. That chain is what turns the Code into the standard against which a Member State is audited under the IMO Member State Audit Scheme.

The Code has four parts - Part 1 Common Areas, Part 2 Flag States, Part 3 Coastal States, Part 4 Port States - and each of Parts 2, 3 and 4 ends with an Evaluation and review section. Limb (b) is simply that section of Part 2 opened up.

2. The objective of the Code

Paragraph 1 states the objective in a single sentence: to enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment, and to assist States in the implementation of instruments of the Organization. The second half matters as much as the first - the Code is an assistance instrument, written because the Organization accepted that some States face real difficulty in complying fully.

Paragraph 2 qualifies it. States view the Code according to their own circumstances and should be bound only for the implementation of those instruments to which they are Contracting Governments or Parties; by virtue of geography and circumstance some States have a greater role as a flag State, others as a coastal or port State. The Code is therefore not a uniform checklist applied identically everywhere.

Two general provisions frame everything that follows. Paragraph 4 makes States responsible for promulgating laws and regulations and taking all other steps necessary to give those instruments full and complete effect. Paragraph 5 requires that, in acting against pollution, States must not transfer damage or hazards from one area to another, nor transform one type of pollution into another.

3. The strategy recommended to the flag State

Paragraph 3 answers the second task in limb (a). To meet the objective a State is recommended to:

  • develop an overall strategy to ensure that its international obligations and responsibilities as a flag, port and coastal State are met (3.1);
  • establish a methodology to monitor and assess that the strategy ensures effective implementation and enforcement of the relevant international mandatory instruments (3.2); and
  • continuously review the strategy to achieve, maintain and improve overall organizational performance and capability as a flag, port and coastal State (3.3).

Read together those three are a develop - monitor - review cycle, not a document written once. Paragraph 9 completes it: the State should communicate its strategy, including information on its national legislation, to all concerned.

Paragraph 7 tells the State what its legislation and administrative procedures must actually address - nine areas: jurisdiction; organization and authority; legislation, rules and regulations; promulgation of the applicable mandatory instruments; enforcement arrangements; control, survey, inspection, audit, verification, approval and certification functions; the selection, recognition, authorization, empowerment and monitoring of recognized organizations and nominated surveyors; investigations required to be reported to the Organization; and reporting to the Organization and other Administrations. Paragraph 8 adds the three initial actions a Government must be capable of when a new or amended instrument enters into force: the ability to promulgate laws giving effective jurisdiction and control, a legal basis for enforcement including investigative and penal processes, and sufficient personnel with maritime expertise.

4. Which IMO instruments the Code covers

This limb has two correct answers and they are not the same, which is where marks are usually lost.

First, the Code's own scope. Paragraph 6 says the Code addresses those aspects necessary to give full and complete effect to the applicable international instruments pertaining to six subject areas:

  • safety of life at sea;
  • prevention of pollution from ships;
  • standards of training, certification and watchkeeping for seafarers;
  • load lines;
  • tonnage measurement of ships; and
  • regulations for preventing collisions at sea.

Note what is absent: maritime security is not one of the six. The preamble to the resolution speaks of safety, security and protection of the marine environment, but the operative scope at paragraph 6 does not extend to the ISPS Code or SOLAS chapter XI-2.

Second, the treaty instruments under which use of the Code is mandatory, which therefore define the audit scope. These are the instruments listed in resolution A.1187(33), the 2023 Non-exhaustive list of obligations under instruments relevant to the III Code, adopted 6 December 2023: SOLAS 1974 with its 1978 and 1988 Protocols; MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol; STCW 1978 and the STCW Code; Load Lines 1966 and the 1988 Load Lines Protocol; Tonnage 1969; and COLREG 1972. A.1187(33) revoked the 2021 list, A.1157(32), and has been the current list for twelve months at this sitting.

A precise point available here, and worth a mark for showing the resolution has been opened: A.1187(33) is organised in seven annexes, and Annex 7 lists the amendments expected to be accepted and to enter into force between 1 January 2024 and 1 July 2024. At this sitting that window has closed - those amendments are in force, and the annex now reads as a record of what came into effect during the year rather than as a forecast. A candidate who notices that the obligations list is itself dated, and moves with the calendar, is demonstrating exactly the distinction between adopted, expected to enter into force and in force that this Code exists to police.

5. Key performance indicators for flag State evaluation

Paragraph 42 requires a flag State to evaluate its performance on a periodic basis with respect to the administrative processes, procedures and resources needed to meet its obligations under the instruments to which it is a Party. Paragraph 43 then lists the measures, inter alia:

  • port State control detention rates;
  • flag State inspection results;
  • casualty statistics;
  • communication and information processes;
  • annual loss statistics, excluding constructive total losses (CTLs); and
  • such other performance indicators as may be appropriate.

The stated purpose is diagnostic, and reproducing it earns the mark: the indicators exist in order to determine whether staffing, resources and administrative procedures are adequate to meet its flag State obligations. They are for the State's own periodic self-evaluation - not a return filed with IMO.

Paragraph 44 then recommends ten areas for regular review:

  1. fleet loss and accident ratios, to identify trends over selected time periods;
  2. the number of verified cases of detained ships in relation to the size of the fleet;
  3. verified cases of incompetence or wrongdoing by holders of certificates or endorsements issued under its authority;
  4. responses to port State deficiency reports or interventions;
  5. investigations into very serious and serious casualties and the lessons learned from them;
  6. technical and other resources committed;
  7. results of inspections, surveys and controls of the ships in the fleet;
  8. investigation of occupational accidents;
  9. the number of incidents and violations under the applicable international pollution prevention regulations; and
  10. the number of suspensions or withdrawals of certificates, endorsements or approvals.

(This paper prints five marks for limb (b) where the January and March 2024 sittings printed four. The extra mark is bought by giving paragraph 44 in its own right rather than compressing it into paragraph 43 - so name the paragraph 43 measures, state their diagnostic purpose, and then give at least six of the paragraph 44 review areas.)

6. Flag State responsibilities

Part 2 runs from paragraph 15 to paragraph 44. Its shape is implementation - delegation - enforcement - surveyors - investigations - review.

  • Implementation (15-17). Issue national legislation and guidance implementing the safety and pollution conventions, and assign responsibility for keeping it updated. Paragraph 16 requires a safety and environmental protection programme with five minimum elements, of which the load-bearing one is 16.2: compliance verified by an audit and inspection programme independent of any body issuing the certificates. Paragraph 17 requires ships to be sufficiently and efficiently manned, on the Principles of Safe Manning.
  • Delegation (18-21). A flag State authorising a recognized organization must determine that it has adequate technical, managerial and research capability; base the authorisation on a formal written agreement; issue specific instructions for a ship found unfit to proceed to sea; supply its national law and interpretations; and require records to be kept. Paragraph 20 requires an oversight programme with supplementary surveys and competent supervising staff.
  • Enforcement (22-27). Prohibit a ship from sailing until it complies; inspect periodically to verify that the actual condition of ship and crew matches the certificates; verify seafarer familiarity and the complement's ability to coordinate in an emergency; provide penalties of adequate severity; and institute proceedings after investigation irrespective of where the violation occurred. Paragraphs 26 and 27 permit certification only after determining that all applicable requirements are met.
  • Surveyors and investigations (28-41). Surveyors qualified against a defined standard under a documented qualification system, and issued an identification document (37). Marine safety investigations by impartial and objective investigators, taking account of the Casualty Investigation Code, with the report forwarded to the Organization together with the flag State's observations (38-41).

7. Coastal State and port State responsibilities

Parts 3 and 4 open with the same principle, at paragraphs 45 and 52: coastal and port States have rights as well as obligations, and when they exercise those rights they incur additional obligations. Both must implement policy through national legislation and guidance (46, 54) and ensure their legislation and procedures deliver consistent implementation and verification (47, 55).

Coastal State (Part 3). Paragraph 48 lists the rights, obligations and responsibilities, inter alia: radiocommunication services; meteorological services and warnings; search and rescue services; hydrographic services; ships' routeing; ship reporting systems; vessel traffic services; and aids to navigation - eight service areas. Note that they are services to shipping, not controls over ships. Paragraph 50 requires a control and monitoring programme to allocate statistical data for trend analysis, to establish mechanisms for timely response to pollution incidents in its waters, and to cooperate with flag and port States in casualty investigations.

Port State (Part 4). Paragraph 53 records that the port State role derives from a combination of treaties, conventions, national laws and in some cases bilateral and multilateral agreements. Paragraph 56 gives three obligations: provision of appropriate reception facilities or the capability to accept all waste streams regulated under IMO instruments; port State control; and keeping a register of fuel oil suppliers. Paragraph 59 carries the no more favourable treatment principle - the conditions of the instruments are imposed on the ships of non-parties as well as parties. Paragraphs 60 and 61 require a PSC programme consistent with the relevant Assembly resolution - at this sitting A.1185(33), the Procedures for Port State Control, 2023 - carried out only by authorized and qualified port State control officers; paragraph 62 requires those officers to be free from commercial, financial and other pressures, to hold no commercial interest in the port, the ships inspected, repair facilities or support services, and not to be employed by or work on behalf of recognized organizations or classification societies.

Both parts close as Part 2 does: paragraphs 51 and 63 require the coastal State and the port State to periodically evaluate their own performance. That symmetry - implement, enforce, evaluate - is the single most useful thing to carry into the hall, because it lets you build all three States from one remembered shape, which is exactly what five marks for three roles requires.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

This question recurs across four MIW-transcribed sittings word-for-word, but this paper does not print the same marks as the January and March 2024 sittings. Those print 6 + 4 + 6. This one prints 6 + 5 + 5. The words are identical; the expected depth is not.

What that means in the hall: limb (b) is now worth more than it looks. Four marks would be covered by naming the paragraph 43 measures. Five will not be - you need paragraph 43 and a real showing of paragraph 44's ten review areas. Conversely limb (c) has lost a mark and now carries five for three States. That is under two marks each, so a discursive treatment of flag State duties will run you out of time. Answer all three on the Code's shared skeleton - implementation, enforcement, evaluation - and you cover three roles in the space most candidates spend on one.

Limb (a) is unchanged at six marks and still hides three tasks: the objective, the strategy, and the instruments. Splitting it across three route steps stops all six marks being spent on the objective sentence.

The mandatory-application chain, and why it matters

Candidates often assert that "the III Code is mandatory" without being able to say why a recommendatory-sounding text binds anybody. The answer is that the Code was never redrafted into obligation language; instead the conventions were amended to require its use. That is why the Code still reads "should" throughout. Being able to say that in one sentence separates a candidate who has read the instrument from one who has read about it.

The audit scheme is the practical consequence. The Code supplies the auditable areas; A.1187(33) supplies the obligation-by-obligation list that auditors and Administrations work from.

The obligations list is dated - and at THIS sitting the date has moved

A.1187(33) was adopted on 6 December 2023 by the 33rd Assembly, revoking the 2021 list A.1157(32). At this December 2024 sitting it has been the current list for twelve months. Material citing the 2021 list is superseded.

The resolution is built in seven annexes, and Annex 7 is headed amendments expected to be accepted and to enter into force between 1 January 2024 and 1 July 2024. Read that heading against the date of your own examination. At a January 2024 sitting the whole window is still ahead and the annex is a forecast - a textbook illustration of adopted but not yet in force. At this sitting, in December 2024, the window has closed: those amendments came into force during the year and the annex is now a record, not a prediction. Same annex, opposite reading, eleven months apart. If you are revising from a solved January 2024 paper, this is the one sentence in it you must turn around.

The six-versus-instruments trap, in detail

The printed words are "Which all IMO instruments covered in the code". Two things are being asked at once and the safe answer gives both. The Code's scope at paragraph 6 is expressed as six subject areas, not as a list of conventions. The instruments under which the Code is mandatory are the SOLAS / MARPOL / STCW / Load Lines / Tonnage / COLREG family with their Protocols, listed in A.1187(33).

A count is risky. The audit scope is usually summarised as nine mandatory instruments - SOLAS 1974, SOLAS PROT 1988, MARPOL, MARPOL PROT 1997, STCW 1978, LL 1966, LL PROT 1988, TONNAGE 1969 and COLREG 1972 - but A.1187(33) annex 1 also carries a SOLAS PROT 1978 column, and neither instrument states a number anywhere. List them; do not lead with a count. If a count is wanted, write "the SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG instruments together with their Protocols, commonly summarised as nine".

The absence of maritime security from paragraph 6 is a genuine examinable point, not a drafting slip: the preamble uses the phrase "safety, security and protection of the marine environment" while the operative scope does not extend to security instruments.

Common mistakes

  • Writing a general essay on flag State duties under UNCLOS article 94. That is a different question. The examiner has named a specific Code and wants its paragraphs.
  • Answering "which instruments" with the six subject areas only, or the convention list only. Give both, and say why they differ.
  • Confusing the III Code with the ISM Code. The III Code binds States; the ISM Code binds companies and ships.
  • Treating the performance indicators as a return made to IMO. They are for the State's own periodic self-evaluation under paragraph 42.
  • Giving the coastal State a control-and-inspection role. Paragraph 48 is a list of services - SAR, hydrography, VTS, aids to navigation.
  • Answering limb (b) with paragraph 43 alone. At five marks on this paper that is not enough - paragraph 44's review areas are wanted too.
  • Spending half the answer on limb (c). On this paper it is worth five marks for three States.
  • Citing A.1054(27), the 2011 Code, as current. It was revoked by A.1070(28).
  • Describing A.1187(33) Annex 7 as a list of amendments still to come. At a December 2024 sitting that window has closed.

Examiner traps

  • Six subject areas versus the instrument list. The single most likely place to lose marks on this question.
  • "Should" throughout the Code. If asked whether the Code is mandatory, say that its use is mandatory under the amended conventions while the text remains recommendatory in form.
  • Excluding constructive total losses. Paragraph 43 says annual loss statistics excluding CTLs. Reproducing that qualifier shows the source has been read.
  • Two independence requirements, different Parts, different purposes. The flag State's audit programme must be independent of the certifying body (16.2); PSCOs must be free of commercial interest and must not work for recognized organizations (62). Do not merge them.
  • The marks are not the ones in last year's paper. 6 + 5 + 5 here, 6 + 4 + 6 in January and March 2024.

Likely oral follow-up

  • "Is the III Code mandatory, and how?" - the amendment chain.
  • "What is IMSAS and what does it audit against?" - the Code supplies the auditable areas; A.1187(33) supplies the obligations list.
  • "Your flag State has a rising detention rate. What does the Code expect it to do?" - paragraphs 43 and 44: treat it as a performance indicator, review detentions against fleet size, review responses to PSC deficiency reports, and test whether staffing and resources are adequate.
  • "What must a flag State do before authorising a class society?" - paragraph 18: capability, a formal written agreement, specific instructions for unfit ships, national law and interpretations, records.
  • "Name three port State obligations under the Code." - reception facilities, port State control, a register of fuel oil suppliers.
  • "Which State investigates a casualty, and who gets the report?" - paragraphs 38 to 41: the flag State provides qualified impartial investigators irrespective of where the casualty occurred; the report goes to the Organization with the flag State's observations.

Memory framework

Two shapes carry the whole question. The Code's shape is COMMON, FLAG, COASTAL, PORT. The shape inside each State part is implement, enforce, evaluate and review. Everything in limb (c) hangs on the second shape, and limb (b) is simply the "evaluation and review" box of the flag State part opened up.

For the coastal State's eight services, group them as talk, warn, rescue, chart (radiocommunication, meteorological warnings, SAR, hydrographic) and route, report, watch, mark (ships' routeing, ship reporting, VTS, aids to navigation).

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution A.1070(28), 4 December 2013 - the III Code. Revoked A.1054(27).
  • Paragraph 1 objective. Paragraph 2 bound only for instruments to which the State is a Party.
  • Paragraph 3 the three-part strategy. Paragraph 9 communicate it.
  • Paragraph 6 the six subject areas. Paragraph 7 the nine areas legislation must address. Paragraph 8 initial actions.
  • Paragraphs 15-17 flag implementation; 18-21 delegation; 22-27 enforcement; 28-37 surveyors; 38-41 investigations.
  • Paragraphs 42-44 flag State evaluation and review - the KPIs, and limb (b) in full.
  • Paragraphs 45-51 coastal State. Paragraph 48 the eight services.
  • Paragraphs 52-63 port State. 56 three obligations. 59 no more favourable treatment. 62 PSCO independence.
  • Resolution A.1187(33), 6 December 2023 - the 2023 obligations list; revoked A.1157(32); seven annexes, Annex 7 covering 1 January to 1 July 2024.
  • Resolution A.1185(33), 6 December 2023 - Procedures for Port State Control, 2023; the operative PSC procedures at this sitting.

Flag State surveyors and investigations - the detail behind route step 6

Limb (c) is five marks for three States, so the Model Answer gives the flag State the headline of paragraphs 28 to 41 rather than the whole regime. The detail below is worth holding for the oral.

  • Qualification (para 29). Either appropriate qualifications from a marine or nautical institution with relevant seagoing experience as a certificated officer holding or having held a valid management level certificate of competency; or a degree or equivalent from a tertiary institution in a relevant field of engineering or science; or accreditation as a surveyor through a formalised training programme reaching the same standard.
  • Experience (paras 30-31). Personnel qualified under 29.1 should have served not less than three years at sea as a deck or engine officer; those qualified under 29.2 should have worked in a relevant capacity for at least three years.
  • Knowledge (para 36). Knowledge of the applicable international and national rules; of survey, certification, control, investigative and oversight procedures; understanding of the goals of the instruments; professional competency; full safety awareness; and training or experience in the tasks performed.
  • Identification (para 37). The flag State issues an identification document for the surveyor to carry when performing his or her tasks.
  • Investigator expertise (para 39). Ready access to expertise in navigation and the Collision Regulations, certificate-of-competency regulations, causes of marine pollution, interviewing techniques, evidence gathering, and evaluation of the effects of the human element.
  • Reporting threshold (para 40). Any accident involving personal injury necessitating absence from duty of three days or more, and any deaths from occupational accidents and casualties, should be investigated and the results made public.

Uncertainty and currency

The Code material is primary throughout: A.1070(28) was read in full and every paragraph number was checked against the source. Two limitations are recorded. The answer deliberately does not lead with a number of instruments - A.1070(28) states six subject areas and no instrument count, and A.1187(33) lists obligations by instrument without totalling them; the figure of nine is offered as a common summary, not as a quotation. And the Framework and Procedures for the Member State Audit Scheme was not re-read this session, so no claim is made about audit periodicity or the scheme's procedural rules.

One currency point is specific to this sitting and must not be "corrected" by a later editor: A.1187(33) of 6 December 2023 is the operative obligations list here, and the A.11xx(33) series generally is the operative Assembly material, because the 34th Assembly did not meet until 3 December 2025 - a full year after this examination.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing The Code, and what made it mandatory
  2. __________a) The objective of the Code
  3. __________a) The strategy recommended to the flag State
  4. __________a) Which IMO instruments the Code covers
  5. __________b) Key performance indicators for flag State evaluation
  6. __________c) Flag State responsibilities
  7. __________c) Coastal State and port State responsibilities
Flashcards 12

1 The Code and what made it mandatory. 2 (a) The objective. 3 (a) The strategy. 4 (a) Which instruments. 5 (b) The performance indicators. 6 (c) Flag State. 7 (c) Coastal and port State. This paper prints 6 + 5 + 5, so step 5 must give paragraph 44 as well as 43, and steps 6 and 7 together have only five marks for three States.

Steps 2, 3 and 4 split limb (a), which hides three tasks in six marks; the re-weighting is what distinguishes this sitting from January and March 2024.

Resolution A.1070(28), adopted 4 December 2013. Operative paragraph 3 revoked A.1054(27), the Code for the Implementation of Mandatory IMO Instruments, 2011.

Citing the revoked 2011 Code as current is a common and visible error.

Paragraph 1: to enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment, and to assist States in the implementation of instruments of the Organization.

The second half - assistance - is routinely dropped, and it is half the sentence.

Develop an overall strategy covering its flag, port and coastal responsibilities; establish a methodology to monitor and assess that the strategy delivers effective implementation and enforcement; and continuously review the strategy to achieve, maintain and improve organizational performance and capability. Paragraph 9 then requires it to be communicated.

This is the 'strategy recommended to flag state' half of limb (a).

Paragraph 6 defines the Code's scope as six SUBJECT AREAS - safety of life at sea, prevention of pollution from ships, STCW, load lines, tonnage measurement, COLREG. Separately, the Code is made mandatory by amendments to the treaty INSTRUMENTS - SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW and its Code, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG with their Protocols, listed in A.1187(33). Give both, and say why they differ.

The highest-value distinction on this question.

Maritime security. The preamble speaks of safety, security and environmental protection, but the six subject areas in the operative scope do not include security, so the ISPS Code and SOLAS chapter XI-2 sit outside the Code's stated scope.

A candidate who lists seven areas including security has not read paragraph 6.

Port State control detention rates; flag State inspection results; casualty statistics; communication and information processes; annual loss statistics excluding constructive total losses; and other appropriate indicators. Their purpose is to determine whether staffing, resources and administrative procedures are adequate to meet the State's flag State obligations.

Limb (b) is FIVE marks on this paper, so this paragraph is necessary but no longer sufficient - paragraph 44 is wanted too.

Fleet loss and accident ratios; detained ships in relation to fleet size; verified cases of incompetence or wrongdoing by certificate holders; responses to port State deficiency reports or interventions; investigations into very serious and serious casualties; technical and other resources committed. The remaining four are inspection and survey results, occupational accident investigation, pollution incidents and violations, and suspensions or withdrawals of certificates.

This is the material that buys limb (b)'s fifth mark at this sitting's 6 + 5 + 5 split.

As a CLOSED window - those amendments are already in force by this sitting, so the annex reads as a record of what took effect during the year, not as a forecast. A January 2024 treatment correctly calls it forward-looking; eleven months later that framing is wrong.

The same annex reads in opposite directions at two sittings of the same year. It is the sharpest adopted-versus-in-force illustration available on this question, and it inverts.

Paragraph 16.2 - the flag State's audit and inspection programme must be independent of any body issuing the certificates, including a delegated entity. Paragraph 62 - port State control officers must be free of commercial, financial and other pressure, hold no commercial interest in the port or ships inspected, and not be employed by or work for recognized organizations or classification societies.

They serve different purposes in different Parts and are often merged into one.

Coastal: radiocommunication services; meteorological services and warnings; search and rescue services; hydrographic services; ships' routeing; ship reporting systems; vessel traffic services; aids to navigation. Port: appropriate reception facilities or capability to accept all regulated waste streams; port State control; and a register of fuel oil suppliers.

The coastal list is SERVICES to shipping, not controls over ships; and the register of fuel oil suppliers is the port obligation candidates forget.

Implementation, then enforcement, then evaluation and review. Paragraphs 42, 51 and 63 are the three evaluation requirements. It matters because limb (c) is worth only five marks for three States at this sitting, and one remembered shape answers all three inside that budget.

One shape answers limb (c) for all three States and simultaneously locates limb (b).

Quick revision
15-second recall
III Code = annex to A.1070(28), 4 December 2013; revoked A.1054(27). Recommendatory in wording, mandatory in use because SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, LL, TONNAGE and COLREG were amended to require it - which is why it still says "should". Four parts: common, flag, coastal, port, each State part ending in evaluation and review. Objective (para 1): enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment and assist States in implementing IMO instruments. Para 2: bound only for instruments to which the State is a Party. Strategy (para 3): develop an overall strategy covering flag, port and coastal roles; establish a methodology to monitor and assess it; continuously review it - a develop-monitor-review cycle; para 9 communicate it. Para 7 nine areas for legislation; para 8 three initial actions. Scope (para 6): six subject areas - safety of life at sea, prevention of pollution, STCW, load lines, tonnage, COLREG. Security is NOT one of them. Instruments under which it is mandatory are listed in A.1187(33) (6 December 2023, revoking A.1157(32), twelve months old at this sitting): SOLAS + Protocols, MARPOL + 1997 Protocol, STCW + Code, LL 1966 + 1988 Protocol, TONNAGE 1969, COLREG 1972. Its Annex 7 covers 1 Jan - 1 Jul 2024, a window already closed at this sitting. KPIs (42-44): para 42 periodic SELF-evaluation; para 43 - PSC detention rates, flag State inspection results, casualty statistics, communication and information processes, annual loss statistics excluding CTLs - purpose is to test whether staffing, resources and procedures are adequate; para 44 - ten review areas including detentions against fleet size, certificate-holder incompetence, responses to PSC deficiency reports, serious casualty investigations, occupational accidents, pollution violations, suspensions and withdrawals. Flag (15-41): legislation and guidance; para 16 five-element programme, 16.2 audit independent of the certifying body; 17 safe manning; 18-21 delegation by formal written agreement plus oversight; 22-27 prohibit sailing, inspect, penalties of adequate severity, proceedings irrespective of where the violation occurred; 28-41 qualified surveyors with an identification document, impartial casualty investigation reported to the Organization. Coastal (45-51): para 48 eight services - radiocommunication, meteorological warnings, SAR, hydrographic, ships' routeing, ship reporting, VTS, aids to navigation. Port (52-63): para 56 three obligations - reception facilities, port State control, register of fuel oil suppliers; 59 no more favourable treatment; 62 PSCO independence; PSC under A.1185(33). 51 and 63 - coastal and port States evaluate their own performance too. This paper prints 6 + 5 + 5, not 6 + 4 + 6.

Critical regulation
A.1070(28) annex paragraph 6 - the Code addresses six subject areas: safety of life at sea; prevention of pollution from ships; standards of training, certification and watchkeeping; load lines; tonnage measurement; and regulations for preventing collisions at sea. Read it with paragraphs 42 to 44, which carry the flag State performance indicators and, at this paper's five-mark weighting for limb (b), must be given in full rather than summarised.
Major trap
Answering "which IMO instruments" with only one of the two correct lists. Paragraph 6 gives six SUBJECT AREAS and names no convention; the treaty instruments under which the Code is mandatory are SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG with their Protocols, listed in A.1187(33). Maritime security is in neither the paragraph 6 list nor the Code's operative scope. Second trap, specific to this sitting: A.1187(33) Annex 7 lists amendments expected to enter into force between 1 January and 1 July 2024. At a January 2024 sitting that annex is a FORECAST; at this December 2024 sitting the window has CLOSED and it is a RECORD. Revising from a solved January 2024 paper will give you the forward-looking version, and here it is wrong.

Unresolved
  • The number of treaty instruments audited under IMSAS is commonly given as NINE - SOLAS 1974, SOLAS PROT 1988, MARPOL, MARPOL PROT 1997, STCW 1978, LL 1966, LL PROT 1988, TONNAGE 1969 and COLREG 1972. A.1187(33) annex 1 additionally carries a SOLAS PROT 1978 column, which is why a count of ten also circulates. Neither the Code nor A.1187(33) states a number, so the answer LISTS the instruments and does not lead with a count. Recorded rather than resolved.
  • The Framework and Procedures for the IMO Member State Audit Scheme (A.1067(28), later A.1156(32)) was not re-read this session. The claim that IMSAS uses the III Code as its audit standard rests on the mandatory-application chain recited in A.1070(28) and A.1187(33), both of which were read. No audit periodicity is asserted anywhere in the answer.
  • The examination day within December 2024 is not printed on the source copy. Nothing in this answer depends on it: no claim turns on a date falling inside December 2024, and A.1187(33) had been in force for twelve months by any day of that month.

December 2024 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.

QTopicAnswer routeCritical rule / numberMajor trap
Q1 UN Sustainable Development Goals and the Maritime Sector
16 marks		 What the Sustainable Development Goals are → The role of the SDGs in promoting global sustainability → Why the maritime sector matters, and how IMO aligns → SDG 13 Climate Action - relevance to shipping → SDG 13 - specific actions → SDG 14 Life Below Water - relevance to shipping → SDG 14 - specific actions → The management-level conclusion The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, UN General Assembly resolution A/RES/70/1 of 25 September 2015, sets 17 Goals and 169 targets which are integrated and indivisible, global in nature and universally applicable; in the maritime sector the binding expression of SDG 13 is the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, resolution MEPC.377(80) of 7 July 2023, which superseded the 2018 Initial Strategy and sets net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050 with indicative checkpoints for 2030 and 2040.
A/RES/70/1, adopted 25 September 2015, in effect 1 January 2016		 Answering with generic aspirations when the stem asks for specific actions the shipping industry can take - 'reduce emissions' scores nothing where 'weather routeing, hull and propeller condition management, onshore power supply, D-2 ballast water compliance' scores. Three currency traps sit alongside it, and all three are things a candidate revising from later material would get wrong: the operative IMO climate instrument is the 2023 Strategy MEPC.377(80), not the superseded 2018 Initial Strategy; no mid-term measure had been adopted at this sitting, the work having been referred onward to MEPC 83 in 2025; and the Hong Kong Convention was NOT YET IN FORCE - its criteria were met in June 2023 but it enters into force on 26 June 2025, six months after this examination.
Q2 IMO Code on Noise Levels On Board Ships
16 marks		 What the Noise Code is, and what made it mandatory → Purpose and significance → Areas of application → Maximum allowable noise limits - chapter 4 → Noise exposure limits - chapter 5 → Measures to reduce noise on board → Impact on crew health and on performance → Compliance requirements - design, equipment and operation SOLAS regulation II-1/3-12, inserted by resolution MSC.338(91) and in force 1 July 2014, requires ships to be constructed to reduce onboard noise and to protect personnel from noise in accordance with the Code on noise levels on board ships adopted by resolution MSC.337(91); it applies to ships of 1,600 gross tonnage and above contracted on or after 1 July 2014, or keel-laid on or after 1 January 2015, or delivered on or after 1 July 2018.
MSC.337(91) adopted 30 November 2012; SOLAS II-1/3-12 in force 1 July 2014		 Writing a general essay on noise and hearing protection instead of producing the Code's actual numbers. The stem asks for FIVE things - purpose and significance, maximum allowable limits, areas of application, measures to reduce noise, impact on health AND performance, and compliance requirements for design, equipment and operation - and each is a separate block of marks. Three specific traps: the whole Code is NOT mandatory (paragraph 1.1.3 keeps all of chapter 5 recommendatory); the 110 dB(A) machinery space limit ASSUMES hearing protectors are worn and is not a permissible unprotected exposure; and only the ACCOMMODATION limits change with ship size, being 5 dB(A) tighter at 10,000 GT and above.
Q3 New Inspection Regime of the Paris MoU
16 marks		 What the NIR is, and what it replaced → The Ship Risk Profile → Company performance - the new parameter → The selection scheme - windows and priorities → Types of inspection → Reporting obligations and THETIS → Refusal of access - banning → Pros of the NIR → Cons of the NIR → How owners and managers should respond The Ship Risk Profile, set out in Table 1 of Annex 7 of the Paris MoU Memorandum, classifies every ship as High, Standard or Low Risk from the type and age of the ship, the performance of its flag, recognized organization and ISM company, and the number of deficiencies and detentions recorded in the region over the preceding 36 months; the resulting profile determines both the interval between inspections and the type of inspection the ship receives.
NIR in force 1 January 2011		 Answering limb (b) with pros and cons only and never addressing the third printed task - how owners and managers should respond. That response must be a management programme aimed at the profile itself: measure it, choose flag and RO deliberately, protect the fleet through fleet-wide corrective action because company performance is shared, prepare for the inspection the ship will actually get, and handle the inspection day professionally. In limb (a) the recurring traps are reversing Priority I and Priority II, and saying only High Risk Ships receive expanded inspections when all bulk carriers, chemical tankers, gas carriers, oil tankers and passenger ships over 12 years old do as well. One currency trap: environmental criteria in the Ship Risk Profile belong to the recast EU Directive applying from 2027 and are NOT part of the regime at this sitting.
Q4 Classification Societies in Rule Formation, and Survey Types
16 marks		 What a classification society is, and its two distinct roles → How class rules are made → What the structural, mechanical and electrical rules cover → Implementation through the ship's life → Why compliance with class rules is now mentioned in SOLAS chapter II-1 → The harmonized system, and where these surveys sit → Annual versus periodical - the difference and the relevance SOLAS regulation II-1/3-1 - ships shall be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with the structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society recognized by the Administration under regulation XI-1/1, or with applicable national standards providing an equivalent level of safety.
RO Code - MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory 1 January 2015		 Failing to explain WHY II-1/3-1 exists - that SOLAS never contained scantling rules, so structural standards were private contract until this regulation made them statutory - or stating a single frequency for the periodical survey when it differs between the Safety Equipment and Safety Radio certificates. There is a third trap peculiar to this sitting: the edition of the HSSC Survey Guidelines. The edition in force here is A.1186(33) of 6 December 2023. A.1207(34) was adopted on 3 December 2025, two years AFTER this examination, and current material will wrongly tell you that citing A.1186(33) is out of date. The survey substance is identical in both, so a wrong citation produces an answer that reads as entirely correct.
Q5 Root Cause Analysis, Active Failures and Latent Failures
16 marks		 What root cause analysis is, and why it is required → The principles of root cause analysis → The steps of a root cause analysis → Proximate cause versus root cause → The accident causation models → Active failures → Latent failures → Why the distinction matters, and how the two combine The Casualty Investigation Code, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) and made mandatory as to Parts I and II by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 with effect from 1 January 2010, requires a marine safety investigation into every very serious marine casualty and provides that such an investigation is conducted to prevent future casualties and is separate and distinct from, and must not seek to apportion, blame or determine liability.
Casualty Investigation Code - resolution MSC.255(84), adopted 2008		 Offering 'human error' as a root cause. It is a proximate cause and never a root cause, because nothing can be done to it - the test of a root cause is that it names something the organisation controls and could change. The same test disposes of 'lack of situational awareness', 'failure to follow procedure' and 'complacency', each of which restates what happened rather than explaining why it was allowed to happen. Two further traps: treating a latent failure as a dormant mechanical defect when it is a management or design DECISION and its consequences; and defining active and latent failures abstractly when the stem expressly prints 'giving suitable examples'.
Q6 MLC 2006 - Structure of the Convention and the DMLC
16 marks		 What the MLC is, and why its structure is unusual → The three-level structure → The five Titles → The amendment position at this sitting → The certification chain - where the DMLC sits → DMLC Part I - the flag State's declaration → DMLC Part II - the shipowner's declaration → The welfare measures covered, and how they bite Standard A5.1.3 paragraph 10 - Part I of the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance is drawn up by the COMPETENT AUTHORITY and identifies the Appendix A5-I matters, the national requirements implementing them, ship-type specific requirements, any substantially equivalent provisions and any Title 3 exemption; Part II is drawn up by the SHIPOWNER and certified by the competent authority or a duly authorized recognized organization, identifying the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance between inspections and the measures proposed to ensure continuous improvement.
Certification mandatory at 500 GT and over - Regulation 5.1.3		 Inverting the two Parts of the DMLC. Part I is the competent authority's statement of national law; Part II is the shipowner's statement of how the ship complies. Two further traps sit alongside it. Appendix A5-I now contains SIXTEEN matters, not fourteen - the two financial security matters were added by the 2014 amendments, in force 18 January 2017, and most revision material (including the ILO's own 2015 FAQ) predates that. And the 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024, the month of this examination: state the date rather than writing 'now in force' or 'not yet in force', because the examination day is not printed on the paper.
Q7 Carrier Responsibility under the Cargo Conventions, and Defences
16 marks		 Why the duration of responsibility matters → Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle → Hamburg - port to port → Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force → The comparison, and the Indian position → The grain claim - how it is framed → The excepted perils that apply to grain → The overriding obligations that can defeat every defence → Procedural and quantum defences → The evidence that makes the defence work Hague-Visby Article I(e) defines 'carriage of goods' as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time when they are discharged from the ship - the tackle-to-tackle period; read with Article III rule 8, under which any clause relieving the carrier from liability otherwise than as provided in the Rules is null and void, so that the period determines both which regime applies and whether an exclusion clause is effective.
Hague-Visby Article I(e) - loaded on to discharged from the ship		 Describing the Rotterdam Rules as if they were in force. They were adopted in 2008 and require twenty ratifications under Article 94, which had not been achieved at this sitting - so they are a proposed regime, not applicable law. In limb (b) the corresponding trap is treating inherent vice as an unconditional defence: under Article IV rule 1 the burden of proving due diligence is on the carrier, so the defence fails if the holds were not fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the grain, or if the cargo was not properly ventilated and cared for under Article III rule 2. Two smaller but visible errors: quoting a per-package limitation for a bulk cargo that has no packages, and omitting the Indian statutory position in an Indian examination.
Q8 SEEMP - The Four-Step Energy Efficiency Cycle
16 marks		 What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live → Step 1 - Planning → Step 2 - Implementation → Step 3 - Monitoring → Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement → Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 requires each ship to keep on board a Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization - which at this sitting are the 2024 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, revoking the 2022 Guidelines MEPC.346(78). Section 3.6 of those Guidelines states that Part I should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency through four steps: planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement.
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - the SEEMP; 26.1 Part I, 26.2 Part II, 26.3 Part III		 Answering the question as a description of four steps when the printed command asks for the CRITICAL ROLE of each, WITH SUITABLE EXAMPLES - three demands, of which description is only one. The citation traps run alongside it: the SEEMP is MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26, not regulation 22 (renumbered by MEPC.328(76) from 1 November 2022); and the operative guidelines at this sitting are MEPC.395(82) of 4 October 2024, not MEPC.346(78), which they revoked two months before this paper was sat. Finally, the four steps are the framework of Part I only.
Q9 IMO Instruments Implementation Code - Objectives and Flag State Performance
16 marks		 The Code, and what made it mandatory → The objective of the Code → The strategy recommended to the flag State → Which IMO instruments the Code covers → Key performance indicators for flag State evaluation → Flag State responsibilities → Coastal State and port State responsibilities A.1070(28) annex paragraph 6 - the Code addresses six subject areas: safety of life at sea; prevention of pollution from ships; standards of training, certification and watchkeeping; load lines; tonnage measurement; and regulations for preventing collisions at sea. Read it with paragraphs 42 to 44, which carry the flag State performance indicators and, at this paper's five-mark weighting for limb (b), must be given in full rather than summarised.
Resolution A.1070(28), adopted 4 December 2013		 Answering "which IMO instruments" with only one of the two correct lists. Paragraph 6 gives six SUBJECT AREAS and names no convention; the treaty instruments under which the Code is mandatory are SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG with their Protocols, listed in A.1187(33). Maritime security is in neither the paragraph 6 list nor the Code's operative scope. Second trap, specific to this sitting: A.1187(33) Annex 7 lists amendments expected to enter into force between 1 January and 1 July 2024. At a January 2024 sitting that annex is a FORECAST; at this December 2024 sitting the window has CLOSED and it is a RECORD. Revising from a solved January 2024 paper will give you the forward-looking version, and here it is wrong.
